# Biology — Answers

Questions: [questions.md](questions.md). Answer numbers match question numbers.

## VCE Units 1 & 2

### Unit 1 AOS 1: How do cells function?

#### Cell theory and cell size

**A1.** B. All cells arise from pre-existing cells.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-1 -->

Explanation: Cell theory states that all living things are made of one or more cells, the cell is the basic unit of life, and all cells come from pre-existing cells. Prokaryotes lack a nucleus, and spontaneous generation was disproved.

**A2.** C. 3:1
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-2 -->

Explanation: SA = 6 × 2² = 24 µm²; V = 2³ = 8 µm³; SA:V = 24:8 = 3:1. A 6:1 ratio would be a 1 µm cube.

**A3.** D. Its SA:V ratio decreases, so exchange per unit volume becomes less efficient.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-3 -->

Explanation: Volume scales with the cube of length and surface area with the square, so SA:V falls as a cell grows. Less membrane is available per unit of cytoplasm to supply materials and remove wastes.

**A4.** C. The 1 cm cube would show the greatest percentage of its volume changed colour.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-4 -->

Explanation: Acid diffuses inward at about the same rate (same penetration depth) in all cubes, but the smallest cube has the highest SA:V, so the largest fraction of its volume changes colour.

**A5.** B. Microvilli or a flattened, elongated shape
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-5 -->

Explanation: Folding the membrane into microvilli or flattening the cell increases surface area without much increase in volume, raising SA:V. A sphere has the lowest SA:V of any shape for a given volume.

**A6.** Cells exchange materials (nutrients, O₂, wastes) across their plasma membrane; small cells have a high SA:V ratio so the membrane area can supply the volume quickly enough / diffusion distances to the centre stay short; large cells would have too little membrane relative to volume and could not meet metabolic demands.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-6 -->

Explanation:

**A7.** X: SA = 6 × 16 = 96 µm², V = 64 µm³, SA:V = 1.5:1; Y: SA = 6 × 64 = 384 µm², V = 512 µm³, SA:V = 0.75:1; cell X is more efficient because it has the higher SA:V ratio (twice that of Y).
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-7 -->

Explanation:

**A8.** Microscopes allowed cells to be observed for the first time (e.g. Hooke's cork cells, van Leeuwenhoek's microorganisms); repeated observation of cells in all plant and animal tissues, and of cells dividing, provided the evidence that all organisms are made of cells and that cells come from pre-existing cells.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-8 -->

Explanation:

**A9.** C. Microorganisms in the broth came from pre-existing microorganisms carried into it from the air.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-9 -->

Explanation: Broth in the intact swan-neck flask stayed sterile while air could still reach it, so the microbes must have come from pre-existing microbes rather than arising spontaneously in the broth. Spontaneous generation is the tempting but incorrect idea that the experiment disproved.

**A10.** A. A cube with a side length of 1 µm
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-10 -->

Explanation: For a cube, SA:V = 6 ÷ side length, so the 1 µm cube has 6:1 while the 20 µm cube has only 0.3:1. Choosing the largest cube because it has the most surface area ignores that its volume grows faster.

**A11.** B. The claim is incorrect, because the SA:V ratio halves when the side length doubles.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-11 -->

Explanation: For a cube, SA:V = 6 ÷ side length, so doubling the side length halves the ratio. Surface area rises by a factor of four and volume by a factor of eight, so they do not increase together. The shape of the cell stays the same when it is scaled up.

**A12.** D. 2:1
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-12 -->

Explanation: A surface area of 54 µm² gives 6s² = 54, so the side length is 3 µm and the volume is 27 µm³. Then 54 ÷ 27 = 2:1. A 6:1 ratio is the constant from the surface area formula, 1:2 inverts the ratio, and 3:1 is the side length rather than the ratio.

**A13.** A. 12:1
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-13 -->

Explanation: A volume of 0.125 µm³ gives a side length of 0.5 µm, so the surface area is 6 × 0.5² = 1.5 µm² and 1.5 ÷ 0.125 = 12. The 24:1 result comes from not squaring the side length in the surface area, and 1:12 inverts the ratio.

**A14.** B. 27
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-14 -->

Explanation: Volume scales with the cube of the side length, so the increase is (6 ÷ 2)³ = 3³ = 27 times. Multiplying by 9 treats volume as if it scaled with area, while 3 uses only the linear increase in length.

**A15.** C. 10–30 µm
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-15 -->

Explanation: Typical eukaryotic cells are around 10–100 µm across, so 10–30 µm is a good estimate. A diameter of 100 nm is about the size of a virus, while 1 mm is large enough to see without a microscope.

**A16.** D. 20 µm
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-16 -->

Explanation: There are 1000 µm in 1 mm, so 0.02 mm × 1000 = 20 µm. Dividing by 1000 gives 2 µm, leaving the number unchanged gives 0.02 µm, and multiplying by 10 000 gives 200 µm.

**A17.** A. 4.2:1
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-17 -->

Explanation: The surface area is 2(1 × 1 + 1 × 10 + 1 × 10) = 42 µm² and the volume is 1 × 1 × 10 = 10 µm³, so SA:V = 4.2:1. Counting each pair of faces only once gives 2.1:1, treating the box as a 1 µm cube gives 6:1, and inverting the ratio gives 0.24:1.

**A18.** B. The electron microscope
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-18 -->

Explanation: Light microscopes cannot resolve structures smaller than about 200 nm, which includes membranes and ribosomes, while electron microscopes can. The light microscope was essential for cell theory but cannot show these details, and X-ray diffraction is used to study molecular structure rather than to image cells.

**A19.** C. A typical bacterium
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-19 -->

Explanation: Typical bacteria are about 1–5 µm across, which is smaller than a human red blood cell (around 7–8 µm) or a plant palisade cell (tens of micrometres). A human egg cell is one of the largest human cells, at about 100 µm in diameter.

**A20.** D. Schleiden and Schwann
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-20 -->

Explanation: Schleiden studied plants and Schwann studied animals, and together they proposed that living things are made of cells. Hooke and van Leeuwenhoek made the first observations of cells and microorganisms, Redi and Pasteur disproved spontaneous generation, and Watson and Crick described the structure of DNA in 1953.

**A21.** A. The eight 1 µm cubes have a total surface area of 48 µm² and a combined SA:V of 6:1, twice the 3:1 of the single cube.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-21 -->

Explanation: Each 1 µm cube has 6 µm² of surface, so the eight cubes have 48 µm², while the single 2 µm cube has 24 µm². Dividing by the shared total volume of 8 µm³ gives 6:1 and 3:1 respectively, so dividing a cell into smaller pieces increases the membrane available per unit of cytoplasm.

**A22.** B. A stage micrometer
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-22 -->

Explanation: A stage micrometer is a slide with a known scale, and comparing it with the eyepiece graticule at each magnification gives the size of each graticule unit. A thermometer, a pH meter and a balance do not measure length under a microscope.

**A23.** C. Cell Q has the greater SA:V, at 3.5:1 compared with 3:1 for cell P.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-23 -->

Explanation: Cell Q has a surface area of 28 µm² against 24 µm² for cell P, while both have a volume of 8 µm³, giving 3.5:1 against 3:1. Equal volumes do not mean equal SA:V ratios, and a cube does not have the highest SA:V of all shapes for a given volume.

**A24.** Surface area = 2(2 × 3 + 3 × 5 + 2 × 5) = 2(6 + 15 + 10) = 62 µm² [1]; volume = 2 × 3 × 5 = 30 µm³ [1]; SA:V = 62 ÷ 30 ≈ 2.1:1 [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-24 -->

Explanation:

**A25.** The claim is incorrect [1]; the large cell has more total membrane but a lower SA:V, so less membrane is available per unit of cytoplasm to exchange materials [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-25 -->

Explanation:

**A26.** Magnification is how many times larger the image appears than the specimen [1]; resolution is the smallest distance between two points that can still be seen as separate points [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-26 -->

Explanation:

**A27.** Actual length = 60 mm ÷ 1500 = 0.04 mm [1]; 0.04 mm × 1000 = 40 µm [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-27 -->

Explanation:

**A28.** Stated that all cells arise only from pre-existing cells, by cell division [1]; this completed cell theory by explaining how new cells, and therefore growth and repair, are produced [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-28 -->

Explanation:

**A29.** As side length increases, the SA:V ratio decreases, so the two are inversely proportional [1]; for a cube, SA:V = 6 ÷ side length [1]; surface area increases with the square of the side length while volume increases with the cube, so volume increases faster [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-ct-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells

**A30.** A. Ribosomes
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-1 -->

Explanation: All cells have ribosomes (prokaryotes have smaller 70S ribosomes, eukaryotes 80S in the cytosol). The other structures are membrane-bound organelles found only in eukaryotes.

**A31.** D. a prokaryote.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-2 -->

Explanation: Circular DNA in a nucleoid region, plasmids and the absence of membrane-bound organelles are defining features of prokaryotes. Fungi and plants have cell walls but are eukaryotes with nuclei.

**A32.** B. Presence of a cell wall and large central vacuole
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-3 -->

Explanation: Plant cells have a cellulose cell wall, a large central vacuole and often chloroplasts. Both cell types have a nucleus, plasma membrane and cytoplasm.

**A33.** B. prokaryotes 0.1–5 µm; eukaryotes 10–100 µm.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-4 -->

Explanation: Most prokaryotes are about 0.1–5 µm, while most eukaryotic cells are about 10–100 µm. Nanometre values are far too small for whole cells.

**A34.** D. It is a single circular chromosome located in the nucleoid region, often with plasmids.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-5 -->

Explanation: Prokaryotes have one main circular chromosome in the nucleoid (not membrane-bound) and may carry small circular plasmids. Linear, histone-bound DNA in a nucleus is eukaryotic.

**A35.** Any two: eukaryotes have a membrane-bound nucleus while prokaryotes have a nucleoid region; eukaryotes have membrane-bound organelles (e.g. mitochondria, ER) while prokaryotes do not; prokaryotic DNA is circular while eukaryotic DNA is linear; prokaryotes have smaller (70S) ribosomes; prokaryotes are generally much smaller.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-6 -->

Explanation:

**A36.** A plant (eukaryotic, photosynthetic) cell; the nucleus and mitochondria show it is eukaryotic, and the chloroplasts and cell wall together indicate a plant cell (animal cells lack both; fungi lack chloroplasts).
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-7 -->

Explanation:

**A37.** Organelles separate incompatible reactions and keep enzymes and substrates concentrated in specific compartments (e.g. hydrolytic enzymes in lysosomes); this increases the efficiency of metabolism and allows eukaryotic cells to be larger and more complex than prokaryotes.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-8 -->

Explanation:

**A38.** C. peptidoglycan.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-9 -->

Explanation: Bacterial cell walls contain peptidoglycan, a polymer of sugars and amino acids. Plant walls are made of cellulose and fungal walls of chitin, while lignin is a strengthening material added to some plant walls rather than a bacterial component.

**A39.** D. Mitochondrion.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-10 -->

Explanation: Mitochondria carry their own 70S ribosomes, consistent with their bacterial origin. The Golgi apparatus, lysosomes and plasma membrane do not contain 70S ribosomes, and the ribosomes in the cytosol are 80S.

**A40.** B. They carry small extra genes, such as antibiotic resistance, that can be passed between bacteria.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-11 -->

Explanation: Plasmids are small, separate circles of DNA that can carry extra genes, such as antibiotic resistance, and can be transferred between bacteria. The main chromosome is the nucleoid DNA rather than a plasmid, and glycogen storage and aerobic respiration are not plasmid functions.

**A41.** D. 5 µm.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-12 -->

Explanation: Actual size = image size ÷ magnification. The image is 2.5 mm = 2500 µm, and 2500 ÷ 500 = 5 µm. Leaving the value as 2500 µm ignores the magnification, 50 µm comes from dividing by 50 instead of 500, and 0.005 µm comes from treating 2.5 mm as 2.5 µm.

**A42.** A. Electrons have a much shorter wavelength than visible light, giving much higher resolution.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-13 -->

Explanation: The electron beam has a much shorter wavelength than visible light, so resolution is far higher and tiny structures can be seen. The other options are false: electron micrographs are black and white (or false colour), and specimens are prepared in a vacuum, so living cells cannot be viewed.

**A43.** A. Mitochondria have their own circular DNA and 70S ribosomes, similar to bacteria.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-14 -->

Explanation: Mitochondria have their own circular DNA and 70S ribosomes, features they share with bacteria. This supports the idea that they were once free-living prokaryotes engulfed by a larger cell. Mitochondria are found in plant and animal cells, have no chlorophyll and are surrounded by a double membrane.

**A44.** D. Capsule.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-15 -->

Explanation: A capsule is a sticky layer of polysaccharide outside the cell wall that can help bacteria evade the immune system and adhere to surfaces. A flagellum is used for movement, ribosomes make proteins and the nucleoid holds the chromosome.

**A45.** C. Binary fission produces two genetically identical cells, and under favourable conditions this can happen quickly.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-16 -->

Explanation: Binary fission copies the single chromosome and splits the cell into two genetically identical daughter cells, without mitosis or meiosis. Under favourable conditions it can be very rapid, so populations can double quickly. Fusion would reduce cell numbers rather than increase them.

**A46.** B. Saccharomyces cerevisiae (baker's yeast).
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-17 -->

Explanation: Yeast is a single-celled fungus with a membrane-bound nucleus and membrane-bound organelles. E. coli, Streptococcus and cyanobacteria are all prokaryotes, even though cyanobacteria are often called blue-green algae.

**A47.** C. 20.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-18 -->

Explanation: 40 ÷ 2 = 20 bacteria. Subtracting the sizes (38) or multiplying them (80) does not count how many fit along a length, and 2 is simply the bacterium's diameter in µm.

**A48.** B. It contains free 70S ribosomes and the nucleoid region.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-19 -->

Explanation: Prokaryotic cytoplasm holds free 70S ribosomes and the nucleoid, with no membrane-bound organelles. Mitochondria are eukaryotic, the nuclear envelope surrounds a eukaryotic nucleus, and ribosomes are present in all cells.

**A49.** A. Chloroplast.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-20 -->

Explanation: Chloroplasts are photosynthetic organelles with their own circular DNA and 70S ribosomes, and they are absent from animal and fungal cells. Lysosomes and centrioles are found in animal cells, and a vacuole is a membrane-bound sac with no DNA of its own.

**A50.** B. Human cells do not make peptidoglycan, so penicillin has no target in them.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-21 -->

Explanation: Penicillin blocks the cross-linking of peptidoglycan, a compound that human cells do not make, so there is no target in human cells. The option about 70S ribosomes is wrong because human cells have 80S ribosomes, and penicillin does not act on ribosomes.

**A51.** A. Pili.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-22 -->

Explanation: Pili are short, hair-like protein projections on bacteria used for attachment to surfaces, and some are used to transfer DNA between cells. Mitochondria, the nucleus and centrioles are all found in eukaryotic animal cells.

**A52.** C. Doubling the side length to 2 µm, which halves the ratio to 3:1.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-23 -->

Explanation: For a cube, surface area to volume ratio = 6 ÷ side length, so doubling the side length from 1 µm to 2 µm halves the ratio to 3:1. Halving the side length to 0.5 µm doubles the ratio to 12:1, so the options claiming a 3:1 ratio from halving are wrong.

**A53.** Site of protein synthesis (translation) [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-24 -->

Explanation:

**A54.** The vacuole takes up water, creating turgor (internal) pressure [1]; this pushes the plasma membrane against the cellulose cell wall, which resists expansion and keeps the cell firm [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-25 -->

Explanation:

**A55.** Image size = 10 mm = 10 000 µm [1]; magnification = image size ÷ actual size = 10 000 ÷ 2 = ×5000 [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-26 -->

Explanation:

**A56.** Controls the movement of substances into and out of the cell (selectively permeable) [1]; separates the cell's contents from its external environment [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-27 -->

Explanation:

**A57.** Surface area = 6 × 3² = 54 µm² and volume = 3³ = 27 µm³ [1]; ratio = 54 ÷ 27 = 2:1 [1]; a larger ratio gives more membrane per unit volume, so exchange of substances with the environment is more efficient [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-28 -->

Explanation:

**A58.** Total magnification = 10 × 40 = ×400 [1]; image size = 8 mm = 8000 µm [1]; actual size = 8000 ÷ 400 = 20 µm [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-29 -->

Explanation:

**A59.** DNA: bacterium in a nucleoid (not membrane-bound), liver cell in a membrane-bound nucleus [1]; ribosomes: bacterium 70S, liver cell 80S [1]; membrane-bound organelles: absent in the bacterium, present in the liver cell (e.g. mitochondria, endoplasmic reticulum, Golgi apparatus) [1]; size: bacterium typically 0.1–5 µm, liver cell typically 10–100 µm [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-30 -->

Explanation:

**A60.** Binary fission replicates the single circular chromosome and the cell divides, so each daughter receives identical DNA [1]; variation arises from mutations and from the transfer of plasmids between bacteria [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-31 -->

Explanation:

**A61.** Plasmids carry genes for antibiotic resistance [1]; plasmids can be transferred from resistant to non-resistant bacteria [1]; bacteria that have received the plasmid survive treatment, reproduce and increase the proportion of resistant bacteria in the population [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pe-32 -->

Explanation:

#### Organelles and their functions

**A62.** B. Mitochondrion
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-1 -->

Explanation: The mitochondrion carries out the Krebs cycle and electron transport chain, which produce most ATP. Chloroplasts carry out photosynthesis.

**A63.** D. rough endoplasmic reticulum and Golgi apparatus.
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-2 -->

Explanation: Secreted proteins are made on ribosomes of the rough ER and modified and packaged by the Golgi apparatus as part of the secretory pathway. Smooth ER synthesises lipids.

**A64.** C. ribosome on rough ER → transport vesicle → Golgi apparatus → secretory vesicle → plasma membrane
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-3 -->

Explanation: Proteins are synthesised by ribosomes on the rough ER, carried in vesicles to the Golgi for modification and packaging, then secretory vesicles fuse with the plasma membrane (exocytosis).

**A65.** D. be damaged by released hydrolytic enzymes digesting its own components.
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-4 -->

Explanation: Lysosomes contain hydrolytic enzymes. If released into the cytosol they can break down cell components. ATP production and protein synthesis depend on other organelles.

**A66.** A. Smooth ER: synthesis of lipids and detoxification
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-5 -->

Explanation: Smooth ER makes lipids (including steroids) and detoxifies drugs, e.g. in liver cells. Ribosomes synthesise polypeptides, the Golgi packages them, and the nucleolus makes ribosomal subunits.

**A67.** Double membrane-bound organelle; contains stacks of thylakoid membranes (grana) containing chlorophyll, surrounded by fluid stroma; site of photosynthesis, converting light energy into chemical energy in glucose (light-dependent reactions on thylakoids, light-independent reactions in stroma).
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-6 -->

Explanation:

**A68.** Flight muscle contracts repeatedly and has a very high energy demand; mitochondria carry out aerobic respiration producing ATP, so more mitochondria supply the large amounts of ATP needed for contraction, whereas skin cells require far less.
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-7 -->

Explanation:

**A69.** The nucleus contains the DNA (chromosomes) holding the genetic instructions; genes are transcribed into mRNA which leaves through nuclear pores to ribosomes, so the nucleus controls which proteins (including enzymes) are made and hence cell activities.
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-8 -->

Explanation:

**A70.** B. The central vacuole takes up water, pressing the cytoplasm against the cell wall
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-9 -->

Explanation: Water enters the central vacuole by osmosis, creating turgor pressure against the rigid cell wall, which keeps the cell firm. The cell wall does not swell, and chloroplasts and lysosomes do not store water.

**A71.** C. A cell wall made mainly of cellulose
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-10 -->

Explanation: Plant cells have a rigid cell wall of cellulose outside the cell membrane, which animal cells lack. Nuclei, cell membranes and ribosomes are found in both cell types.

**A72.** D. Organising spindle fibres during cell division
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-11 -->

Explanation: Centrioles, found in animal cells, help organise the spindle fibres that separate chromosomes during mitosis. Photosynthesis, starch storage and digestion of bacteria are not centriole functions; the last is a lysosome function.

**A73.** A. Sorting, modifying and packaging proteins into vesicles
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-12 -->

Explanation: The Golgi apparatus modifies, sorts and packages proteins into vesicles, while the rough ER synthesises polypeptides on its ribosomes. Hydrogen peroxide is broken down in peroxisomes and ATP is made in mitochondria.

**A74.** B. Cytoskeleton
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-13 -->

Explanation: The cytoskeleton is a network of protein fibres that supports cell shape and moves organelles and vesicles. Animal cells have no cell wall, the nuclear envelope surrounds the nucleus, and the large central vacuole is a plant cell feature.

**A75.** C. It has ribosomes attached to its outer surface
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-14 -->

Explanation: Rough ER has ribosomes attached to its surface, which is why it is the main site of protein synthesis for export. Smooth ER lacks ribosomes and is the site of lipid synthesis, while chlorophyll is found in chloroplasts.

**A76.** D. Lysosome
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-15 -->

Explanation: Lysosomes contain hydrolytic enzymes that digest engulfed material such as bacteria. Ribosomes make proteins, chloroplasts carry out photosynthesis, and the nucleolus makes ribosomal subunits.

**A77.** A. It increases the surface area for electron transport chain proteins and ATP synthase
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-16 -->

Explanation: Folding increases the membrane surface area, so more electron transport chain proteins and ATP synthase can be packed in, increasing ATP production. The other options describe functions that do not relate to cristae.

**A78.** B. Stroma
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-17 -->

Explanation: The light-independent reactions (Calvin cycle) occur in the fluid stroma, using ATP and NADPH made in the light-dependent reactions. Thylakoid membranes are where the light-dependent reactions occur, and cristae are found in mitochondria.

**A79.** D. DNA wound around histone proteins
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-18 -->

Explanation: Chromosomes are DNA bound to histone proteins, which package the DNA within the nucleus. RNA, lipids, glucose polymers, ribosomes and ATP are not the main components of chromosomes.

**A80.** A. Both contain their own DNA and ribosomes
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-19 -->

Explanation: Both organelles contain their own DNA and ribosomes, which is consistent with the endosymbiotic theory. Both have a double membrane, only chloroplasts release oxygen, and mitochondria occur in plant cells as well as animal cells.

**A81.** B. Golgi apparatus
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-20 -->

Explanation: Lysosomes form from vesicles that bud off the Golgi apparatus. Mitochondria and chloroplasts do not give rise to lysosomes, and the nucleolus is located inside the nucleus.

**A82.** Site of protein synthesis (translation of mRNA into a polypeptide) [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-21 -->

Explanation:

**A83.** Production of ribosomal subunits, including synthesis of ribosomal RNA [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-22 -->

Explanation:

**A84.** Lack of a nucleus leaves more space for haemoglobin, so more oxygen can be carried per cell [1]; without mitochondria the cell does not use oxygen for its own respiration, so more oxygen is delivered to the tissues [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-23 -->

Explanation:

**A85.** The cell wall is rigid and strong, made of cellulose [1]; it resists the outward pressure (turgor pressure) generated by the vacuole and cytoplasm, so the cell does not burst [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-24 -->

Explanation:

**A86.** Membrane-bound sacs (vesicles) containing hydrolytic (digestive) enzymes [1]; the enzymes break down materials taken into the cell, such as food particles [1]; they also digest worn-out organelles so their components can be recycled [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-25 -->

Explanation:

**A87.** Double membrane: a smooth outer membrane and an inner membrane [1]; the inner membrane is folded into cristae [1]; a fluid matrix containing enzymes, mitochondrial DNA and ribosomes [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-26 -->

Explanation:

**A88.** mRNA is made in the nucleus by transcription of DNA [1]; mRNA cannot pass through the nuclear pores into the cytoplasm [1]; ribosomes in the cytoplasm cannot receive the mRNA template for translation [1]; so fewer proteins (including enzymes) are made, disrupting cell activities [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-27 -->

Explanation:

**A89.** Both have a double outer membrane [1]; the chloroplast has thylakoids stacked in grana containing chlorophyll and a stroma, while the mitochondrion has cristae and a matrix [1]; the chloroplast carries out photosynthesis, converting light energy into chemical energy in glucose [1]; the mitochondrion carries out aerobic respiration, releasing energy from glucose to make ATP [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-28 -->

Explanation:

**A90.** The claim is incorrect [1]; prokaryotic cells such as bacteria have no nucleus, and their DNA lies in a nucleoid region in the cytoplasm [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-or-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Plasma membrane structure

**A91.** B. the scattered pattern of proteins embedded in the phospholipid bilayer.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-1 -->

Explanation: 'Mosaic' describes the varied proteins dotted through the bilayer; 'fluid' describes the lateral movement of phospholipids and proteins.

**A92.** D. their phosphate heads are hydrophilic and their fatty acid tails are hydrophobic.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-2 -->

Explanation: The amphipathic phospholipids orient with hydrophilic heads facing the aqueous fluid on both sides and hydrophobic tails facing inward, away from water.

**A93.** A. Cholesterol
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-3 -->

Explanation: Cholesterol reduces fluidity at high temperatures and prevents tight packing of phospholipids at low temperatures. Glycoproteins are involved in cell recognition.

**A94.** B. glycoprotein.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-4 -->

Explanation: Glycoproteins and glycolipids on the outer surface act as recognition markers (e.g. MHC markers). Phospholipids and cholesterol are structural.

**A95.** B. have kinks that prevent tight packing, keeping the membrane fluid at low temperatures.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-5 -->

Explanation: Double bonds create kinks in unsaturated tails, so phospholipids cannot pack closely, which maintains fluidity in cold conditions.

**A96.** A phospholipid bilayer with hydrophilic heads facing outward and hydrophobic tails inward; proteins (integral/transmembrane and peripheral) are embedded in or attached to the bilayer in a mosaic pattern, with cholesterol and glycoproteins/glycolipids present; components can move laterally, so the membrane is fluid.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-6 -->

Explanation:

**A97.** O₂ is small and non-polar so it dissolves in and passes through the hydrophobic core of the bilayer; Na⁺ is charged and is repelled by the hydrophobic tails, so it needs channel or carrier proteins to cross.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-7 -->

Explanation:

**A98.** Any two: transport (channel or carrier proteins); receptors for signalling molecules such as hormones; enzymes; cell recognition (glycoproteins); attachment/adhesion to other cells or the cytoskeleton.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-8 -->

Explanation:

**A99.** A. nonpolar amino acids whose side chains interact with the fatty acid tails.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-9 -->

Explanation: Nonpolar side chains interact favourably with the fatty acid tails in the core. Polar or charged residues would be repelled by the core, so they are found at the ends of the protein facing the aqueous fluid.

**A100.** C. A carbohydrate head group in place of a phosphate-containing head group.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-10 -->

Explanation: Glycolipids have a sugar head group instead of the phosphate-containing head found in phospholipids, and they are located on the outer surface of the membrane. A protein chain is not part of a glycolipid, which is why the last option is wrong.

**A101.** B. the phosphate heads would have to pass through the hydrophobic core of the bilayer.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-11 -->

Explanation: Moving a charged, hydrophilic head through the nonpolar core is energetically unfavourable, so flip-flop is rare while sideways movement within one layer is common. The layers are held together by hydrophobic interactions, not covalent bonds, which makes the first option wrong.

**A102.** C. Fluorescent markers on proteins of two fused cells spread over the whole combined membrane over time.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-12 -->

Explanation: Labelled proteins that mix across a fused membrane show that they are free to move sideways in the bilayer. The other options describe the distribution or structure of proteins, not their movement.

**A103.** A. A hydrophilic lining that forms a water-filled pathway through the bilayer, so Na⁺ does not contact the hydrophobic core.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-13 -->

Explanation: Charged ions need a hydrophilic pathway through the membrane, which a water-filled pore provides. A hydrophobic lining would repel the charged ion rather than let it pass.

**A104.** D. more fluid, because short tails have fewer contacts between neighbouring tails.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-14 -->

Explanation: Shorter tails have less contact (van der Waals attraction) between neighbouring tails, so they pack less tightly and the membrane is more fluid. The option claiming closer packing is tempting, but it reverses the effect of shorter tails.

**A105.** B. A carrier protein binds a specific substance and changes shape to move it across, while a channel protein forms a pore.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-15 -->

Explanation: Channel proteins form open pores, whereas carrier proteins bind a specific solute and change shape to move it. Some carriers use ATP but many do not, so the option saying carriers always use ATP is wrong.

**A106.** B. Cellulose
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-16 -->

Explanation: Cellulose is a structural polysaccharide found in plant cell walls and is absent from animal plasma membranes. Cholesterol, glycoproteins and phospholipids are all found in animal plasma membranes.

**A107.** C. the phospholipids gain kinetic energy, so they move more and the bilayer becomes looser.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-17 -->

Explanation: Higher temperature increases the kinetic energy of the phospholipids, so they move more and pack less tightly. Cholesterol does not melt; it actually reduces fluidity changes at high temperatures.

**A108.** D. has a shape and chemical properties complementary to that hormone.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-18 -->

Explanation: Specific binding depends on a complementary shape and chemistry, so only the matching hormone fits the site. Receptors are proteins, not phospholipids, which makes the lipid-soluble option wrong.

**A109.** B. Among the phospholipid fatty acid tails, with its small hydroxyl group near the phospholipid heads.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-19 -->

Explanation: Cholesterol inserts between phospholipids, with its hydroxyl group near the polar heads and its rigid ring and hydrocarbon body among the fatty acid tails. The option placing the ring in the aqueous fluid is wrong because the ring is hydrophobic.

**A110.** A. It allows membranes to bend, fuse and mix their components during vesicle budding and fusion.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-20 -->

Explanation: A fluid bilayer lets membranes bend and fuse, and lets lipids and proteins rearrange as vesicles bud from and fuse with membranes. A rigid membrane would make these changes difficult, which is why the rigidity option is wrong.

**A111.** A. Carbohydrate chains of glycoproteins and glycolipids are found only on the extracellular (outer) surface.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-21 -->

Explanation: The plasma membrane is asymmetric, and its carbohydrate chains are always on the outer surface. Cholesterol occurs in both leaflets, and the two leaflets differ in composition, so the other options are wrong.

**A112.** D. Phospholipids
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-22 -->

Explanation: Phospholipids form the bilayer and are the most abundant lipid in the membrane. Cholesterol is present but in smaller amounts, and triglycerides are energy-storage lipids that do not form membrane bilayers.

**A113.** Peptide hormones are polar and charged, so they cannot pass through the hydrophobic core of the bilayer [1]; they bind to a specific receptor protein on the outer surface of the membrane [1]; the binding causes a change in the receptor that triggers a response inside the cell, such as a second messenger [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-23 -->

Explanation:

**A114.** The product of one enzyme is passed directly to the next enzyme in the sequence [1]; this reduces the time for intermediates to diffuse away, so the pathway runs faster [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-24 -->

Explanation:

**A115.** Hydrophobic tails exposed at the edges of the tear are unfavourable in water [1]; the phospholipids rearrange so the tails are shielded and the heads face the water, closing the gap [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-25 -->

Explanation:

**A116.** 3 200 : 12 800 = 1 : 4 [1]; both values are divided by 3 200 (12 800 ÷ 3 200 = 4) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-26 -->

Explanation:

**A117.** 20 × 0.15 nm = 3.0 nm [1]; this equals the thickness of the hydrophobic core, so the helix can just span the bilayer [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-27 -->

Explanation:

**A118.** The high temperature damages the membranes, disrupting their structure and denaturing their proteins [1]; the membranes lose their selective permeability [1]; the red betalain pigment stored in the cells diffuses out into the water [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pm-28 -->

Explanation:

#### Passive transport across membranes

**A119.** D. requires membrane channel or carrier proteins.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-1 -->

Explanation: Facilitated diffusion is passive (no ATP) and occurs down a concentration gradient but needs a protein channel or carrier. Moving against a gradient is active transport.

**A120.** A. Water leaves the cell and it shrinks (crenates).
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-2 -->

Explanation: In a hypertonic solution the solute concentration outside is higher, so water moves out by osmosis and the cell shrinks. Osmosis is the movement of water, not solute.

**A121.** B. the cell wall resists expansion, so the cell becomes turgid.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-3 -->

Explanation: Water enters by osmosis, but the rigid cellulose wall exerts pressure back, so the cell becomes turgid rather than bursting.

**A122.** A. The potato cells are isotonic to about 0.3 mol L⁻¹ sucrose.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-4 -->

Explanation: No net mass change means no net water movement, so the cell sap is approximately isotonic to 0.3 mol L⁻¹. At 0.6 mol L⁻¹ the solution is hypertonic to the cells, so they lost water.

**A123.** A. molecules gain kinetic energy and move faster.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-5 -->

Explanation: Higher temperature increases the kinetic energy of particles, so they move and collide more, increasing diffusion rate. Diffusion does not use ATP.

**A124.** The net movement of water molecules across a selectively (partially) permeable membrane; from a region of lower solute concentration (higher free water concentration) to a region of higher solute concentration.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A125.** Cl⁻ ions normally diffuse out through CFTR channels into the mucus (facilitated diffusion); without this, solute concentration outside the cell stays low; so less water follows by osmosis into the mucus, leaving it dehydrated, thick and sticky.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A126.** Glucose is a relatively large, polar molecule that cannot pass through the hydrophobic core of the phospholipid bilayer; it needs specific carrier (GLUT) proteins to move down its concentration gradient.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A127.** A. oxygen (O₂)
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-9 -->

Explanation: Small non-polar molecules such as O₂ dissolve in the hydrophobic core of the bilayer and diffuse directly. Glucose, charged ions and amino acids are too large or too polar to cross the core without a carrier or channel protein.

**A128.** B. Water moves in and out at equal rates, so there is no net change in cell volume.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-10 -->

Explanation: An isotonic solution has the same solute concentration as the cytoplasm, so water moves in and out at equal rates and there is no net change. The option describing lower solute outside the cell describes a hypotonic solution, which makes the cell swell, not shrink.

**A129.** C. the amount of ATP in the cell
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-11 -->

Explanation: Simple diffusion is passive, so its rate depends on the concentration gradient, surface area, diffusion distance, temperature and molecular size. ATP is used only in active transport. Temperature is a tempting wrong answer because it does change the kinetic energy of particles.

**A130.** D. sodium ions pumped out of a cell against their gradient by the sodium–potassium pump
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-12 -->

Explanation: Active transport moves substances against their concentration gradient using energy from ATP, as the sodium–potassium pump does. The other three move down a concentration gradient without ATP, so they are passive.

**A131.** A. plasmolysis
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-13 -->

Explanation: Plasmolysis occurs when a plant cell loses water to a hypertonic solution and the protoplast shrinks away from the cell wall. Lysis is bursting and crenation is shrinking, both of animal cells; turgidity is the firm state of a plant cell in a hypotonic solution.

**A132.** B. a high surface area to volume ratio
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-14 -->

Explanation: A high surface area to volume ratio means the membrane is large relative to the cell's contents, so diffusion can reach every part of the cell quickly. A low ratio, or a thick membrane that slows diffusion, makes supply harder. A cell wall does not improve diffusion into the cell.

**A133.** C. glucose only
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-15 -->

Explanation: Glucose molecules are small enough to pass through the pores of the tubing and diffuse out, while starch molecules are too large and stay inside. The starch-only option reverses the effect of pore size, and the both option ignores the size limit.

**A134.** D. all the carrier proteins are occupied, so transport is at its maximum rate
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-16 -->

Explanation: Facilitated diffusion depends on a limited number of carrier proteins. When all are occupied, adding more glucose cannot increase the rate. The gradient still exists and the process stays passive, so reversal and ATP-driven pumping are wrong.

**A135.** A. It has a lower solute concentration than the cytoplasm, so water tends to move into the cell by osmosis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-17 -->

Explanation: A hypotonic solution has less solute than the cytoplasm, so water moves into the cell by osmosis. The description of higher solute outside the cell is a hypertonic solution. Osmosis moves water, not solute, so the last option is wrong.

**A136.** C. Into the cell, down its concentration gradient from the higher outside concentration.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-18 -->

Explanation: Oxygen is at a higher concentration outside (0.10 mmol L⁻¹) than inside (0.02 mmol L⁻¹), so it diffuses into the cell down the gradient without ATP. Its non-polar nature allows it to cross, so there is net movement, and active transport is not needed for a downhill move.

**A137.** D. 12.0%
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-19 -->

Explanation: Percentage change = (change in mass ÷ initial mass) × 100 = (0.60 ÷ 5.00) × 100 = 12.0%. Dividing by the final mass gives 10.7%, leaving out the ×100 gives 0.12%, and 0.60% is just the raw mass gain.

**A138.** A. the sucrose concentration of the solutions
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-20 -->

Explanation: The independent variable is the one the investigator deliberately changes, here the sucrose concentration. The change in mass is measured, so it is the dependent variable, and length and time are kept constant as controlled variables.

**A139.** B. each binding site has a shape that fits only particular molecules
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-21 -->

Explanation: Specificity comes from the shape of the binding site on the carrier protein, which is complementary to the shape of one type of molecule. Carrier proteins are not made of lipid, and facilitated diffusion does not use ATP. Water is moved by aquaporins, not carried along by carriers.

**A140.** C. approximately halve
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-22 -->

Explanation: The rate of diffusion is inversely proportional to the diffusion distance, so doubling the distance roughly halves the rate. Doubling the rate reverses the relationship, staying the same ignores distance, and a fourfold increase wrongly applies a squared relationship.

**A141.** The net movement of particles from a region of higher concentration to a region of lower concentration [1]; down a concentration gradient, without the input of energy (ATP) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-23 -->

Explanation:

**A142.** Heat denatures the membrane proteins and disrupts the phospholipid bilayer, making the membrane more permeable [1]; so the pigment molecules can diffuse out of the cell down their concentration gradient [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-24 -->

Explanation:

**A143.** Similarity: both are passive and require no ATP [1]; both move substances down a concentration gradient [1]. Difference: simple diffusion needs no membrane protein, whereas facilitated diffusion requires channel or carrier proteins [1]; facilitated diffusion can become saturated when all carriers are occupied, whereas the rate of simple diffusion rises in proportion to the gradient [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-25 -->

Explanation:

**A144.** Iodine molecules are small enough to pass through the pores of the dialysis tubing, moving down their concentration gradient into the bag [1]; the iodine reacts with starch to form a blue-black colour [1]; starch molecules are too large to pass through the pores and remain in the bag [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-26 -->

Explanation:

**A145.** Aquaporins are channel proteins embedded in the membrane [1]; they allow water molecules to cross the membrane rapidly by facilitated diffusion, down the water concentration gradient [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-27 -->

Explanation:

**A146.** Saline is isotonic to the cytoplasm of the red blood cells [1]; so water moves in and out at equal rates, with no net water movement [1]. Distilled water is hypotonic to the cytoplasm (lower solute concentration) [1]; so water moves into the cells by osmosis, causing them to swell and lyse (burst) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-28 -->

Explanation:

**A147.** The concentration gradient becomes smaller as particles spread out [1]; so there is a smaller difference between the rates of particles moving in each direction, reducing the net movement [1]; at equilibrium particles move in both directions at equal rates, so the net rate is zero [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pt-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Active transport, endocytosis and exocytosis

**A148.** D. 3 Na⁺ out of the cell and 2 K⁺ in, using ATP.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-1 -->

Explanation: The Na⁺/K⁺ pump uses one ATP per cycle to move 3 Na⁺ out and 2 K⁺ in, both against their concentration gradients.

**A149.** C. phagocytosis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-2 -->

Explanation: Phagocytosis ('cell eating') is a type of endocytosis in which large solid particles are engulfed in a vesicle. Pinocytosis takes in fluid.

**A150.** D. requires ATP from respiration and so is active transport.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-3 -->

Explanation: Uptake against a concentration gradient that stops when respiration (ATP production) is blocked indicates active transport. Passive processes would not depend on ATP.

**A151.** B. Exocytosis
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-4 -->

Explanation: Insulin is a protein packaged into secretory vesicles by the Golgi; the vesicles fuse with the plasma membrane and release their contents by exocytosis.

**A152.** B. all carrier proteins being in use (saturated).
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-5 -->

Explanation: Carrier-mediated transport plateaus when all carrier proteins are occupied. Dependence on oxygen indicates ATP is needed, so it is active transport.

**A153.** Similarity: both use specific membrane proteins; difference: active transport moves substances against their concentration gradient whereas facilitated diffusion moves them down the gradient; active transport requires energy from ATP whereas facilitated diffusion is passive.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-6 -->

Explanation:

**A154.** The plasma membrane folds inward (invaginates) around the material outside the cell; the membrane pinches off to form a vesicle that carries the material into the cytoplasm; it requires energy (ATP) and is a form of bulk transport.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-7 -->

Explanation:

**A155.** Active transport requires ATP to power protein pumps; mitochondria produce most ATP through aerobic respiration, so many mitochondria supply the energy needed for continuous active transport.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-8 -->

Explanation:

**A156.** D. ATP
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-9 -->

Explanation: ATP is hydrolysed to ADP and inorganic phosphate, releasing the energy the pump uses to change shape. Glucose and oxygen are used in aerobic respiration to make ATP, so they are not the molecule that directly powers the pump.

**A157.** A. Release of a neurotransmitter from a vesicle into the synapse
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-10 -->

Explanation: In exocytosis a vesicle fuses with the plasma membrane and releases its contents outside the cell, as neurotransmitters are released into a synapse. The other options are passive movements through membrane proteins or osmosis, with no vesicle fusing with the membrane.

**A158.** C. pinocytosis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-11 -->

Explanation: Pinocytosis ('cell drinking') is endocytosis of fluid and dissolved solutes in small vesicles. Phagocytosis takes in large solid particles, exocytosis releases material from the cell, and osmosis is the passive movement of water.

**A159.** B. 800
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-12 -->

Explanation: 1200 ÷ 3 = 400 cycles, and each cycle moves 2 K⁺ in, giving 400 × 2 = 800 K⁺. Dividing 1200 by 2 gives 600, which uses the K⁺ number in the wrong place, and 400 counts cycles rather than ions.

**A160.** D. 100
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-13 -->

Explanation: 300 ÷ 3 = 100 cycles, each using one ATP, so 100 ATP molecules are hydrolysed. Using 300 assumes one ATP per ion, and 150 divides by the K⁺ number instead of the Na⁺ number.

**A161.** A. Lysosome
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-14 -->

Explanation: Lysosomes contain hydrolytic enzymes that fuse with the phagosome and break down the engulfed material. Ribosomes make proteins, centrioles organise microtubules, and the nucleolus makes ribosomal subunits.

**A162.** B. Its fluid phospholipid bilayer
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-15 -->

Explanation: The phospholipid bilayer is fluid, so the membrane can deform and merge with a vesicle membrane. Cellulose is a component of plant cell walls, not the plasma membrane, and membrane proteins can move within the bilayer.

**A163.** C. Uptake of specific molecules bound to receptors in coated pits
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-16 -->

Explanation: In receptor-mediated endocytosis, specific molecules bind to receptor proteins in clathrin-coated pits, which then pinch off as vesicles, making the process selective. Random fluid uptake is pinocytosis, engulfment of particles is phagocytosis, and hormone release is exocytosis.

**A164.** C. It moves substances down their concentration gradients.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-17 -->

Explanation: Active transport moves substances against their concentration gradients using membrane proteins and ATP. Moving substances down a gradient is passive transport, such as diffusion, and does not need ATP.

**A165.** A. It fuses with the plasma membrane, adding to the cell surface membrane.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-18 -->

Explanation: When the vesicle fuses with the plasma membrane, its membrane becomes part of the cell surface membrane and its contents are released outside. Pinching off inside the cytoplasm describes endocytosis, the opposite process.

**A166.** B. Endocytosis moves material in bulk inside vesicles, while active transport moves individual molecules or ions through membrane proteins.
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-19 -->

Explanation: Endocytosis is bulk transport of material within membrane-bound vesicles, whereas active transport moves individual molecules or ions through membrane proteins. Both processes need ATP, so the last option is wrong, and active transport moves substances against gradients, not down them.

**A167.** Golgi apparatus [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-20 -->

Explanation:

**A168.** Endocytosis requires energy from ATP, whereas simple diffusion is passive [1]; endocytosis moves material in bulk inside a membrane-bound vesicle, whereas diffusion moves individual small molecules directly across the membrane [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-21 -->

Explanation:

**A169.** The membrane must extend outwards to surround the bacterium [1]; the flexible phospholipid bilayer and cytoskeleton allow the membrane to deform and enclose the bacterium in a vesicle [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-22 -->

Explanation:

**A170.** Three Na⁺ bind to the pump protein on the inside of the cell [1]; ATP is hydrolysed, the phosphate attaches to the pump and it changes shape, releasing the Na⁺ outside [1]; two K⁺ bind from outside, the phosphate is released and the pump returns to its original shape, releasing the K⁺ inside [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-23 -->

Explanation:

**A171.** The enzyme is synthesised on ribosomes attached to the rough endoplasmic reticulum [1]; it is transported to the Golgi apparatus, where it is packaged into vesicles [1]; the vesicles move to the plasma membrane, fuse with it and release the enzyme outside the cell by exocytosis [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-24 -->

Explanation:

**A172.** In endocytosis the membrane folds inwards around material outside the cell and pinches off a vesicle that carries it into the cytoplasm [1]; in exocytosis a vesicle fuses with the plasma membrane and releases its contents outside [1]; both processes require energy from ATP [1]; the fluid phospholipid bilayer allows the membrane to deform and fuse with vesicle membranes [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-at-25 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 1 AOS 2: Cell division and systems

#### Binary fission

**A173.** C. prokaryotes such as bacteria.
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-1 -->

Explanation: Binary fission is how prokaryotes reproduce asexually. Eukaryotic cells divide by mitosis and cytokinesis.

**A174.** A. DNA replicates → copies move to opposite ends as the cell elongates → septum/cell wall forms → two cells separate
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-2 -->

Explanation: The circular chromosome is copied, the copies separate as the cell elongates, then the membrane pinches in and a new wall (septum) forms between them. There is no spindle or nuclear envelope in prokaryotes.

**A175.** C. 64
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-3 -->

Explanation: 2 hours = 120 min = 6 generations. 2⁶ = 64 cells.

**A176.** D. Because each receives a copy of the replicated circular chromosome.
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-4 -->

Explanation: The single chromosome is replicated and one copy goes to each daughter cell, so they are clones. Crossing over and meiosis occur in sexual reproduction of eukaryotes.

**A177.** C. Formation of a mitotic spindle
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-5 -->

Explanation: Prokaryotes lack spindle fibres made from microtubules. Both processes involve DNA replication, cytoplasm division and produce identical cells.

**A178.** The circular chromosome (and any plasmids) is replicated; the cell elongates and the two chromosome copies move to opposite ends; the plasma membrane pinches inward and a new cell wall (septum) forms, splitting the cell into two genetically identical daughter cells.
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-6 -->

Explanation:

**A179.** 4 h = 8 generations; 10 × 2⁸ = 10 × 256 = 2560 cells; growth slows because nutrients run out / wastes accumulate / space becomes limited.
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-7 -->

Explanation:

**A180.** Binary fission is a simple, rapid process with short generation times (e.g. ~20 min); the number of cells doubles each generation, so the population grows exponentially and can reach very large numbers quickly.
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-8 -->

Explanation:

**A181.** B. small, circular DNA molecules separate from the main chromosome that can replicate independently
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-9 -->

Explanation: Plasmids are small circles of DNA found in many bacteria, often carrying non-essential genes such as antibiotic resistance. Linear chromosomes are a eukaryotic feature, so that option wrongly applies eukaryotic structure to bacteria.

**A182.** C. 12
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-10 -->

Explanation: Each generation doubles the number of cells, so 2ⁿ = 4096 gives n = 12, because 2¹² = 4096. Choosing 4096 confuses the number of cells with the number of generations.

**A183.** D. about twice its original size
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-11 -->

Explanation: The cell must roughly double its size so that each daughter cell reaches a normal size after division. Halving the size describes the daughter cells, not the parent before it divides.

**A184.** A. 1 circular chromosome
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-12 -->

Explanation: E. coli has one circular chromosome, which is replicated before division; two copies exist only briefly during replication. The 23 pairs and 46 options describe the human karyotype, not a bacterium.

**A185.** B. mutation and transfer of DNA between cells, such as plasmid exchange
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-13 -->

Explanation: Bacteria do not undergo meiosis or fertilisation, so their variation comes from mutation and from transfer of DNA such as plasmids between cells. The other three options occur only in the sexual reproduction of eukaryotes.

**A186.** C. binary fission
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-14 -->

Explanation: Mitochondria contain their own circular DNA and divide in a way similar to binary fission, which supports the endosymbiotic theory. Mitosis is the nuclear division of eukaryotic cells, which mitochondria do not undergo.

**A187.** D. 120
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-15 -->

Explanation: 256 = 2⁸, so 8 generations are needed, and 8 × 15 = 120 minutes. The answer 240 comes from doubling the time again, while 90 comes from counting only 6 generations.

**A188.** A. the time taken for the population to double in number
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-16 -->

Explanation: Generation time is the time the population takes to double, which under given conditions equals one cycle of binary fission. The other options describe single steps or phases rather than the doubling time.

**A189.** B. a J-shaped curve that becomes steeper over time
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-17 -->

Explanation: Each generation doubles the population, so each time interval adds more cells than the one before, making the curve steeper. A straight line would mean the same number of cells is added in every interval.

**A190.** C. enzymes involved in DNA replication and cell division work more slowly
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-18 -->

Explanation: Binary fission depends on enzymes, and their rate falls as temperature drops below the optimum. The chromosome does not change shape with temperature, and bacteria do not undergo meiosis.

**A191.** A. in the middle of the cell, between the two chromosome copies
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-19 -->

Explanation: The membrane pinches inward and a new wall forms at the midpoint, between the separated chromosome copies. The septum forms before the daughter cells separate, so the last option is wrong.

**A192.** B. one-sixteenth the size
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-20 -->

Explanation: At 37 °C there are 2⁶ = 64 cells after 2 hours, and at 25 °C there are 2² = 4 cells, and 4 ÷ 64 = 1/16. Describing the population as 16 times larger inverts the ratio.

**A193.** C. 3
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-21 -->

Explanation: One chromosome plus two plasmids gives three separate circular DNA molecules. An answer of 1 ignores the plasmids, and an answer of 2 ignores the chromosome.

**A194.** The chromosome is replicated before division, so the parent has two copies of its DNA [1]; each daughter receives one complete copy, the same amount of DNA as the original parent cell [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-22 -->

Explanation:

**A195.** 8000 ÷ 250 = 32 = 2⁵, so 5 generations occurred [1]; 3 hours = 180 minutes [1]; generation time = 180 ÷ 5 = 36 minutes [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-23 -->

Explanation:

**A196.** Some bacteria carry plasmids with genes for antibiotic resistance [1]; the plasmid can be transferred to other bacteria, spreading the resistance genes [1]; the surviving resistant cells divide by binary fission, so the resistant population grows rapidly [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-24 -->

Explanation:

**A197.** Similarity: the DNA is replicated before the cell divides [1]; similarity: both produce two genetically identical daughter cells [1]; difference: binary fission does not use a spindle of microtubules, whereas mitosis does [1]; difference: a prokaryote has one circular chromosome not enclosed in a nucleus, whereas a eukaryote has multiple linear chromosomes enclosed in a nucleus [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-25 -->

Explanation:

**A198.** Each daughter cell must receive one complete copy of the chromosome [1]; separating before replication is complete would leave a cell with incomplete genetic information [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-bf-26 -->

Explanation:

#### The eukaryotic cell cycle and mitosis

**A199.** D. S
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-1 -->

Explanation: DNA synthesis occurs in the S phase of interphase. G1 and G2 are growth phases, and M is mitosis.

**A200.** C. Anaphase
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-2 -->

Explanation: In anaphase the centromeres split and spindle fibres pull sister chromatids to opposite poles. In metaphase chromosomes align at the equator.

**A201.** B. 46
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-3 -->

Explanation: Mitosis produces two daughter cells genetically identical to the parent, each with the full diploid number of 46.

**A202.** B. 1 hour
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-4 -->

Explanation: Time is proportional to the fraction of cells in that stage: 10/200 × 20 h = 1 hour.

**A203.** A. a cell plate forms between the two nuclei.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-5 -->

Explanation: Plant cells have rigid walls, so vesicles from the Golgi form a cell plate that becomes the new wall. Animal cells pinch in by a cleavage furrow.

**A204.** Prophase: chromosomes condense and become visible as two sister chromatids joined at a centromere; the nuclear envelope breaks down and spindle fibres begin to form from the centrosomes; metaphase: chromosomes line up along the equator (metaphase plate) with spindle fibres attached to each centromere.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A205.** Checkpoints are control points where the cell checks conditions before proceeding; G1 checks cell size, nutrients and DNA damage before replication, and G2 checks that DNA has been replicated completely and correctly before mitosis; if problems are detected the cycle is halted for repair or apoptosis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A206.** Mitosis produces genetically identical daughter cells; this allows growth, repair/replacement of damaged or worn-out cells, and asexual reproduction, while keeping the chromosome number constant.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A207.** A. G1, S, G2, M
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-9 -->

Explanation: The cell cycle runs G1, S (DNA replication), G2 and then M (mitosis). Orders that put M before S misplace DNA replication, which must be completed before division.

**A208.** B. 16
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-10 -->

Explanation: Eight chromosomes with two sister chromatids each gives 8 × 2 = 16 chromatids. Answering 8 counts chromosomes instead of chromatids, and 32 doubles the correct count again.

**A209.** C. It is the region where sister chromatids are joined and where spindle fibres attach.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-11 -->

Explanation: The centromere holds sister chromatids together and is the point where spindle fibres attach during mitosis. The structure that organises microtubules is the centrosome, which is not membrane-bound.

**A210.** D. Nuclear envelopes reform around each set of chromosomes at the poles.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-12 -->

Explanation: In telophase the chromosomes reach the poles and decondense, and a nuclear envelope reforms around each set. Lining up is metaphase, centromere splitting is anaphase, and condensation is prophase.

**A211.** A. They organise the spindle microtubules that pull sister chromatids apart.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-13 -->

Explanation: The centrosomes duplicate in interphase, move to opposite poles and nucleate the spindle microtubules. Mitochondria make ATP, and a cell plate is a plant cytokinesis structure rather than a centrosome role.

**A212.** B. Tubulin
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-14 -->

Explanation: Spindle fibres are microtubules built from tubulin. Actin forms the contractile ring in animal cytokinesis, while collagen and keratin are structural proteins that are not part of the spindle.

**A213.** C. They bind cyclin-dependent kinases (CDKs) and regulate progression through the cell cycle.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-15 -->

Explanation: Cyclins rise and fall in concentration through the cycle and activate cyclin-dependent kinases, which drive transitions between phases. Spindle fibres are made of tubulin, not cyclins.

**A214.** A. the number of cells in mitosis divided by the total number of cells observed
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-16 -->

Explanation: The mitotic index is the proportion of cells that are in mitosis, so the number in mitosis is divided by the total. Inverting the division gives a value above 1, which cannot be a proportion.

**A215.** B. Mitosis divides the nucleus, while cytokinesis divides the cytoplasm to form two cells.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-17 -->

Explanation: Mitosis separates the duplicated chromosomes into two nuclei, and cytokinesis divides the cytoplasm to form two cells. The reversed option swaps the two processes, and the four-cell option describes meiosis, not mitosis.

**A216.** D. Interphase occupies most of the cell cycle, while mitosis is comparatively brief.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-18 -->

Explanation: In most cells interphase (G1, S and G2) makes up the majority of the cycle, while mitosis is short. The options that make mitosis dominant or equal reverse the actual proportions.

**A217.** A. They are copies made by DNA replication of the same chromosome.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-19 -->

Explanation: S phase replicates each chromosome exactly, so sister chromatids carry identical DNA sequences. Crossing over, homologous pairing and parental origin relate to meiosis, not to sister chromatids.

**A218.** B. Anaphase is delayed until all chromosomes are correctly attached to spindle fibres.
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-20 -->

Explanation: The checkpoint holds the cell at metaphase until every chromosome is attached, so sister chromatids separate only when correctly aligned. Proceeding early would be the tempting error, risking daughter cells with missing or extra chromosomes.

**A219.** C. 8
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-21 -->

Explanation: Each round of mitosis doubles the number of cells, so 2 × 2 × 2 = 8. Six assumes each round adds two cells, and nine is 3 squared, which multiplies the number of rounds rather than doubling the cells.

**A220.** Cleavage furrow (formed by a contractile ring of actin filaments) [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-22 -->

Explanation:

**A221.** The period of the cell cycle between divisions in which the cell grows, replicates its DNA and prepares for mitosis [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-23 -->

Explanation:

**A222.** A chromosome consists of two sister chromatids joined at the centromere after S phase [1]; a chromatid is one of the two identical copies of DNA that make up a replicated chromosome [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-24 -->

Explanation:

**A223.** Telophase: the chromosomes reach the poles and decondense [1]; a nuclear envelope reforms around each set of chromosomes [1]; cytokinesis: a cleavage furrow forms and pinches the cytoplasm into two separate daughter cells [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-25 -->

Explanation:

**A224.** G1: the cell grows and makes proteins and organelles [1]; S: DNA is replicated so that each chromosome has two sister chromatids [1]; G2: the cell continues to grow, checks the replicated DNA and makes proteins needed for division, such as tubulin for spindle fibres [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-26 -->

Explanation:

**A225.** Mitosis produces two daughter cells, not four [1]; each daughter cell has the same (diploid) chromosome number as the parent, not half [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-27 -->

Explanation:

**A226.** Embryonic cells divide rapidly to produce the cells needed for growth [1]; so a greater proportion of cells are in mitosis at any one time [1]; cells in mature liver tissue divide only rarely, so few are in mitosis [1]; the mitotic index is the proportion of cells in mitosis, so it is higher in the embryo [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-28 -->

Explanation:

**A227.** Time in mitosis = 18 − (8 + 7 + 2) = 1 hour [1]; time in interphase = 8 + 7 + 2 = 17 hours [1]; 17 ÷ 18 × 100 = 94.4% (about 94%) of the cycle [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-cc-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Apoptosis and cancer

**A228.** B. programmed cell death that occurs in a controlled sequence.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-1 -->

Explanation: Apoptosis is programmed, controlled cell death. Uncontrolled death from injury is necrosis; spreading cancer is metastasis.

**A229.** D. Formation of membrane-bound apoptotic bodies that are engulfed by phagocytes
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-2 -->

Explanation: In apoptosis the cell shrinks, its DNA fragments and the membrane blebs into apoptotic bodies that phagocytes engulf without inflammation. Swelling and bursting describe necrosis.

**A230.** A. break down cell components during apoptosis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-3 -->

Explanation: Caspases are proteases activated in apoptosis that break down proteins and the cytoskeleton, leading to dismantling of the cell.

**A231.** A. cells with damaged DNA are no longer halted at checkpoints or sent into apoptosis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-4 -->

Explanation: p53 halts the cell cycle when DNA is damaged and can trigger apoptosis. Without it, damaged cells keep dividing and accumulate mutations.

**A232.** C. apoptosis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-5 -->

Explanation: Webbing between digits is removed by apoptosis during embryonic development, which shapes structures without causing inflammation.

**A233.** The intrinsic pathway is triggered by signals from within the cell (e.g. DNA damage) causing mitochondria to release cytochrome c, which activates caspases; the extrinsic pathway is triggered by external signal molecules (e.g. from immune cells) binding to death receptors on the plasma membrane, which activates caspases.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-6 -->

Explanation:

**A234.** Proto-oncogenes code for proteins that promote normal cell division; a mutation can convert it into an oncogene that is permanently 'switched on' / overactive; cells divide uncontrollably regardless of signals, forming a mass of cells (tumour).
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-7 -->

Explanation:

**A235.** Mitosis replaces cells while apoptosis removes old, damaged or unneeded cells; if apoptosis is too low, cells accumulate (e.g. cancer); if too high, tissue is lost (e.g. degenerative disease), so balance maintains a constant, healthy cell number.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-8 -->

Explanation:

**A236.** B. inhibit cell division or trigger apoptosis when a cell is damaged.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-9 -->

Explanation: Tumour suppressors such as p53 slow division or trigger apoptosis when needed, so losing them removes a brake on growth. Stimulating division describes the normal role of proto-oncogenes, and spindle fibres are structures rather than regulatory proteins.

**A237.** A. Phosphatidylserine moves to the outer surface of the membrane.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-10 -->

Explanation: Exposed phosphatidylserine acts as an 'eat me' signal that phagocytes recognise, so apoptotic cells are cleared without spilling their contents. Rupture and release of cytoplasm happens in necrosis, not apoptosis.

**A238.** D. triggering apoptosis in the infected cell.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-11 -->

Explanation: Cytotoxic T cells trigger apoptosis in the infected cell, which removes it without releasing viral particles or causing inflammation. Bursting by necrosis would release virus into the tissue, so it is the wrong mechanism.

**A239.** B. Benzene in tobacco smoke
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-12 -->

Explanation: Benzene is a chemical that damages DNA and is present in tobacco smoke. X-rays, UV-B and gamma rays are physical agents, so they are not chemical carcinogens.

**A240.** B. Cells accumulate more mutations over time from repeated DNA copying and environmental exposure.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-13 -->

Explanation: Mutations build up with each cell division and each exposure to mutagens, and several are usually needed for cancer, so risk rises over decades. The option saying older cells divide less is wrong because many adult tissues keep dividing throughout life.

**A241.** C. 4 000
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-14 -->

Explanation: Three doublings occur in 6 days: 500 → 1 000 → 2 000 → 4 000. Adding 500 cells every 2 days gives 2 000, while 8 000 counts four doublings instead of three.

**A242.** A. increases the rate of change in the base sequence of DNA.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-15 -->

Explanation: A mutagen raises the rate of DNA mutation, which may or may not lead to cancer. Necrosis, caspase activation and faster mitosis are not defining features of a mutagen.

**A243.** D. formation of new blood vessels that supply nutrients and oxygen to the tumour.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-16 -->

Explanation: A tumour larger than a few millimetres needs its own blood supply, so it stimulates new vessel growth. The other options describe apoptosis, blood-cell production and the intrinsic apoptosis pathway rather than angiogenesis.

**A244.** D. Mitosis produces two genetically identical daughter cells, whereas apoptosis is a controlled way for a cell to die.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-17 -->

Explanation: Mitosis makes two genetically identical daughter cells, whereas apoptosis is a controlled way for a cell to die. The other options swap these roles or misattribute caspase activity and changes in cell number.

**A245.** A. causing DNA damage that cells cannot repair, leading to cell death.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-18 -->

Explanation: Radiation breaks DNA strands, and cancer cells that cannot repair the damage stop dividing or undergo apoptosis. Increasing mitosis or angiogenesis would worsen the cancer, and radiotherapy does not work by boiling tissue.

**A246.** C. Loss of blood supply to a tissue, so its cells run out of oxygen and ATP
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-19 -->

Explanation: Loss of oxygen and ATP makes cells swell and burst, releasing contents that trigger inflammation. The other options describe programmed, controlled cell death, which is apoptosis.

**A247.** C. A cell with DNA damage that cannot be repaired
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-20 -->

Explanation: Apoptosis removes cells whose DNA damage cannot be repaired, which stops them passing mutations to daughter cells. The other cells are healthy and functioning, so the body keeps them.

**A248.** A. An abnormal mass of cells formed by uncontrolled cell division.
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-21 -->

Explanation: A tumour is an abnormal growth of cells, which may be benign or malignant. Apoptotic cells and phagocyte clusters are removed by the body, and red blood cells do not form tumours.

**A249.** A benign tumour stays in one place and does not invade surrounding tissue [1]; a malignant tumour invades nearby tissue and can spread to other parts of the body, disrupting organ function [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-22 -->

Explanation:

**A250.** The drugs damage DNA or block mitosis, killing cells that divide rapidly [1]; hair follicle cells and the cells lining the digestive tract also divide rapidly [1]; so these healthy cells are damaged too, causing side effects [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-23 -->

Explanation:

**A251.** 12 000 − 9 500 = 2 500 cells gained per day [1]; the tissue is growing, as its cell number is increasing [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-24 -->

Explanation:

**A252.** Mitotic index = 21 ÷ 350 × 100 [1] = 6% [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-25 -->

Explanation:

**A253.** UV radiation is absorbed by DNA, forming thymine dimers that distort the DNA [1]; if these are not repaired correctly, the DNA base sequence is changed (a mutation) [1]; mutations in genes controlling the cell cycle or apoptosis can cause cells to divide uncontrollably and form a tumour [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-26 -->

Explanation:

**A254.** Cells in a tumour continue to acquire mutations as they divide [1]; cells with mutations that increase division or help them escape apoptosis have a growth advantage and out-compete other cells [1]; this produces a more aggressive population that may spread to other tissues [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-27 -->

Explanation:

**A255.** Normal cells receive a signal from neighbouring cells that halts division [1]; cancer cells do not respond to this signal, so they keep dividing and form a mass [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-28 -->

Explanation:

**A256.** Without a working enzyme, DNA errors and damage are not corrected [1]; mutations then accumulate in genes controlling cell division, increasing the likelihood of cancer [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-29 -->

Explanation:

**A257.** Cancer cells detach from the primary tumour and invade the surrounding tissue [1]; they enter the blood or lymphatic system [1]; they travel to a distant site, where they settle and divide to form a secondary tumour [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-ap-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Stem cells and specialisation

**A258.** C. totipotent.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-1 -->

Explanation: Totipotent cells (e.g. the zygote) can form all cell types including extra-embryonic tissue. Pluripotent cells (embryonic stem cells) form all body cell types but not the placenta.

**A259.** D. multipotent.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-2 -->

Explanation: Multipotent stem cells form a limited range of cell types within a particular tissue or lineage.

**A260.** A. express different genes.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-3 -->

Explanation: All somatic cells contain the same DNA, but differentiation involves switching specific genes on or off, producing different proteins.

**A261.** A. the inner cell mass of a blastocyst.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-4 -->

Explanation: Embryonic stem cells come from the inner cell mass of a blastocyst (about 5 days after fertilisation) and are pluripotent.

**A262.** D. It can self-renew by mitosis and can differentiate into specialised cells.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-5 -->

Explanation: Stem cells are unspecialised cells that can divide repeatedly (self-renew) and differentiate into specialised cell types. Adult stem cells exist in many tissues.

**A263.** Advantage: they are pluripotent, so can potentially form any body cell type to treat many diseases (e.g. replace damaged nerve or heart cells); concern: obtaining them destroys an embryo, which some people believe has the moral status of a person.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A264.** Function is to transport oxygen; it lacks a nucleus and most organelles so there is more room for haemoglobin, and its biconcave disc shape gives a high SA:V for rapid diffusion of O₂ (and flexibility to pass through capillaries).
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A265.** Specialised cells of a similar type form tissues (e.g. cardiac muscle cells form cardiac muscle tissue); different tissues work together to form an organ (e.g. the heart contains muscle, nervous and connective tissue); organs work together in an organ system (e.g. heart and blood vessels form the circulatory system).
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A266.** A. stem cells that keep dividing and can differentiate into specialised plant cells.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-9 -->

Explanation: Meristem cells are stem cells that divide by mitosis and differentiate, so plants can keep growing. Fully differentiated cells have generally lost the ability to divide, and gametes do not form plant tissue in a meristem.

**A267.** B. A long, thin extension that greatly increases surface area.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-10 -->

Explanation: The long extension increases the surface area to volume ratio, speeding up absorption. A thick lignified wall would be rigid and slow movement of water, and chloroplasts are of little use in roots that are underground.

**A268.** C. Hollow, dead cells with lignified walls and no end walls.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-11 -->

Explanation: At maturity, xylem vessels are dead hollow tubes with lignin-strengthened walls, and their end walls have broken down to form a continuous channel. The option describing living cells with nuclei does not match xylem vessels.

**A269.** D. A long axon, often insulated by a myelin sheath.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-12 -->

Explanation: A long axon covered in myelin allows fast conduction over long distances, as the impulse jumps between the gaps in the sheath. Animal neurons do not contain chloroplasts, and a freely permeable membrane could not maintain the ion gradients needed for an impulse.

**A270.** C. together direct the large amount of protein synthesis needed for contraction.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-13 -->

Explanation: Each nucleus controls a region of the cytoplasm, so many nuclei support the high rate of protein synthesis needed for contraction. Nuclei do not store energy, and the number of nuclei does not change how quickly gases diffuse.

**A271.** D. To increase surface area for absorption of digested nutrients.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-14 -->

Explanation: Microvilli greatly increase the surface area of the cell membrane, speeding up the absorption of digested food. Mucus is made by other cells, and the cells lining the intestine do not contract to move food along.

**A272.** A. opens, allowing carbon dioxide to enter the leaf.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-15 -->

Explanation: When guard cells take in water and become turgid, they bend apart and open the stoma, letting carbon dioxide in for photosynthesis. The stoma closes when guard cells lose water, so the option describing closure on turgidity has the direction reversed.
