**A273.** B. To sweep mucus and trapped particles up towards the throat.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-16 -->

Explanation: Cilia beat in a coordinated wave to move mucus, with trapped dust and microbes, up and away from the lungs. Surfactant is made by other cells in the alveoli, and cilia do not contract to move air.

**A274.** C. The stem cells are genetically matched to the patient, which reduces the risk of immune rejection.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-17 -->

Explanation: The nucleus comes from the patient's own cell, so the stem cells are a genetic match and are less likely to be rejected. The stem cells are pluripotent rather than totipotent, and the method does create an embryo, which is why it is ethically contested.

**A275.** D. They can differentiate into all the cell types of the body.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-18 -->

Explanation: iPSCs are pluripotent, like embryonic stem cells, so they can form all body cell types but not extra-embryonic tissue. Totipotency is a feature of very early embryonic cells, and iPSCs are not specialised.

**A276.** A. They produce ATP to power active transport of sugars into the sieve tubes.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-19 -->

Explanation: Companion cells respire aerobically to make ATP, which powers the active loading of sugars into the sieve tubes. Lignin is found in xylem rather than phloem, and storing water is not a role of companion cells.

**A277.** B. absorbing light energy for photosynthesis.
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-20 -->

Explanation: Palisade cells sit near the leaf surface and contain many chloroplasts, so they capture the most light for photosynthesis. Starch storage occurs in other tissues, water is carried by xylem rather than palisade cells, and gas exchange is needed for photosynthesis.

**A278.** Differentiation [1]
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-21 -->

Explanation:

**A279.** Collection is non-invasive, so the donor does not need surgery or experience pain [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-22 -->

Explanation:

**A280.** Division of labour means each cell type can be structurally and functionally adapted to carry out one role efficiently [1]; cells can be grouped into tissues and organs that perform complex tasks no single cell could carry out [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-23 -->

Explanation:

**A281.** Embryonic stem cells come from the early embryo, before cells have committed to a lineage, so they can form all body cell types (pluripotent) [1]; adult stem cells are found in mature tissues and have become restricted to forming the cell types of that tissue (multipotent) [1]; as development proceeds, the genes for other cell types are progressively switched off [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-24 -->

Explanation:

**A282.** Stem cells at the base of intestinal crypts divide by mitosis to produce new daughter cells [1]; some daughter cells differentiate into specialised cells, such as absorptive cells, by switching on particular genes [1]; older cells move up to the tip of the villus, where they are shed, so the lining is replaced about every few days [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-25 -->

Explanation:

**A283.** Cells in the tissue are undifferentiated, stem-like cells that can still divide and form new tissues [1]; hormones in the growth medium stimulate them to divide, forming an undifferentiated mass called a callus [1]; cells in the callus then differentiate into roots and shoots, producing genetically identical plants (clones) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-sc-26 -->

Explanation:

#### Plant transport systems

**A284.** A. water and dissolved minerals from roots to leaves.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-1 -->

Explanation: Xylem carries water and mineral ions upward from roots. Phloem carries sugars (mainly sucrose) in both directions.

**A285.** D. Transpiration pull, relying on cohesion and adhesion of water molecules
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-2 -->

Explanation: Evaporation from leaves through stomata creates tension that pulls a continuous column of water up, held together by cohesion (hydrogen bonds) and adhesion to xylem walls. Xylem vessels are dead and cannot actively transport.

**A286.** B. translocation.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-3 -->

Explanation: Translocation is the movement of sugars and other organic compounds in phloem from sources (e.g. leaves) to sinks (e.g. roots, fruits).

**A287.** D. removes water vapour near the leaf, increasing the concentration gradient for evaporation.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-4 -->

Explanation: Wind removes the humid layer around the leaf, steepening the water vapour gradient between the air spaces and the atmosphere, so transpiration and water uptake increase.

**A288.** B. They are living cells with sieve plates, supported by companion cells.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-5 -->

Explanation: Sieve tube elements are living but lack a nucleus; companion cells supply their metabolic needs. Lignified dead cells describe xylem vessels.

**A289.** Water enters root hair cells by osmosis because the cell sap has a lower water potential / higher solute concentration than the soil (minerals are actively transported in); water moves across the cortex via the cell walls and cytoplasm; at the endodermis the Casparian strip forces water through the cytoplasm before it enters the xylem.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-6 -->

Explanation:

**A290.** When water loss exceeds uptake, the plant closes its stomata (guard cells lose turgor) to conserve water; less water vapour can diffuse out of the leaf, so transpiration rate decreases.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-7 -->

Explanation:

**A291.** Xylem consists of dead, hollow, lignified vessels while phloem consists of living sieve tube elements and companion cells; xylem transports water and minerals while phloem transports sugars (sucrose) and other organic compounds; xylem flow is upward only (from roots) while phloem transports sugars in both directions from sources to sinks.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-8 -->

Explanation:

**A292.** A. water to enter the guard cells by osmosis, increasing turgor and bowing the cells apart.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-9 -->

Explanation: Active uptake of K⁺ lowers the water potential inside the guard cells, so water enters by osmosis, the cells become turgid and the pore opens. Water leaving the cells would make them flaccid and close the pore.

**A293.** B. root pressure pushes xylem sap out through hydathodes when transpiration is low and humidity is high.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-10 -->

Explanation: Root pressure builds when transpiration is low, such as on humid nights, and pushes xylem sap out through hydathodes. Guttation is not caused by high transpiration, and the liquid is xylem sap, not phloem sap.

**A294.** C. Apoplast pathway, through cell walls and intercellular spaces.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-11 -->

Explanation: The apoplast pathway moves water through non-living cell walls and spaces. The symplast pathway passes through cytoplasm via plasmodesmata, and the Casparian strip at the endodermis forces water into the symplast before it reaches the xylem.

**A295.** D. Warm, dry, windy and bright.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-12 -->

Explanation: Warmth, low humidity, wind and light all increase transpiration, because they speed evaporation, steepen the water vapour gradient and keep stomata open. Each of the other sets includes at least one factor that slows transpiration.

**A296.** A. active transport, with companion cells using ATP.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-13 -->

Explanation: Companion cells use ATP to pump sucrose into sieve tube elements against a concentration gradient. Xylem and the transpiration pull are not involved in loading phloem, and root pressure acts on xylem sap, not on phloem loading.

**A297.** B. loading sucrose at the source lowers water potential, water enters by osmosis and pressure rises; unloading at the sink lowers the pressure, so sap flows down the gradient.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-14 -->

Explanation: Water entering by osmosis at the source raises hydrostatic pressure, while unloading at the sink lowers it. This pressure gradient moves sap by mass flow. Transpiration drives the xylem, not the phloem, and sieve plates are not pumps.

**A298.** C. Pits in the lignified walls, where the wall is thin and unlignified.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-15 -->

Explanation: Pits are thin, unlignified areas of the vessel walls that let water pass sideways between vessels. Sieve plates belong to phloem, and xylem vessels have no living cytoplasm.

**A299.** D. lose water and become flaccid.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-16 -->

Explanation: Water moves by osmosis from higher to lower water potential, so the cell loses water, loses turgor and becomes flaccid. The cell would gain water only if the solution had a higher water potential than the cell sap.

**A300.** A. 1.5 mL h⁻¹
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-17 -->

Explanation: Rate = volume ÷ time = 4.5 mL ÷ 3 h = 1.5 mL h⁻¹. Adding the numbers gives 7.5, multiplying gives 13.5, and dividing time by volume gives about 0.67, so these are all wrong.

**A301.** B. A large surface area from many long, thin extensions.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-18 -->

Explanation: Root hairs are long, thin extensions of epidermal cells, which give a large surface area for absorbing water and ions. Lignified walls and a waxy cuticle would restrict uptake, and root cells generally lack chloroplasts.

**A302.** D. stomata, as water vapour.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-19 -->

Explanation: Stomata account for most transpiration because they are open to the air and are numerous. The cuticle is waterproof and loses little water, lenticels are minor in stems, and root hairs take in water rather than losing it.

**A303.** A. without light, guard cells stop actively taking in K⁺, so water leaves them and they lose turgor.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-20 -->

Explanation: Light drives ATP production in guard cells that powers K⁺ uptake. Without light, guard cells lose K⁺ and turgor, so the pore closes. Xylem transport does not simply stop at night, and leaf temperature usually falls.

**A304.** B. Soil → root hair → root cortex → endodermis → xylem → leaf mesophyll → air spaces → stomata → atmosphere.
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-21 -->

Explanation: Water enters root hairs, crosses the cortex and endodermis into the xylem, then moves through the leaf mesophyll and air spaces to leave through stomata. Phloem does not carry water to the leaves, and water does not enter the xylem before the root hairs.

**A305.** Transpiration is the loss of water vapour from the aerial parts of a plant [1]; most of it occurs through the stomata in the leaves [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-22 -->

Explanation:

**A306.** Xylem vessels lose their cytoplasm and nucleus at maturity, leaving hollow, lignified tubes [1]; with no living contents to obstruct flow, water moves continuously through the vessels as a column [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-23 -->

Explanation:

**A307.** Dry soil reduces the water available for uptake by the roots, so less water reaches the leaves [1]; stomata close to conserve water, reducing the entry of CO₂ into the leaf [1]; the lower CO₂ concentration in the leaf reduces the rate of photosynthesis [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-24 -->

Explanation:

**A308.** The waxy cuticle is waterproof, so it reduces evaporation from the leaf epidermis [1]; most water loss therefore occurs through the stomata [1]; if the cuticle is damaged, water loss through the epidermis increases and the rate of transpiration rises [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-25 -->

Explanation:

**A309.** Guard cells [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-26 -->

Explanation:

**A310.** Mineral ions are often at a lower concentration in the soil than in root cells, so they must be moved against a concentration gradient [1]; this requires ATP from cellular respiration, as the process is active transport [1]; water moves down its water potential gradient by osmosis, which requires no energy input [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-27 -->

Explanation:

**A311.** Advantage: the transpiration pull moves water and dissolved minerals up the xylem [1]; advantage: evaporation of water from the leaf cools the leaf [1]; disadvantage: water loss can cause wilting if uptake cannot keep pace [1]; disadvantage: stomata must be open for CO₂ uptake, so water is lost at the same time [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-28 -->

Explanation:

**A312.** Small or needle-shaped leaves reduce the surface area available for transpiration [1]; sunken stomata in pits trap humid air around the pores, reducing water loss [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-29 -->

Explanation:

**A313.** The cut shoot takes up water, so the movement of an air bubble along the capillary tube measures water uptake [1]; the distance moved by the bubble per unit time gives the rate [1]; assumption: water taken up equals water lost by transpiration, so water used in photosynthesis or growth is negligible [1].
<!-- id: bio12a1-pl-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Digestive system

**A314.** B. small intestine.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-1 -->

Explanation: The small intestine receives pancreatic enzymes and bile, and its villi absorb the products of digestion. The stomach begins protein digestion but absorbs very little, apart from substances such as alcohol.

**A315.** D. increase the surface area available for absorption.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-2 -->

Explanation: Villi and microvilli greatly increase the surface area of the intestinal lining, which increases the rate of absorption. Peristalsis is done by the smooth muscle layers, not the villi.

**A316.** A. Bile is produced by the liver, stored in the gallbladder and emulsifies lipids, increasing their surface area for lipase.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-3 -->

Explanation: The liver makes bile and the gallbladder only stores and concentrates it, so bile still flows after removal. Bile contains no enzymes; it emulsifies fats mechanically so lipase can act on a larger surface area.

**A317.** C. pepsin, secreted in the stomach.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-4 -->

Explanation: The stomach is highly acidic (about pH 1.5–3), and pepsin works best in these conditions. Amylase, lipase and maltase work in roughly neutral to slightly alkaline conditions.

**A318.** A. Absorbing water and some minerals and vitamins, and forming faeces
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-5 -->

Explanation: Undigested material entering the large intestine is mostly water and fibre. The colon reabsorbs water and electrolytes, gut bacteria produce some vitamins (such as vitamin K), and faeces are formed. Amino acid absorption happens in the small intestine.

**A319.** Mechanical digestion physically breaks food into smaller pieces without changing its chemical make-up, e.g. chewing by teeth or churning in the stomach; chemical digestion uses enzymes to break the bonds in large molecules to form smaller molecules, e.g. amylase hydrolysing starch to maltose or pepsin breaking proteins into polypeptides.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-6 -->

Explanation:

**A320.** It is very long and folded, with villi and microvilli, giving a very large surface area for absorption; the epithelium of each villus is only one cell thick, so the diffusion distance into the blood is short; each villus has a dense capillary network (and a lacteal for lipids) that carries absorbed nutrients away, keeping a steep concentration gradient.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-7 -->

Explanation:

**A321.** Flattened villi greatly reduce the surface area of the small intestine; so the rate of absorption of nutrients such as glucose, amino acids, iron and vitamins decreases; the person may show malnutrition, weight loss, fatigue or anaemia even though they eat enough (undigested or unabsorbed material may also cause diarrhoea).
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-8 -->

Explanation:

**A322.** C. It begins starch digestion in the mouth, breaking starch into smaller sugars such as maltose.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-9 -->

Explanation: Salivary amylase is secreted by the salivary glands and hydrolyses starch into smaller sugars, mainly maltose, while food is in the mouth. It does not produce glucose from starch; that final step is done by maltase in the small intestine.

**A323.** A. To neutralise acidic chyme so that pancreatic enzymes can work at their optimum pH.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-10 -->

Explanation: Chyme leaving the stomach is acidic, and the hydrogencarbonate raises the pH of the duodenum to about 7–8, which suits pancreatic amylase, lipase and trypsin. Emulsification is done by bile, and hydrochloric acid is made in the stomach rather than the pancreas.

**A324.** B. It protects the stomach wall from being damaged by hydrochloric acid and pepsin.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-11 -->

Explanation: The alkaline mucus forms a barrier between the stomach wall and its acidic, enzyme-rich contents, so the wall is not digested. Mucus does not digest or absorb food, and emulsifying fats is a function of bile.

**A325.** B. It denatures food proteins, kills many ingested microorganisms and converts pepsinogen to pepsin.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-12 -->

Explanation: Hydrochloric acid denatures food proteins, kills many microbes in ingested food and activates pepsinogen, creating the low pH that pepsin needs. Bile emulsifies fats rather than being neutralised by acid, and starch is hydrolysed by amylase, not by acid.

**A326.** A. Lacteals, which drain into the lymphatic system before joining the blood.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-13 -->

Explanation: Inside the villus cell the fatty acids and monoglycerides are re-formed into triglycerides and packaged into chylomicrons, which are too large to enter blood capillaries and so enter a lacteal. The hepatic portal vein carries water-soluble products such as glucose and amino acids, not most lipids.

**A327.** C. It carries nutrients absorbed by the small intestine, such as glucose and amino acids, to the liver.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-14 -->

Explanation: Blood from the capillaries of the villi drains into the hepatic portal vein, which delivers absorbed glucose and amino acids to the liver for processing and storage. Oxygenated blood reaches the intestine from the aorta, bile travels through bile ducts, and lipids enter the lymphatic system via lacteals rather than the portal vein.

**A328.** B. The active site of maltase fits maltose, but not the much larger starch molecule.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-15 -->

Explanation: Enzymes are specific: the active site has a shape complementary to its substrate, so maltase binds maltose but not starch. Starch is a polymer of glucose, so the claim that it has no glucose units is wrong, and denaturation by the substrate is not the cause.

**A329.** D. Lipase: triglycerides → fatty acids and glycerol.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-16 -->

Explanation: Lipase hydrolyses triglycerides into fatty acids and glycerol. Amylase acts on starch to form smaller sugars, pepsin acts on proteins to form polypeptides, and no enzyme in this list makes amino acids from triglycerides or glucose from proteins.

**A330.** A. plant material is harder to digest, so a longer gut gives more time for digestion and absorption.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-17 -->

Explanation: Plant cell walls contain cellulose, which most animals cannot digest, so herbivores need a longer gut to digest and absorb the nutrients that are available. Meat is richer in protein than plant material, and the length of the gut is not set by pepsin or by water absorption.

**A331.** C. Incisors cut food; molars grind food.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-18 -->

Explanation: Incisors have a sharp, chisel-shaped edge for cutting, while the broad, flat surfaces of molars grind food into smaller pieces. Canines tear food, and molars are not adapted for cutting. This is mechanical digestion, which increases the surface area available to enzymes.

**A332.** B. It controls the movement of chyme from the stomach into the duodenum.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-19 -->

Explanation: The pyloric sphincter is a ring of smooth muscle at the base of the stomach that regulates the release of chyme into the duodenum. The lower oesophageal sphincter prevents reflux into the oesophagus, and bile is stored in the gallbladder.

**A333.** D. not be digested, because the enzyme's active site has been permanently changed by heat.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-20 -->

Explanation: Temperatures well above the optimum for human enzymes break the weak bonds that hold the enzyme's shape, so the active site is permanently altered. Cooling does not restore the shape, so the enzyme cannot digest starch.

**A334.** A. Glycogen.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-21 -->

Explanation: Glycogen is a branched polymer of glucose that humans store in the liver and muscles, and it is broken down to release glucose when blood sugar falls. Starch is the storage polysaccharide of plants, cellulose is structural in plant cell walls, and maltose is a disaccharide rather than a storage polymer.

**A335.** D. Amino acids.
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-22 -->

Explanation: Pepsin, trypsin and peptidases hydrolyse proteins into amino acids, which are absorbed by the villi of the small intestine. Glucose and maltose come from carbohydrates, and fatty acids and glycerol come from lipids.

**A336.** Rhythmic, wave-like contractions of the smooth muscle in the wall of the alimentary canal that push food along the gut. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-23 -->

Explanation:

**A337.** Pancreatic lipase, trypsin and other pancreatic enzymes cannot reach the duodenum, so lipids and proteins are not hydrolysed into smaller molecules that can be absorbed [1]; undigested fats and proteins pass into the large intestine, causing malabsorption and possibly fatty faeces [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-24 -->

Explanation:

**A338.** Contraction of the stomach's smooth muscle in several directions churns the food and mixes it with gastric juice [1]; this breaks the food into smaller pieces, increasing the surface area available for enzymes such as pepsin to act on [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-25 -->

Explanation:

**A339.** Early samples turn blue-black, showing that starch is still present [1]; later samples stay brown-orange, the colour of iodine alone, showing that no starch remains [1]; amylase has hydrolysed the starch into smaller sugars such as maltose, which do not give a blue-black colour with iodine [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-26 -->

Explanation:

**A340.** The tongue pushes the bolus back into the pharynx and the swallowing reflex begins [1]; the epiglottis closes over the trachea so the bolus enters the oesophagus [1]; peristalsis moves the bolus down the oesophagus and the lower oesophageal sphincter relaxes to let it into the stomach [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-27 -->

Explanation:

**A341.** Stomach: hydrochloric acid activates pepsinogen to pepsin [1]; pepsin hydrolyses proteins into polypeptides [1]; small intestine: trypsin from the pancreas hydrolyses polypeptides into smaller peptides [1]; peptidases on the microvilli hydrolyse these into amino acids, which are absorbed [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-28 -->

Explanation:

**A342.** Rate = 6.0 g ÷ 4.0 min = 1.5 g min⁻¹ [1]; increase the concentration of amylase in the mixture [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-29 -->

Explanation:

**A343.** Active trypsin would digest the pancreas's own tissue, causing self-digestion [1]; trypsinogen is converted to active trypsin only in the duodenum, by the enzyme enterokinase, so proteins are digested in the correct place [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-dig-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Endocrine and excretory systems

**A344.** B. being carried in the bloodstream and binding to specific receptors on or in target cells.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-1 -->

Explanation: Endocrine glands are ductless and secrete hormones into the blood. Only cells with the matching receptor respond. Ducts are a feature of exocrine glands.

**A345.** C. nephron.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-2 -->

Explanation: Each kidney contains about a million nephrons, each of which filters blood and modifies the filtrate into urine. The ureter only carries urine to the bladder.

**A346.** D. Plasma proteins
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-3 -->

Explanation: Filtration at the glomerulus is based on size. Water, glucose, urea and ions pass into the capsule, but large plasma proteins (and blood cells) are too large to pass through the filtration barrier.

**A347.** A. completely reabsorbed into the blood, mainly in the proximal convoluted tubule.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-4 -->

Explanation: All filtered glucose is normally reabsorbed by active transport in the proximal convoluted tubule. Glucose appears in urine only when blood glucose is so high that the carriers are saturated, as in untreated diabetes mellitus.

**A348.** B. secretes hormones into the blood and digestive enzymes through a duct.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-5 -->

Explanation: The islets of Langerhans secrete insulin and glucagon into the blood (endocrine), while other pancreatic cells release digestive enzymes into the duodenum through the pancreatic duct (exocrine). Bile and glycogen storage are liver functions.

**A349.** Filtration: blood pressure forces water and small solutes (glucose, salts, urea) out of the glomerulus into the Bowman's capsule; reabsorption: useful substances such as glucose, amino acids, many ions and much of the water move from the tubule back into the blood; secretion: extra wastes or ions such as H⁺, K⁺ and some drugs are actively moved from the blood into the tubule.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-6 -->

Explanation:

**A350.** A hormone can only act on cells that have receptors complementary in shape to that hormone; only these target cells bind the hormone and start a response, while other cells lack the receptor and are not affected.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-7 -->

Explanation:

**A351.** The anterior pituitary secretes tropic hormones such as TSH, ACTH, FSH and LH; these stimulate other endocrine glands (thyroid, adrenal cortex, gonads) to release their own hormones, so it controls many glands; however, the pituitary is itself controlled by releasing hormones from the hypothalamus (e.g. TRH), which links the nervous system to the endocrine system.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-8 -->

Explanation:

**A352.** C. beta cells
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-9 -->

Explanation: Beta cells of the islets of Langerhans secrete insulin into the blood. Alpha cells secrete glucagon, while acinar cells are exocrine cells that release digestive enzymes through the pancreatic duct.

**A353.** A. thyroxine
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-10 -->

Explanation: Thyroxine is made by the thyroid gland from iodine and tyrosine. Insulin and antidiuretic hormone are peptides, and adrenaline is derived from tyrosine without iodine, so none of these depends on iodine.

**A354.** D. High blood glucose stimulates insulin release; insulin lowers blood glucose, which then reduces insulin release.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-11 -->

Explanation: Insulin lowers blood glucose by promoting glucose uptake and storage, and the falling glucose removes the stimulus for further insulin release. Glucagon and adrenaline raise blood glucose, so they are not released in response to high glucose, and an insulin-driven rise in glucose would reinforce the problem rather than correct it.

**A355.** B. ADH secretion increases, so the collecting ducts become more permeable to water and more concentrated urine is produced.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-12 -->

Explanation: Dehydration raises the solute concentration of the blood, which increases ADH release from the posterior pituitary. ADH makes the collecting ducts more permeable to water, so less water is lost and the urine is concentrated. The other options reverse either the direction of the ADH change or its effect on urine.

**A356.** A. Collecting duct
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-13 -->

Explanation: ADH makes the cells of the collecting duct more permeable to water, so more water is reabsorbed. Bowman's capsule and the glomerulus are sites of filtration, and the ureter only transports urine to the bladder.

**A357.** D. To create a concentration gradient in the medulla so that water can be reabsorbed in the collecting duct.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-14 -->

Explanation: The ascending limb of the loop of Henle actively pumps out salts, so the medulla becomes concentrated and water can leave the collecting duct by osmosis when ADH is present. Filtration occurs at the glomerulus, urea is made in the liver, and glucose is reabsorbed mainly in the proximal tubule.

**A358.** B. ammonia produced when excess amino acids are broken down
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-15 -->

Explanation: Excess amino acids are deaminated, producing toxic ammonia, which the liver converts into urea. Glucose, glycogen and cholesterol contain no nitrogen, so they cannot be a source of nitrogenous waste.

**A359.** A. To hold a lower concentration of waste substances than the blood, so wastes diffuse out of the blood.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-16 -->

Explanation: Haemodialysis relies on diffusion across a semi-permeable membrane down a concentration gradient, so wastes such as urea move from the blood into the fluid. The other options describe adding wastes, replacing cells, or pumping blood, none of which removes wastes by diffusion.

**A360.** D. The beta cells of the islets are destroyed, usually by the immune system, so little or no insulin is made.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-17 -->

Explanation: Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune destruction of beta cells, so the body makes little or no insulin and must be treated with injections. Type 2 diabetes is mainly a problem of cells responding poorly to insulin, and glucose spills into urine because blood glucose is too high rather than because a tubule is blocked.

**A361.** B. Increased heart rate and dilation of the bronchioles.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-18 -->

Explanation: Adrenaline prepares the body for fight or flight by increasing heart rate, dilating the bronchioles and stimulating glycogen breakdown to release glucose. It also redirects blood towards skeletal muscle, so blood flow to the digestive system falls rather than rises.

**A362.** C. adrenal cortex.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-19 -->

Explanation: Aldosterone is a steroid hormone made by the outer part of the adrenal gland, the adrenal cortex. The adrenal medulla secretes adrenaline, the posterior pituitary releases ADH, and the thyroid makes thyroxine.

**A363.** D. Release of carbon dioxide from the lungs.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-20 -->

Explanation: Excretion is the removal of metabolic wastes produced by cells, such as carbon dioxide. Undigested fibre is egestion because it was never made by or absorbed into body cells, while enzyme secretion and glucose absorption are part of digestion.

**A364.** A. Proximal convoluted tubule, where about two-thirds of the filtered water is reabsorbed.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-21 -->

Explanation: The proximal convoluted tubule reabsorbs about two-thirds of the filtered water, along with nearly all the glucose and amino acids. Bowman's capsule is the site of filtration, and the ureter and urethra carry urine without reabsorbing water.

**A365.** C. They are lipid-soluble, cross the cell membrane and bind receptors inside the target cell.
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-22 -->

Explanation: Steroid hormones are derived from cholesterol, which makes them lipid-soluble, so they diffuse through the cell membrane and bind intracellular receptors. Water-soluble hormones such as insulin cannot cross the membrane and instead bind to receptors on the cell surface.

**A366.** The posterior pituitary stores and releases ADH and oxytocin, which are made in the hypothalamus [1]; the anterior pituitary makes and secretes its own tropic hormones, whose release is controlled by releasing hormones from the hypothalamus [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-23 -->

Explanation:

**A367.** Falling thyroxine removes inhibition of the hypothalamus, which releases TRH [1]; TRH stimulates the anterior pituitary to secrete TSH [1]; TSH stimulates the thyroid to secrete more thyroxine, which returns to normal and then inhibits TRH and TSH release [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-24 -->

Explanation:

**A368.** Insulin is a protein, so it would be digested by enzymes in the stomach and small intestine and lose its function [1]; injection delivers intact insulin directly into the blood [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-25 -->

Explanation:

**A369.** Lungs remove carbon dioxide (and water vapour) [1]; skin removes water, salts and urea in sweat [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-26 -->

Explanation:

**A370.** Urea is normally filtered at the glomerulus and removed from the body in urine [1]; when kidney function fails, urea is not filtered out or excreted, so it builds up in the blood [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-27 -->

Explanation:

**A371.** Glycogenesis [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-28 -->

Explanation:

**A372.** Drinking a large volume lowers the solute concentration of the blood [1]; less ADH is released from the posterior pituitary [1]; the collecting ducts become less permeable to water, so less water is reabsorbed and dilute urine is produced [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-29 -->

Explanation:

**A373.** Alpha cells of the islets of Langerhans respond to the fall by secreting glucagon into the blood [1]; glucagon binds to receptors on liver cells and stimulates the breakdown of glycogen (glycogenolysis) [1]; glucose is released into the blood, raising blood glucose towards normal [1]; the rising glucose reduces glucagon secretion, which is negative feedback [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-30 -->

Explanation:

**A374.** Microvilli on the cell surface increase surface area for reabsorption of glucose, amino acids and ions [1]; many mitochondria supply the ATP needed for active transport [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-endex-31 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 1 AOS 3: Homeostasis

#### Stimulus-response model and negative feedback

**A375.** C. Stimulus → receptor → modulator → effector → response
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-1 -->

Explanation: A change (stimulus) is detected by a receptor, which sends a message to a modulator (control centre). The modulator signals an effector, which produces the response.

**A376.** B. the response counteracts the original change, returning the variable towards its set point.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-2 -->

Explanation: In negative feedback the response reverses the change that triggered it, keeping the variable within a narrow range. A response that amplifies the change is positive feedback.

**A377.** A. modulator.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-3 -->

Explanation: The medulla receives information from the receptors and coordinates a response, so it is the modulator (control centre). The heart is the effector and the rise in blood pressure is the stimulus.

**A378.** D. Release of oxytocin during childbirth, which causes stronger contractions that trigger more oxytocin release
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-4 -->

Explanation: During labour, contractions push the baby against the cervix, stimulating more oxytocin release and even stronger contractions. The change is amplified until the baby is born. The other three responses reverse the change, so they are negative feedback.

**A379.** B. homeostasis keeps a variable within a narrow tolerance range rather than at one fixed value.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-5 -->

Explanation: Homeostatic variables fluctuate around a set point. Small changes are detected and corrected, so the variable stays within a narrow range. A range of less than 1 °C over a day is normal.

**A380.** In negative feedback, the response reduces or reverses the original stimulus, returning the variable towards its set point and maintaining homeostasis; in positive feedback, the response increases or amplifies the original stimulus, moving the variable further from its starting value until an end point is reached (e.g. childbirth, blood clotting).
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-6 -->

Explanation:

**A381.** Stimulus: high temperature of the pan on the skin; receptor: thermoreceptors/pain receptors in the skin of the hand; modulator: the spinal cord (a reflex arc through interneurons); effector: skeletal muscles of the arm (e.g. biceps); response: muscle contraction that moves the hand away from the pan. (3 marks for all five correct, 2 for four, 1 for two or three.)
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-7 -->

Explanation:

**A382.** Cell metabolism depends on enzymes, which have an optimum temperature and pH; large changes alter the shape of the active site or denature the enzyme (or slow reactions a lot if too cold), so metabolic reactions could not continue at the rates cells need.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-8 -->

Explanation:

**A383.** B. set point.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-9 -->

Explanation: The set point is the reference value that the variable is kept close to. A stimulus is the change that is detected, and the modulator is the control centre that processes information; neither is the reference value.

**A384.** A. a structure that detects a change in the internal or external environment and sends information to the modulator.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-10 -->

Explanation: A receptor detects the stimulus and passes information on to the modulator. The effector is the structure that produces the response, the stimulus is the change itself, and the modulator is the control centre.

**A385.** D. effector.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-11 -->

Explanation: Sweat glands carry out the response by producing sweat that cools the body, so they are the effector. The rise in body temperature is the stimulus and the thermoreceptors are the receptors, so neither describes the glands.

**A386.** C. It is negative feedback, because the response lowers blood glucose back towards the set point.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-12 -->

Explanation: Insulin acts to reverse the rise in blood glucose, which is the defining feature of negative feedback. Feedback loops can involve hormones as well as nerve impulses, so the hormonal route does not make it 'not feedback'.

**A387.** B. It is positive feedback, because each clotting factor activates more clotting factors, amplifying the response until a clot forms.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-13 -->

Explanation: In clotting, each step activates further steps, so the response is amplified until a clot seals the wound, which is the defining feature of positive feedback. Describing clotting as negative feedback is a common mistake, because the clot seals the wound rather than reversing the cut.

**A388.** C. 7.31
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-14 -->

Explanation: A pH of 7.31 is below the lower limit of 7.35, so it is outside the normal range. The value 7.36 is only just above the lower limit, so it is still within the range.

**A389.** A. The collecting ducts reabsorb more water, so the blood becomes more dilute.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-15 -->

Explanation: ADH increases the water reabsorbed by the collecting ducts, which dilutes the blood and reverses the original rise in concentration. Reabsorbing less water would make the blood more concentrated, so that option has the wrong direction.

**A390.** B. Negative feedback keeps the variable close to its set point, so conditions stay within the limits cells need.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-16 -->

Explanation: Negative feedback reverses changes, so variables such as temperature and pH stay within the narrow ranges that enzymes and cells need. Positive feedback drives a change further and would move a variable away from its set point.

**A391.** D. fall in blood glucose concentration.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-17 -->

Explanation: The stimulus is the change in the internal environment that is detected, here the fall in blood glucose. Glucagon release and glucose release from the liver are later steps in the response.

**A392.** A. Shivering, the rapid contraction of skeletal muscles that generates heat.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-18 -->

Explanation: Shivering is an action carried out by an effector, so it is a response. The other three options are changes in the body or environment that receptors detect, which makes them stimuli.

**A393.** B. Negative feedback, because the response still acts to reduce the deviation from the set point.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-19 -->

Explanation: The response still opposes the change, so it is negative feedback. Overshoot happens because the response takes time to act, and it does not mean the response is amplifying the original change.

**A394.** C. To rapidly drive a process to completion, such as childbirth or blood clotting.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-20 -->

Explanation: Positive feedback amplifies a change until an end point is reached, which suits processes that must finish quickly. Keeping a variable near its set point is the role of negative feedback.

**A395.** D. The skeletal muscles, which contract to shiver and produce heat.
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-21 -->

Explanation: Skeletal muscles carry out the response by contracting and producing heat, so they are the effector. The thermoreceptors are the receptors, the hypothalamus is the modulator, and the fall in temperature is the stimulus.

**A396.** A change in the internal or external environment that is detected by a receptor. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-22 -->

Explanation:

**A397.** The modulator (control centre) receives information from the receptor and coordinates the response by signalling the effector. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-23 -->

Explanation:

**A398.** The narrow band of values, either side of the set point, within which a homeostatic variable is kept. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-24 -->

Explanation:

**A399.** The stimulus is a rise in body temperature, which is detected and triggers sweating. [1] Evaporation of sweat cools the body, reversing the original rise and returning temperature towards the set point. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-25 -->

Explanation:

**A400.** Stimulus: a rise in blood carbon dioxide concentration (fall in pH), detected by chemoreceptors. [1] Response: increased rate and depth of breathing, produced by the diaphragm and intercostal muscles, expels carbon dioxide and returns blood pH towards its set point. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-26 -->

Explanation:

**A401.** The modulator determines whether the variable has deviated from the set point. [1] This allows an appropriately sized and directed response to be sent to the effector to reverse the change. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-27 -->

Explanation:

**A402.** The response must reverse the stimulus quickly and strongly enough to counteract it. [1] If it is too slow or too weak, the variable keeps changing and can move outside the tolerance range, which may damage cells or disrupt function. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-28 -->

Explanation:

**A403.** Receptor: osmoreceptors that detect the increased solute concentration of the blood. [1] Modulator: the hypothalamus, which signals the posterior pituitary to release ADH into the blood. [1] Effector: the kidney collecting ducts, which reabsorb more water, diluting the blood and reversing the change. [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-srm-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Thermoregulation

**A404.** C. hypothalamus.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-1 -->

Explanation: The hypothalamus receives information from central and peripheral thermoreceptors and coordinates responses. The thyroid gland is an effector gland that it controls indirectly through the pituitary.

**A405.** D. Vasoconstriction of arterioles supplying the skin
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-2 -->

Explanation: Vasoconstriction lowers blood flow near the skin surface, so less heat is lost by radiation and conduction. Vasodilation and sweating increase heat loss, and less thyroxine would lower heat production.

**A406.** A. skeletal muscles, which contract rapidly and involuntarily.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-3 -->

Explanation: Shivering is rapid, involuntary contraction of skeletal muscles. The extra cellular respiration releases heat. Thermoreceptors are receptors, not effectors.

**A407.** B. less sweat evaporates, so less heat is removed from the skin.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-4 -->

Explanation: Sweat cools the body only when it evaporates, because evaporation takes heat energy from the skin. When the air is already humid, evaporation is slow, so sweat drips off without much cooling.

**A408.** C. behavioural thermoregulation by an ectotherm.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-5 -->

Explanation: Bearded dragons are ectotherms; they get most body heat from the environment. Moving between sun and shade is a behaviour that adjusts how much heat they gain.

**A409.** The hypothalamus releases TRH, which stimulates the anterior pituitary to release TSH; TSH stimulates the thyroid to secrete thyroxine, which increases the metabolic (cellular respiration) rate of body cells, releasing more heat.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-6 -->

Explanation:

**A410.** Exercise raises core temperature, which is detected and leads to vasodilation of arterioles supplying the skin; more warm blood flows close to the skin surface, making it look red and increasing heat loss by radiation.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-7 -->

Explanation:

**A411.** Stimulus: fall in skin and core temperature, detected by thermoreceptors in the skin and in the hypothalamus; the hypothalamus acts as modulator and sends nerve impulses to effectors; e.g. skeletal muscles shiver to produce heat, and smooth muscle in skin arterioles contracts (vasoconstriction) to reduce heat loss (also accept piloerection or a behavioural response such as putting on clothing or seeking shelter); this is negative feedback because the responses counteract the fall in temperature.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-8 -->

Explanation:

**A412.** A. They are located in the skin and detect changes in external temperature.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-9 -->

Explanation: Peripheral thermoreceptors in the skin respond to temperature at the body surface and send impulses to the hypothalamus. The option describing receptors in the hypothalamus describes central thermoreceptors, and the other two options describe effectors, not receptors.

**A413.** B. Thermoreceptors detect the rise, the hypothalamus receives the impulses, sweat glands secrete sweat, and evaporation removes heat from the skin.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-10 -->

Explanation: Thermoreceptors are the receptors, the hypothalamus is the control centre, and the sweat glands are the effectors, so the correct order runs from receptor to control centre to effector. The option in which the hypothalamus secretes sweat confuses the control centre with an effector, and the last option describes a heat-producing response that would not cool the runner.

**A414.** C. have a large surface area with many blood vessels close to the skin, so heat is lost by radiation and convection.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-11 -->

Explanation: Heat leaves the body across the surface of the ears, so a large, thin ear with blood vessels close to the skin increases heat loss in hot conditions. Trapping warm air describes insulating fur rather than thin ears, and absorbing sunlight would add heat.

**A415.** D. A behavioural response that reduces heat loss.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-12 -->

Explanation: Huddling is an action the animal performs, so it is behavioural, and it reduces the exposed surface area through which heat is lost. Vasoconstriction, shivering and thyroxine secretion are physiological responses that occur inside the body, so the other options describe the wrong type of response.

**A416.** A. They rely mainly on heat from the external environment and on behaviour to regulate their body temperature.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-13 -->

Explanation: Ectotherms gain most of their body heat from the environment and use behaviours such as basking or sheltering to adjust it. Metabolic heat production alone describes endotherms, and ectotherms' body temperatures generally follow their surroundings, so the other options are incorrect.

**A417.** B. A mouse has a larger surface area to volume ratio, so more heat is lost through its surface for each gram of body tissue.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-14 -->

Explanation: Heat is produced throughout the body's volume but lost through its surface, and small animals have a larger surface area relative to volume. The option about a lower metabolic rate is wrong because small endotherms have a higher metabolic rate per gram than large ones.

**A418.** C. Water conducts heat away from the skin much more rapidly than air does.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-15 -->

Explanation: Water has a much higher thermal conductivity than air, so heat moves from the skin into the surrounding water far more quickly by conduction. A submerged person cannot lose heat by evaporation, so the option about evaporation is wrong.

**A419.** D. Vasoconstriction and shivering increase heat production and reduce heat loss.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-16 -->

Explanation: Relative to the raised set point, the body now detects its temperature as too low, so it conserves heat and produces more of it until the core temperature reaches the new set point. Sweating and vasodilation are responses to a temperature above the set point, so the first two options are wrong.

**A420.** B. Less water is available to be secreted as sweat, so less sweat evaporates and less heat is removed.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-17 -->

Explanation: Sweat is formed mostly from water in the blood plasma, so a lower water volume means less sweat is available to evaporate and less heat is removed. A dehydrated person's hypothalamus still detects temperature changes, so the first option is wrong.

**A421.** C. The responses counteract the change, moving the core temperature back towards the set point.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-18 -->

Explanation: In negative feedback, the output of the response opposes the original change, which keeps the core temperature close to the set point. The first option describes positive feedback, which does not normally control body temperature.

**A422.** A. 37 °C
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-19 -->

Explanation: Core temperature is regulated at around 37 °C in healthy adults. A temperature of 39 °C is a fever in many people, and 42 °C is dangerously high, while 35 °C is below normal.

**A423.** B. trapped air is a poor conductor, so heat loss by conduction and convection is reduced.
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-20 -->

Explanation: Still air trapped among the fur is a poor conductor, so it slows heat moving away from the skin by conduction and reduces convection. Fur does not cause evaporation or increase blood flow, and the hair itself does not generate heat.

**A424.** As the lizard's body temperature falls, enzyme-controlled reactions, including cellular respiration, slow down [1]; less energy is released and nerve impulses and muscle contraction slow, so the lizard moves sluggishly [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-21 -->

Explanation:

**A425.** High temperature denatures enzymes and other proteins, so their shape and function are lost [1]; metabolic reactions, including cellular respiration, cannot proceed normally, damaging cells and organs [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-22 -->

Explanation:

**A426.** Fat has a low thermal conductivity, so heat conducts slowly from the warm core through the fat towards the surrounding water [1]; fat tissue has relatively few blood vessels, so less blood flows to the outer layer and less heat is carried to the skin surface [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-23 -->

Explanation:

**A427.** Surface area = 6 × 2² = 24 cm² [1]; volume = 2³ = 8 cm³, so the ratio is 24 : 8 = 3 : 1 (3 cm⁻¹) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-24 -->

Explanation:

**A428.** Water evaporates from the moist surfaces of the tongue and respiratory tract [1]; the evaporation removes heat energy from the blood and body, cooling the dog [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-25 -->

Explanation:

**A429.** Light-coloured fur or skin reflects solar radiation [1]; nocturnal activity avoids the hottest part of the day [1]; burrowing into cooler soil or shade reduces heat gained from the environment [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-thermo-26 -->

Explanation:

#### Blood glucose regulation

**A430.** B. beta cells of the islets of Langerhans in the pancreas.
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-1 -->

Explanation: Beta cells in the pancreatic islets secrete insulin. Alpha cells secrete glucagon. The adrenal medulla secretes adrenaline.

**A431.** C. stimulate glycogenolysis, breaking glycogen down into glucose that enters the blood.
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-2 -->

Explanation: Glucagon makes liver cells break down stored glycogen and release glucose into the blood, raising the blood glucose level. Glycogenesis is promoted by insulin.

**A432.** A. Insulin secretion increases
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-3 -->

Explanation: Rising blood glucose is detected by beta cells, which secrete more insulin. Insulin increases glucose uptake into cells and glycogen storage, which explains the return to normal. Glucagon secretion would decrease.

**A433.** D. Stimulating the breakdown of glycogen into glucose
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-4 -->

Explanation: Breaking down glycogen raises blood glucose and is stimulated by glucagon (and adrenaline). Insulin has the opposite effect, promoting glucose uptake and storage.

**A434.** A. be broken down by proteases in the stomach and small intestine before it reached the blood.
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-5 -->

Explanation: Proteases such as pepsin and trypsin would digest the insulin into amino acids, so active hormone would not reach the blood. Insulin is hydrophilic and is too large to diffuse through membranes.

**A435.** The fall in blood glucose is detected by alpha cells (and beta cells) in the islets of Langerhans; alpha cells secrete more glucagon and beta cells secrete less insulin; glucagon causes liver cells to break glycogen down into glucose (glycogenolysis), which is released into the blood, so blood glucose rises back towards the set point and the stimulus is removed.
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A436.** When blood glucose is high, insulin causes liver cells to take up glucose and store it as glycogen (glycogenesis); when blood glucose is low, glucagon causes liver cells to break glycogen down and release glucose into the blood (glycogenolysis), so the liver acts as a glucose store that buffers changes.
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A437.** Adrenaline stimulates the breakdown of glycogen in the liver (and muscles), releasing glucose; the extra glucose supplies muscle and brain cells with more fuel for cellular respiration, giving more ATP for a fight-or-flight response.
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A438.** A. A narrow range, around 4–6 mmol L⁻¹ when fasting, that the body works to keep blood glucose within
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-9 -->

Explanation: The set point is a narrow target range that negative feedback works to maintain, not a single unchanging value. Blood glucose rises after a meal and falls during fasting, while the renal threshold and the point where insulin secretion stops are different quantities.

**A439.** B. Each hormone binds to its own specific receptor on target cells, and the cell's response depends on which hormone binds
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-10 -->

Explanation: Each hormone acts only on target cells carrying its specific receptor, so the hormone that binds determines the response. Both hormones are peptides that cannot cross the plasma membrane, and they are secreted by different islet cells (beta and alpha cells), so the other options are wrong.

**A440.** C. Blood glucose rises higher and stays elevated for longer, because cells take up and store less glucose
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-11 -->

Explanation: Without beta cells, little or no insulin is secreted, so cells take up less glucose and the liver stores less glycogen, and blood glucose stays high after a meal. Insulin is needed after meals to lower blood glucose, and the liver does not make insulin.

**A441.** D. Target cells respond less strongly than normal to a given concentration of insulin
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-12 -->

Explanation: In insulin resistance the pancreas usually still secretes insulin, but target cells such as muscle and fat cells respond poorly to it. Complete loss of insulin production describes type 1 diabetes, and excess glucagon would raise blood glucose rather than describe resistance.

**A442.** A. The brain relies almost entirely on glucose from the blood for cellular respiration and has very little stored energy
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-13 -->

Explanation: Brain cells depend on a continuous supply of blood glucose because they store only small amounts of glycogen. Insulin does not accumulate in brain cells, and the brain does not make glucose from amino acids.

**A443.** B. Facilitated diffusion through glucose transporter proteins; insulin increases the number of transporters in the membrane
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-14 -->

Explanation: Glucose moves down its concentration gradient through transporter proteins, which is facilitated diffusion, and insulin increases the number of these transporters in the membrane. Osmosis describes water movement, and simple diffusion through the lipid bilayer does not suit a polar molecule like glucose.

**A444.** C. Glucagon secretion increases and insulin secretion decreases, both of which help raise blood glucose
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-15 -->

Explanation: A falling blood glucose is detected by alpha cells, which secrete more glucagon, while beta cells reduce insulin secretion. The other options reverse these responses or ignore the fall altogether.

**A445.** A. hyperglycaemia
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-16 -->

Explanation: Hyperglycaemia is blood glucose above the normal range, which 16 mmol L⁻¹ clearly is. Hypoglycaemia is below normal, normoglycaemia is within the normal range, and glycolysis is a metabolic pathway rather than a condition.

**A446.** B. lipogenesis
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-17 -->

Explanation: Lipogenesis is the synthesis of fat for storage, whereas lipolysis breaks stored fat down. Glycogenolysis breaks glycogen into glucose, and proteolysis breaks proteins into amino acids.

**A447.** C. Hormones are carried in the blood to target cells throughout the body, giving slower but longer-lasting responses
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-18 -->

Explanation: Hormones are transported in the blood and can reach many target cells, producing slower but more sustained responses than nerve impulses. The other options describe nerve signals or misstate how hormones act.

**A448.** B. 50%
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-19 -->

Explanation: The decrease is 9.0 − 4.5 = 4.5 mmol L⁻¹, and 4.5 ÷ 9.0 × 100 = 50%. Dividing the change by the new value gives 100%, dividing the original value by the new value gives 200%, and 25% would require a change of only 2.25 mmol L⁻¹.

**A449.** D. A specific receptor protein on the outer surface of the target cell membrane
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-20 -->

Explanation: Because insulin cannot enter the cell, it binds to a receptor protein on the outer surface of the plasma membrane and triggers a response inside the cell. Insulin does not bind glucose, and it does not enter the nucleus or cytoplasm.

**A450.** C. The hepatic portal vein
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-21 -->

Explanation: Blood from the small intestine drains into the hepatic portal vein, which delivers absorbed glucose to the liver before it reaches the rest of the body. The hepatic vein carries blood away from the liver, the aorta carries blood away from the heart, and the renal artery supplies the kidneys.

**A451.** Glycogenesis is the conversion of glucose into glycogen for storage [1]; glycogenolysis is the breakdown of glycogen into glucose [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-22 -->

Explanation:

**A452.** Contracting muscle cells take up glucose from the blood, lowering blood glucose [1]; this improves the response of body cells to insulin and reduces the amount of insulin needed [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-23 -->

Explanation:

**A453.** Increase = 0.40 × 5.5 = 2.2 mmol L⁻¹ [1]; new concentration = 5.5 + 2.2 = 7.7 mmol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-24 -->

Explanation:

**A454.** Without insulin, body cells cannot take up glucose, so they break down stored fats and proteins for energy [1]; blood glucose rises above the renal threshold, so glucose is excreted in the urine [1]; the glucose draws water into the urine by osmosis, increasing urine output [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-25 -->

Explanation:

**A455.** Insulin is secreted by beta cells when blood glucose is high [1]; glucagon is secreted by alpha cells when blood glucose is low [1]; insulin promotes glucose uptake by cells and the conversion of glucose into glycogen [1]; glucagon promotes the breakdown of liver glycogen into glucose, which is released into the blood [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-26 -->

Explanation:

**A456.** The islets of Langerhans secrete hormones directly into the blood, which is an endocrine function [1]; acinar cells secrete digestive enzymes into ducts that carry them to the small intestine, which is an exocrine function [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-27 -->

Explanation:

**A457.** Cells, especially brain cells, need a constant supply of glucose for cellular respiration [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-28 -->

Explanation:

**A458.** Blood glucose can fall well below the set point [1]; the brain depends on glucose for cellular respiration, so it is deprived of fuel, causing confusion and, in severe cases, unconsciousness [1]; the body's glucagon and adrenaline responses may not restore blood glucose quickly enough to prevent this [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-29 -->

Explanation:

**A459.** Glycogen is a large, insoluble molecule, so storing glucose as glycogen does not raise the solute concentration of the cytoplasm and does not draw water into the cell by osmosis [1]; glycogen can be quickly broken down into glucose when the cell needs it [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-bgr-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Water balance

**A460.** B. posterior pituitary gland.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-1 -->

Explanation: ADH is made by neurosecretory cells in the hypothalamus, then travels down their axons and is stored in and released from the posterior pituitary. The anterior pituitary makes its own hormones, such as TSH.

**A461.** C. increasing the permeability of the distal tubules and collecting ducts to water, so more water is reabsorbed.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-2 -->

Explanation: ADH causes more aquaporins to be inserted into the membranes of distal tubule and collecting duct cells. More water moves by osmosis back into the blood, producing a small volume of concentrated urine.

**A462.** A. osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-3 -->

Explanation: Osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus detect changes in blood solute concentration and adjust ADH release and thirst. Baroreceptors detect pressure, not concentration.

**A463.** D. ADH secretion decreases; a large volume of dilute urine is produced
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-4 -->

Explanation: Drinking lots of water dilutes the blood. Osmoreceptors detect this and ADH release falls. Collecting ducts become less permeable to water, so more water stays in the filtrate and a large volume of dilute urine results.

**A464.** B. produce more urine and become dehydrated.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-5 -->

Explanation: With less ADH, less water is reabsorbed from the collecting ducts. More dilute urine is produced and the body loses water, which contributes to dehydration.

**A465.** Water loss in sweat raises the solute concentration of the blood, which is detected by osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus; the hypothalamus causes more ADH to be released from the posterior pituitary (and thirst is stimulated); ADH increases the permeability of the distal tubules and collecting ducts to water, so more water is reabsorbed into the blood and a small volume of concentrated urine is produced.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-6 -->

Explanation:

**A466.** Very long loops of Henle (relative to kidney size); these build a very high solute concentration in the medulla, so a large amount of water moves by osmosis out of the collecting ducts and is reabsorbed, leaving a very small volume of highly concentrated urine. (Also accept high ADH levels or highly permeable collecting ducts with matching explanation.)
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-7 -->

Explanation:

**A467.** Water lost by sweating raises blood solute concentration, so more ADH is released and more water is reabsorbed from the collecting ducts; the same amount of urea and pigment is dissolved in a smaller volume of water, so the urine is more concentrated and darker.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-8 -->

Explanation:

**A468.** C. glomerulus and Bowman's capsule.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-9 -->

Explanation: Blood pressure in the glomerular capillaries forces water and small solutes out into Bowman's capsule. The loop of Henle, distal tubule and collecting duct mainly reabsorb and secrete substances rather than filter the blood.

**A469.** B. Bowman's capsule → proximal convoluted tubule → loop of Henle → distal convoluted tubule → collecting duct
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-10 -->

Explanation: Filtrate passes from Bowman's capsule to the proximal tubule, then down and up the loop of Henle, through the distal tubule and finally into the collecting duct. The loop of Henle always lies between the proximal and distal tubules.

**A470.** D. The filtrate becomes more dilute, and the surrounding medulla becomes more concentrated.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-11 -->

Explanation: Removing ions while keeping water in the tubule makes the filtrate more dilute as it rises, and the ions build up in the surrounding medulla. This gradient later allows water to be drawn out of the collecting duct by osmosis.

**A471.** A. 0.4 mol L⁻¹ sodium chloride solution
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-12 -->

Explanation: Sodium chloride dissociates into two ions, so 0.4 mol L⁻¹ gives about 0.8 osmol L⁻¹, more than 0.6 osmol L⁻¹ from glucose or 0.5 osmol L⁻¹ from urea. Ignoring dissociation makes the sodium chloride solution look like only 0.4 osmol L⁻¹, which is lower than the glucose solution.

**A472.** B. Blood osmolarity rises, ADH release rises, more water is reabsorbed, and osmolarity falls back towards normal.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-13 -->

Explanation: Negative feedback reverses the change: higher osmolarity raises ADH, which increases water reabsorption and dilutes the blood back towards its set point. The other options either push the change further (positive feedback) or have ADH moving in the wrong direction.

**A473.** C. It takes in very little water by mouth and excretes a large volume of dilute urine.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-14 -->

Explanation: Water enters the fish by osmosis across its gills and body surface, so it drinks very little and produces large volumes of dilute urine while actively taking up salts. A marine fish has the opposite problem, losing water by osmosis and drinking seawater.

**A474.** A. Too little ADH is produced, or the collecting ducts do not respond to ADH.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-15 -->

Explanation: Without ADH, or without a response to it, the collecting ducts stay impermeable to water, so little water is reabsorbed. Excess ADH would produce a small volume of concentrated urine, and insulin deficiency causes diabetes mellitus rather than insipidus.

**A475.** B. 0.83%
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-16 -->

Explanation: 1.5 ÷ 180 × 100 ≈ 0.83%, so more than 99% of the filtrate is reabsorbed. Misplacing a decimal point gives 8.3%, which would wrongly suggest ten times as much filtrate is excreted.

**A476.** C. They increase the surface area for reabsorption of substances from the filtrate.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-17 -->

Explanation: The microvilli form a brush border that greatly increases surface area, so more glucose, ions and water can be reabsorbed into the blood. Filtration occurs in the glomerulus, and ADH is made in the hypothalamus rather than the tubule.

**A477.** A. plasma proteins
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-18 -->

Explanation: Plasma proteins are too large to pass through the glomerular filter, so they stay in the blood. Urea, creatinine and excess sodium ions are wastes or regulated substances that are excreted in urine.

**A478.** D. about 4 times
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-19 -->

Explanation: Human urine can reach about 1200 mOsm L⁻¹, while plasma is about 300 mOsm L⁻¹, so urine is roughly four times more concentrated. Answers of 40 or 100 times assume the kidney can concentrate urine far beyond this.

**A479.** B. Sweat loses more water than solute, leaving the blood more concentrated.
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-20 -->

Explanation: Sweat has a lower solute concentration than plasma, so the body loses proportionally more water than solute and the blood becomes more concentrated. The first option ignores the word hypotonic, and urea is not a major component of sweat.

**A480.** Proximal convoluted tubule [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-21 -->

Explanation:

**A481.** Blood pressure forces water and small solutes (such as glucose, urea and ions) out of the glomerular capillaries and into Bowman's capsule [1]; blood cells and large molecules are too big to pass through the filtration membrane, so they remain in the blood [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-22 -->

Explanation:

**A482.** The descending limb is permeable to water [1]; water moves out of the filtrate by osmosis into the more concentrated medulla, so the filtrate becomes more concentrated as it descends [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-23 -->

Explanation:

**A483.** Blood flows past a semi-permeable (dialysis) membrane [1]; urea diffuses across the membrane from the blood, where its concentration is higher, into the dialysis fluid, where it is lower [1]; small waste molecules pass through the membrane while blood cells and large proteins are retained in the blood [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-24 -->

Explanation:

**A484.** Sweat from the skin [1]; water vapour in exhaled air [1] (water lost in faeces is also accepted).
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-25 -->

Explanation:

**A485.** The total concentration of dissolved solute particles in a solution, measured in osmol L⁻¹ [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-26 -->

Explanation:

**A486.** Osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus detect the rise in blood osmolarity and stimulate the sensation of thirst [1]; the person drinks water, which dilutes the blood back towards normal [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-wb-27 -->

Explanation:

#### Malfunctions of homeostasis

**A487.** A. the immune system destroying the beta cells of the pancreas, so little or no insulin is produced.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-1 -->

Explanation: Type 1 diabetes is an autoimmune disease in which beta cells are destroyed, so insulin must be supplied by injection or pump. Reduced cell response to insulin describes type 2.

**A488.** C. Insulin is produced, but target cells respond poorly to it (insulin resistance).
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-2 -->

Explanation: In type 2 diabetes, insulin is often still produced, sometimes in large amounts, but cells such as muscle and fat cells respond poorly. It is linked to genetic and lifestyle factors, not one allele.

**A489.** D. P has type 2 diabetes and Q has type 1 diabetes.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-3 -->

Explanation: P has high glucose despite high insulin, which points to insulin resistance (type 2). Q produces almost no insulin, which fits beta cell destruction (type 1). A healthy fasting level is about 4–6 mmol L⁻¹.

**A490.** B. Weight loss, a fast heart rate and feeling hot
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-4 -->

Explanation: Thyroxine raises metabolic rate. Too much speeds up cellular respiration and heat production, causing weight loss, heat intolerance and a fast heart rate. Weight gain and feeling cold fit hypothyroidism.

**A491.** C. the amount of glucose in the filtrate is greater than the nephron's carriers can reabsorb.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-5 -->

Explanation: Glucose is always filtered. Normally the proximal tubule reabsorbs all of it, but when blood glucose is very high the transport proteins become saturated and the excess stays in the urine.

**A492.** Type 1 is an autoimmune disease in which beta cells are destroyed, so little or no insulin is made; type 2 involves insulin resistance (cells respond poorly to insulin), sometimes with reduced insulin output later, and is linked to genetics, obesity and inactivity; type 1 is managed with insulin injections or a pump, while type 2 is often first managed with diet, exercise and weight loss, then oral medication and sometimes insulin.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-6 -->

Explanation:

**A493.** Without ADH, the distal tubules and collecting ducts stay poorly permeable to water, so little water is reabsorbed; the person produces a very large volume of dilute urine; as a result they lose water and become dehydrated, and blood solute concentration rises, which is detected by osmoreceptors, so they feel extreme thirst and drink large amounts.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-7 -->

Explanation:

**A494.** Thyroxine normally inhibits the release of TRH from the hypothalamus and TSH from the anterior pituitary (negative feedback); with low thyroxine, this inhibition is removed, so TSH levels stay high; continuous TSH stimulation makes thyroid cells grow and divide, so the gland enlarges, yet it still cannot make thyroxine without iodine.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-8 -->

Explanation:

**A495.** B. Alpha cells release glucagon, which stimulates the liver to break down glycogen and release glucose.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-9 -->

Explanation: Falling blood glucose is detected by the alpha cells of the pancreas, which secrete glucagon to trigger glycogen breakdown in the liver. Releasing more insulin would push glucose even lower, and ADH controls water balance rather than blood glucose.

**A496.** C. Working muscles take up glucose from the blood, even when the response to insulin is weak.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-10 -->

Explanation: Contracting muscles take up glucose through a pathway that does not depend on a strong insulin response, so blood glucose falls. Glucagon raises blood glucose, and ADH controls water balance rather than glucose.

**A497.** A. Iodine is a component of the thyroxine molecule, so thyroxine cannot be made without it.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-11 -->

Explanation: Thyroxine contains four iodine atoms, so a dietary lack of iodine stops thyroxine production. The other options link iodine to hormones or processes that it does not control.

**A498.** D. Urine contains no glucose, and blood glucose is normal.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-12 -->

Explanation: Diabetes insipidus results from too little ADH and does not involve glucose, so urine contains no glucose and blood glucose is normal. Diabetes mellitus causes glucose to spill into the urine, where it draws water with it.

**A499.** B. Without insulin, cells cannot use glucose effectively, so fats are broken down for energy and acidic ketones build up.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-13 -->

Explanation: Without insulin, body cells cannot take in glucose, so the body breaks down fat for energy, producing acidic ketones that lower blood pH. The other options describe excess insulin, kidney reabsorption or water balance, none of which causes this.

**A500.** C. Its temperature set point is raised, so the body conserves and produces heat until it reaches the new set point.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-14 -->

Explanation: Pyrogens released during infection reset the hypothalamic set point to a higher temperature. The body then shivers and constricts skin blood vessels to conserve and produce heat, and cooling responses only begin once the temperature is above the new set point.

**A501.** A. From the blood into the dialysis fluid, by diffusion down a concentration gradient.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-15 -->

Explanation: The dialysis fluid has little or no urea, so urea diffuses out of the blood through the semi-permeable membrane. Movement in the other direction would need more urea in the fluid than in the blood, and the membrane allows small waste molecules through.

**A502.** D. 50%
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-16 -->

Explanation: (16.0 − 8.0) ÷ 16.0 × 100 = 50%. Dividing by the new value (8.0) gives 100%, dividing the starting value by the new value gives 200%, and 8% comes from reading the difference of 8 mmol L⁻¹ as a percentage.

**A503.** A. Glucose left in the filtrate lowers its water potential, so less water is reabsorbed by osmosis.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-17 -->

Explanation: Unabsorbed glucose makes the filtrate solute-rich, so water stays in the tubule and is excreted (osmotic diuresis). ADH would reduce urine volume rather than increase it, and insulin does not control filtrate production.

**A504.** C. Urea
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-18 -->

Explanation: The kidneys normally excrete urea, so it accumulates when they fail. The other substances are not waste products the kidneys remove in this way, and pepsin is a digestive enzyme made in the stomach.

**A505.** The pump delivers a small, continuous (basal) dose of insulin over 24 hours, similar to the steady insulin release of a healthy pancreas between meals [1]; it can also deliver extra bolus doses at mealtimes, so insulin is available as glucose enters the blood after eating [1]; the dose can be adjusted for activity and food, so blood glucose stays closer to the normal range and hyperglycaemia or hypoglycaemia is less likely [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-19 -->

Explanation:

**A506.** Beta cells in the islets of Langerhans detect the rise and secrete insulin into the blood [1]; insulin causes liver and muscle cells to take up glucose and store it as glycogen, so blood glucose falls back towards normal [1]; in type 1 diabetes the beta cells have been destroyed by the immune system, so no insulin is released and glucose stays high in the blood [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-20 -->

Explanation:

**A507.** The failed kidneys cannot excrete urea and other nitrogenous wastes, so these build up in the blood [1]; excess water and ions such as potassium also accumulate, which can cause swelling, high blood pressure and heart problems [1]; in haemodialysis, blood flows past a semi-permeable membrane and wastes diffuse into the dialysis fluid down their concentration gradient [1]; the dialysis fluid has suitable ion concentrations so useful ions are not lost, and excess water can be removed by applying pressure to the blood side [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-21 -->

Explanation:

**A508.** Desmopressin binds to receptors on the cells of the distal tubule and collecting duct, increasing their permeability to water [1]; more water is reabsorbed by osmosis, so less urine is produced and it is more concentrated [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-22 -->

Explanation:

**A509.** Sweating and the evaporation of sweat are the main cooling responses, so with sweating stopped much less heat is lost from the body [1]; heat is still produced by metabolism and gained from the environment, so body temperature keeps rising [1]; at this temperature enzymes and proteins begin to denature, disrupting metabolic reactions and damaging cells, especially in the brain [1]; the negative feedback responses have failed to return the body to its set point, so the condition becomes life-threatening without rapid cooling [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-23 -->

Explanation:

**A510.** At 37 °C with a set point of 39 °C, the body detects its temperature as too low, so it shivers and constricts skin blood vessels to produce and conserve heat [1]; with the set point back at 37 °C and body temperature at 38.5 °C, the temperature is detected as too high, so the body sweats and dilates skin arterioles to lose heat [1]; in both cases the responses oppose the change and stop once body temperature returns to the set point [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-24 -->

Explanation:

**A511.** Target cells respond poorly to insulin, so blood glucose stays high and the beta cells are stimulated to secrete more insulin [1]; over years the beta cells are overworked and can be damaged, so their insulin output falls [1]; with less insulin and continuing resistance, glucose uptake and storage decline, so blood glucose rises further and is harder to control [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-25 -->

Explanation:
