**A512.** The brain relies almost entirely on glucose as its energy source for cellular respiration [1]; low blood glucose reduces the glucose supplied to brain cells, so less ATP is produced and neurons cannot function normally, causing confusion [1]; the body responds by releasing adrenaline and glucagon, and the adrenaline causes shakiness [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mal-26 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 2 AOS 1: Inheritance

#### Genomes, genes and alleles

**A513.** B. the complete set of genetic material (DNA) present in its cells.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-1 -->

Explanation: The genome is all the DNA of an organism, including genes and non-coding regions (and mitochondrial DNA in eukaryotes). The full set of proteins is the proteome.

**A514.** C. one of the alternative forms of a gene, found at the same locus.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-2 -->

Explanation: Different alleles of a gene have slightly different base sequences but sit at the same locus on homologous chromosomes. The position itself is the locus.

**A515.** A. It contains about 3 billion base pairs, and less than 2% codes for proteins.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-3 -->

Explanation: The human haploid nuclear genome has about 3 billion base pairs and around 20 000 protein-coding genes. These make up only a small fraction (about 1–2%) of the DNA. Apart from identical twins, individuals differ in sequence.

**A516.** D. heterozygous for the wing shape gene.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-4 -->

Explanation: Having two different alleles at a locus means the fly is heterozygous. Its phenotype depends on which allele is dominant (here normal wings is dominant), but it is heterozygous either way.

**A517.** B. a difference in the sequence of nucleotide bases in the gene.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-5 -->

Explanation: Alleles differ in their base sequences. These differences may change the amino acid sequence of the protein produced. Alleles of the same gene share the same locus.

**A518.** Genome: the complete set of DNA (genetic material) of an organism; gene: a section of DNA at a particular locus that carries the code for a functional product such as a protein; allele: one of the different forms (variants) of a gene, with a slightly different base sequence, found at the same locus.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-6 -->

Explanation:

**A519.** Prokaryotes usually have a single circular chromosome found free in the cytosol (nucleoid region), while eukaryotes have several linear chromosomes inside a nucleus; prokaryotes often also carry small circular plasmids, whereas eukaryotes have DNA in mitochondria (and chloroplasts) and much more non-coding DNA wound around histone proteins.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-7 -->

Explanation:

**A520.** The alleles have different base sequences, so they may code for a different amino acid sequence; this changes the shape and function of the protein (or stops it being made or working), which changes the observable characteristic.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-8 -->

Explanation:

**A521.** B. 23
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-9 -->

Explanation: Gametes are haploid, carrying one chromosome from each homologous pair, so 46 ÷ 2 = 23. The 92 comes from doubling the chromosome number, and 46 is the body cell number, not the gamete number.

**A522.** A. A pair of chromosomes with the same genes at the same loci, one inherited from each parent.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-10 -->

Explanation: Homologous chromosomes carry the same genes in the same order, though they may carry different alleles. Identical copies made by replication are sister chromatids, and the X and Y are only partly homologous.

**A523.** C. Genotype is the set of alleles an organism carries; phenotype is the observable characteristic that results.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-11 -->

Explanation: Genotype is the allele combination an organism carries, while phenotype is the observable trait that results, which can also be affected by the environment. The option that reverses the two terms is the most common mistake.

**A524.** C. pp
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-12 -->

Explanation: White flowers need two copies of the recessive allele, pp. Pp has one dominant P allele, so the plant is purple, and PP is also purple.

**A525.** B. Mutation, which changes the base sequence of a gene.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-13 -->

Explanation: Mutation changes base sequences, creating new variants of a gene at the same locus. Meiosis reshuffles existing alleles into new combinations but does not create new ones.

**A526.** A. Each diploid individual can carry at most two of these alleles, but the population as a whole can carry all three.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-14 -->

Explanation: A diploid individual has two homologous chromosomes, so it can carry at most two alleles at that locus, while the population gene pool can contain more. Carrying all three would need three homologous chromosomes.

**A527.** D. The protein is unchanged, because more than one codon can code for the same amino acid.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-15 -->

Explanation: The genetic code is degenerate, so some base changes leave the amino acid unchanged. A single base change does not move a gene or change the chromosome number, and not every change to a protein makes it non-functional.

**A528.** C. 22
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-16 -->

Explanation: A human cell has 23 pairs of chromosomes, one of which is the sex chromosome pair, leaving 22 pairs of autosomes. The 44 is the number of autosome chromosomes, not pairs.

**A529.** D. the phenotype can be influenced by the environment as well as the genotype.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-17 -->

Explanation: Phenotype results from genotype and environment together, so identical genotypes can produce different phenotypes under different conditions. The genotype itself did not change.

**A530.** B. about 650 times
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-18 -->

Explanation: 3 × 10⁹ ÷ 4.6 × 10⁶ ≈ 6.5 × 10², which is about 650 times. The 65 and 6 500 distractors come from errors in powers of ten.

**A531.** A. nucleotide, gene, chromosome, genome
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-19 -->

Explanation: A nucleotide is a single unit, a gene is many nucleotides, a chromosome carries many genes, and the genome is the full set of chromosomes. Placing the genome before the chromosome reverses the order.

**A532.** A. It is a single long molecule of DNA associated with histone proteins.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-20 -->

Explanation: Each eukaryotic chromosome is one linear DNA molecule wound around histones and carries many genes. Chromosomes in a haploid gamete are unpaired, so the last option is a tempting but wrong idea.

**A533.** DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-21 -->

Explanation:

**A534.** A variation in the DNA sequence in which a single base (nucleotide) differs at a specific position [1], and the variant is common enough to be found in a substantial proportion of a population [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-22 -->

Explanation:

**A535.** Diploid genome = 2 × haploid genome [1]; 2 × 3 × 10⁹ = 6 × 10⁹ base pairs [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-23 -->

Explanation:

**A536.** Comparing sequences can identify genes or alleles linked to a trait or disease [1]; this can support diagnostic tests, treatments or the study of evolutionary relationships [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-24 -->

Explanation:

**A537.** Dominance describes how an allele's effect shows in a heterozygote, not how often the allele occurs [1]; allele frequency depends on factors such as mutation, selection and chance in a population [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-25 -->

Explanation:

**A538.** Copies of allele A = 4 × 2 + 3 × 1 = 11 [1]; total alleles = 10 × 2 = 20 [1]; frequency of A = 11 ÷ 20 = 0.55 (55%) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-26 -->

Explanation:

**A539.** Having two identical alleles of a gene at a locus (for example AA or aa) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-27 -->

Explanation:

**A540.** Both cells developed by mitosis from the same fertilised egg, so each carries an identical copy of the DNA [1]; the cells express different sets of genes, producing different proteins that give each cell its structure and function [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-28 -->

Explanation:

**A541.** Having a single set of chromosomes, so one allele of each gene in a cell [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gen-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Chromosomes and karyotypes

**A542.** C. 46 chromosomes: 22 pairs of autosomes and one pair of sex chromosomes.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-1 -->

Explanation: Human somatic cells are diploid (2n = 46), with 22 pairs of autosomes and one pair of sex chromosomes. After replication there are still 46 chromosomes, each made of two sister chromatids.

**A543.** A. size, centromere position and banding pattern.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-2 -->

Explanation: Karyotypes pair and order chromosomes by length, centromere position and the pattern of light and dark bands from staining (e.g. G-banding). Dominance and alleles cannot be seen in a karyotype.

**A544.** B. a male with Down syndrome.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-3 -->

Explanation: Three copies of chromosome 21 is trisomy 21 (Down syndrome). The XY sex chromosomes indicate a male. Klinefelter syndrome is XXY, and Turner syndrome is a single X (45,X).

**A545.** D. They carry the same genes at the same loci, but may have different alleles, with one inherited from each parent.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-4 -->

Explanation: Homologous chromosomes have the same genes in the same order, but one comes from the mother and one from the father, so alleles may differ. Identical copies joined at a centromere are sister chromatids.

**A546.** C. a single base substitution in the gene linked to cystic fibrosis.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-5 -->

Explanation: Karyotypes show chromosome number and large structural changes. A change of one nucleotide in a gene is far too small to see and needs DNA analysis instead.

**A547.** At metaphase the chromosomes are fully condensed (supercoiled), so they are at their shortest and thickest and easiest to see and count; each chromosome is made of two sister chromatids joined at the centromere, so their size, shape, centromere position and banding pattern can be compared to pair them.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A548.** Diploid number 2n = 78; haploid number n = 39; a sperm cell has 38 autosomes, because a gamete contains one chromosome from each of the 39 pairs, and one of these is a sex chromosome (X or Y), leaving 38 autosomes.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A549.** Nondisjunction occurred during meiosis in one parent; the sex chromosomes (homologues in meiosis I, or sister chromatids in meiosis II) failed to separate, producing a gamete with no sex chromosome; this gamete was fertilised by a normal gamete carrying one X, giving a zygote with 45 chromosomes and a single X.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A550.** A. sister chromatids, each now an individual chromosome.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-9 -->

Explanation: In anaphase the centromeres divide and sister chromatids are pulled to opposite poles, each becoming an individual chromosome. Homologous chromosomes separate only in meiosis I, and bivalents do not form in mitosis.

**A551.** B. the region where two sister chromatids are joined and where spindle fibres attach.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-10 -->

Explanation: The centromere is the constricted region that holds sister chromatids together and is the attachment point for spindle fibres. The tip is a telomere, and crossing over happens along the arms between non-sister chromatids, not at the centromere.

**A552.** C. a long DNA molecule wound around histone proteins and condensed for division.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-11 -->

Explanation: Chromosomes are long DNA molecules packaged around histone proteins, and they condense into visible structures for division. Chromosomes are not made of RNA, and genes sit on DNA rather than on a protein chain.

**A553.** A. a deletion.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-12 -->

Explanation: A deletion loses the segment and the genes on it, so that chromosome carries less genetic material. A duplication adds an extra copy, an inversion reverses the segment in place, and a translocation moves it to a different chromosome.

**A554.** B. an inversion.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-13 -->

Explanation: In an inversion the segment stays on the same chromosome but is reversed, so the same genes are present in reverse order. A deletion removes material, a duplication adds a copy, and a translocation moves a segment to another chromosome.

**A555.** D. 4
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-14 -->

Explanation: A diploid number of 8 means four homologous pairs, because each pair has two chromosomes. 8 counts individual chromosomes rather than pairs, 2 is too few, and 16 is double the diploid number.

**A556.** A. Y chromosome.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-15 -->

Explanation: The SRY gene on the Y chromosome directs development of male gonads, so the presence of a Y chromosome normally leads to male development. The X chromosome does not carry SRY, and autosomes such as chromosomes 1 and 21 are not involved in sex determination.

**A557.** B. chromosomes with new combinations of alleles, while the chromosome number stays the same.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-16 -->

Explanation: Crossing over swaps segments between homologous chromosomes, creating new allele combinations without changing the chromosome number. Changes in number come from nondisjunction, and a doubling of the number would be polyploidy.

**A558.** C. a photographed, ordered display of the chromosomes from one cell, arranged in homologous pairs.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-17 -->

Explanation: A karyotype is an ordered image of the chromosomes from a single stained cell, usually at metaphase. A gene map shows loci along a chromosome and a genome sequence lists the bases, so neither is a karyotype.

**A559.** D. white blood cells stimulated to divide in culture.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-18 -->

Explanation: White blood cells can be stimulated to divide in culture and then arrested at metaphase. Mature red blood cells have no nucleus, and neurons and mature sperm cells are not dividing, so their chromosomes cannot be arranged for a karyotype.

**A560.** A. trisomy 18, also known as Edwards syndrome.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-19 -->

Explanation: Three copies of an autosome is a trisomy, and trisomy 18 is Edwards syndrome. Monosomy means one copy is missing, while deletions and translocations change the segments of a chromosome without adding a whole extra chromosome.

**A561.** B. a human sperm cell.
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-20 -->

Explanation: Sperm cells are gametes and contain one set of 23 chromosomes. A zygote forms when two gametes fuse, so it is diploid, and neurons and liver cells are somatic cells that are also diploid.

**A562.** 47,XXX [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-21 -->

Explanation:

**A563.** A karyotype is an image of the chromosomes from one cell, showing their number and structure [1]; a pedigree is a family diagram that traces the inheritance of a trait across generations [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-22 -->

Explanation:

**A564.** The X and Y differ in size and carry different genes, so most of their length does not pair [1]; they share only a small pairing region at their tips, where they can pair and cross over in meiosis I [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-23 -->

Explanation:

**A565.** Crossing over: non-sister chromatids of a homologous pair exchange segments in prophase I [1], so the chromatids carry new combinations of alleles [1]; independent assortment: during metaphase I each homologous pair lines up and orients at random [1], so each gamete receives a random mix of maternal and paternal chromosomes [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-24 -->

Explanation:

**A566.** Diploid number 2n = 24 [1]; a triploid cell has 3n = 36 chromosomes [1]; triploidy adds a complete extra haploid set so every chromosome appears three times, whereas trisomy involves an extra copy of only one chromosome [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-25 -->

Explanation:

**A567.** Klinefelter (male): 47 chromosomes [1], sex chromosomes XXY [1]; Turner (female): 45 chromosomes [1], a single X chromosome with no second sex chromosome (45,X) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-26 -->

Explanation:

**A568.** Monosomy removes one copy of every gene on the chromosome, halving the expression of those genes and usually disrupting development so the embryo does not survive [1]; trisomy 21 adds one copy of one of the smallest autosomes, which has relatively few genes, so the extra dose is tolerated and the embryo can develop to birth [1]; the extra dose still causes Down syndrome features, such as intellectual disability [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-27 -->

Explanation:

**A569.** Giemsa stain [1]
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-28 -->

Explanation:

**A570.** Female (XX) [1]; 46 chromosomes, so there is no change in the total number [1]; a translocation in which segments are exchanged between chromosomes 9 and 22 [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-chr-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Meiosis and variation

**A571.** A. prophase I, between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-1 -->

Explanation: In prophase I, homologous chromosomes pair up and non-sister chromatids exchange segments at chiasmata. This makes new combinations of alleles on each chromatid.

**A572.** D. the random orientation of homologous pairs at the cell equator during metaphase I.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-2 -->

Explanation: Each homologous pair lines up at metaphase I independently of the others. Which parental chromosome goes to each pole is random, so many combinations of maternal and paternal chromosomes are possible.

**A573.** C. 16
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-3 -->

Explanation: The number of combinations is 2ⁿ, where n is the haploid number. Here n = 4, so 2⁴ = 16. A common error is to use 2n instead and calculate 2⁸ = 256, or to just double 8.

**A574.** B. four genetically different haploid cells.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-4 -->

Explanation: Meiosis has two divisions, giving four cells, each with half the chromosome number. Crossing over and independent assortment make each one genetically different. Two identical diploid cells is the result of mitosis.

**A575.** C. all abnormal: two with an extra chromosome and two missing a chromosome.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-5 -->

Explanation: If a homologous pair moves to the same pole in meiosis I, one cell gets both and the other gets neither. Both cells then divide normally, so all four gametes are abnormal (two n + 1 and two n − 1). Nondisjunction in meiosis II gives two normal and two abnormal gametes.

**A576.** Mitosis involves one division and produces two daughter cells, while meiosis involves two divisions and produces four; mitosis keeps the chromosome number the same (diploid to diploid), while meiosis halves it (diploid to haploid); mitosis produces genetically identical cells for growth and repair, while meiosis produces genetically different gametes because of crossing over and independent assortment (homologues pair up only in meiosis).
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-6 -->

Explanation:

**A577.** During prophase I, non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes exchange equivalent segments of DNA at chiasmata; this creates chromatids with new combinations of maternal and paternal alleles (recombinants), so each gamete gets a unique combination of alleles.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-7 -->

Explanation:

**A578.** Crossing over in prophase I swaps segments between non-sister chromatids, making new allele combinations on each chromosome; independent assortment of homologous pairs at metaphase I produces many different combinations of maternal and paternal chromosomes in each gamete; random fertilisation means any one of the father's genetically different sperm can fuse with any one of the mother's genetically different eggs.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-8 -->

Explanation:

**A579.** A. A bivalent, also called a tetrad.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-9 -->

Explanation: Synapsis brings a homologous pair together, and because each chromosome has already replicated, the pair contains four chromatids. A dyad is a single replicated chromosome with only two chromatids, as seen in meiosis II.

**A580.** B. 32
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-10 -->

Explanation: An egg is a haploid gamete formed by meiosis, so it has half the diploid number: 64 ÷ 2 = 32. The answer 128 is the number of chromatids after DNA replication, and 16 wrongly halves the number twice, as if meiosis had two halving steps.

**A581.** C. Mutation, which changes the base sequence of DNA.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-11 -->

Explanation: Mutations change the DNA sequence and are the only source of entirely new alleles. The other three processes shuffle alleles that already exist into new combinations.

**A582.** D. Anaphase II.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-12 -->

Explanation: In anaphase II the centromeres divide and the sister chromatids are pulled to opposite poles, as in mitosis. In anaphase I it is homologous chromosomes that separate, while sister chromatids remain joined.

**A583.** A. One chromosome from each parent, carrying genes for the same characteristics at the same loci.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-13 -->

Explanation: Homologues are a matching pair, one inherited from each parent, carrying the same genes in the same order, although the alleles may differ. Two identical copies joined at the centromere are sister chromatids, and X and Y are only partly homologous.

**A584.** B. DNA replicates during interphase, before meiosis begins.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-14 -->

Explanation: During S phase of interphase, each chromosome replicates into two identical sister chromatids joined at the centromere. Crossing over and the separation of homologues happen later in meiosis, and fertilisation occurs after meiosis is complete.

**A585.** D. The testes and ovaries.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-15 -->

Explanation: Meiosis produces gametes, which are sperm in the testes and eggs in the ovaries. Skin, liver, intestinal and bone marrow cells divide by mitosis to grow, repair and replace cells.

**A586.** A. 25%
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-16 -->

Explanation: Each pair assorts independently, so the chance of an A allele is 1/2 and of a B allele is 1/2, giving 1/2 × 1/2 = 1/4, or 25%. The answer 50% accounts for only one gene, and 12.5% comes from multiplying three halves as if a third gene were involved.

**A587.** B. 3 bivalents, 12 chromatids
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-17 -->

Explanation: Six chromosomes form three homologous pairs, so there are three bivalents, each with four chromatids, giving 12 in total. Six bivalents would count each chromosome as a pair, and 24 chromatids wrongly gives four chromatids for every chromosome.

**A588.** C. trisomy 21, usually caused by nondisjunction during meiosis.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-18 -->

Explanation: Having three copies of one chromosome is trisomy, usually caused when homologues or sister chromatids fail to separate (nondisjunction). Polyploidy adds whole sets of chromosomes, and monosomy has only one copy of a chromosome.

**A589.** D. haploid, with each chromosome still made of two chromatids.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-19 -->

Explanation: Meiosis I separates homologous pairs, halving the chromosome number, but sister chromatids stay joined until anaphase II. Cells with one chromatid per chromosome describe the state after meiosis II, and diploid cells would result from mitosis.

**A590.** B. It halves the chromosome number, so that fertilisation restores the diploid number.
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-20 -->

Explanation: Gametes must be haploid so that fusing two of them produces a diploid zygote with the normal chromosome number. Mitosis, not meiosis, makes identical cells for growth and repair, and the zygote is diploid, not haploid.

**A591.** Homologous chromosomes are separated and move into different daughter cells [1]; each daughter cell therefore has half the chromosome number of the parent cell, so it is haploid [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-21 -->

Explanation:

**A592.** Crossing over occurs in prophase I and exchanges segments between non-sister chromatids, creating chromosomes with mixed alleles [1]; independent assortment occurs at metaphase I when each homologous pair is oriented at random, so each gamete receives a random mix of whole maternal and paternal chromosomes [1]; both occur in the same meiosis, so together they make each gamete genetically different from the others [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-22 -->

Explanation:

**A593.** Sister chromatids are two copies of the same chromosome, made by DNA replication during S phase [1]; homologous chromosomes come from different parents, so they may carry different alleles of the same genes [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-23 -->

Explanation:

**A594.** 46 chromosomes in the replicated cell [1]; 92 chromatids, since each chromosome has two chromatids [1]; 23 chromosomes in each gamete, because gametes are haploid [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-24 -->

Explanation:

**A595.** A mutation is a change in the base sequence of DNA, so it creates new alleles [1]; crossing over only rearranges existing alleles into new combinations on chromosomes [1]; mutations are rare and random, whereas crossing over occurs regularly during prophase I of meiosis [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-25 -->

Explanation:

**A596.** The centromere of each chromosome divides, so the two sister chromatids become separate chromosomes [1]; spindle fibres attached to the centromeres pull each chromosome to opposite poles of the cell [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-26 -->

Explanation:

**A597.** In prophase I, homologous chromosomes pair up (synapsis) to form bivalents, and crossing over may occur [1]; at metaphase I, bivalents line up at the equator with the two homologues of each pair facing opposite poles [1]; at anaphase I, homologous chromosomes are pulled to opposite poles while sister chromatids remain joined at their centromeres [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-27 -->

Explanation:

**A598.** Crossing over and independent assortment occur only in meiosis, not in mitosis [1]; in mitosis homologous chromosomes do not pair and the daughter cells are genetically identical, so no new variation is produced [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-28 -->

Explanation:

**A599.** n = 6, so independent assortment gives 2⁶ = 64 different gametes [1]; 64 different sperm types can each combine with 64 different egg types [1]; 64 × 64 = 4096 possible zygotes [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-mei-29 -->

Explanation:

#### Genotype, phenotype and environment

**A600.** B. its observable characteristics, resulting from the interaction of its genotype with the environment.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-1 -->

Explanation: Phenotype covers observable traits, including biochemical ones such as blood type. It depends on genotype and environment together. The alleles an organism carries are its genotype.

**A601.** D. the same genotype produced different phenotypes because of differences in the environment.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-2 -->

Explanation: The cuttings are clones with the same genotype. Soil pH affects how much aluminium the plant can take up, which changes flower pigment colour. Genes still matter, since the plant must have the genes for the pigment.

**A602.** A. dark, because the enzyme is active at the lower temperature.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-3 -->

Explanation: The cat has the same genotype in all skin cells. The temperature-sensitive enzyme works only in cooler regions, so cooling the skin lets dark pigment form. This shows the environment influencing phenotype.

**A603.** C. Height
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-4 -->

Explanation: Height is controlled by many genes and is also affected by nutrition. It produces a continuous range of values, not distinct categories. ABO blood group and cystic fibrosis are each controlled by one gene and fall into distinct classes.

**A604.** C. influenced by both genetic and environmental factors.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-5 -->

Explanation: Identical twins share all their DNA. Their higher concordance shows a genetic influence, but if genes alone decided the trait concordance would be close to 100%. A value of 45% shows the environment also matters.

**A605.** Continuous variation shows a range of values with no distinct categories, usually because the trait is polygenic and affected by environment, e.g. height or skin colour; discontinuous variation falls into a few distinct categories, usually controlled by one or a few genes, e.g. ABO blood group or attached versus free earlobes.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-6 -->

Explanation:

**A606.** The genotype (homozygous recessive) cannot be changed, so the enzyme is still non-functional; the environment (a diet low in phenylalanine) stops phenylalanine building up to harmful levels; so the phenotype depends on the interaction of genotype and environment, and the same genotype can produce a different phenotype under different conditions.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-7 -->

Explanation:

**A607.** Methyl groups can be added to DNA (or histones can be modified), which can switch genes off or reduce their expression without changing the base sequence; the twins experience different environments (diet, smoking, stress), which cause different methylation patterns, so different genes are expressed and their phenotypes diverge.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-8 -->

Explanation:

**A608.** A. norm of reaction
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-9 -->

Explanation: The norm of reaction is the range of phenotypes a genotype can produce depending on the environment. Codominance and linkage describe patterns of inheritance, and mutation rate describes how often changes occur in DNA.

**A609.** D. Two copies of the allele for free earlobes
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-10 -->

Explanation: Curly hair, blue eyes and a height of 172 cm are observable characteristics, so they are phenotypes. Two copies of an allele describe the genetic makeup of an individual.

**A610.** C. Weight gain after a long period of high-energy eating
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-11 -->

Explanation: Diet is an environmental factor, and eating more kilojoules than are used can increase body mass without any change in alleles. The other options are all determined by inherited alleles.

**A611.** B. ABO blood group
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-12 -->

Explanation: ABO blood group is set by the alleles inherited, and diet or exercise does not change it. Body mass, tanning and muscle size all respond to food, sunlight and training.

**A612.** A. The trait is usually controlled by many genes and influenced by the environment.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-13 -->

Explanation: Continuous variation arises when many genes and environmental factors together produce a range of values. A single gene with two alleles usually gives distinct categories rather than a continuous range, and continuous traits can be measured on a scale.

**A613.** D. Day length is an environmental factor that changes coat colour, so one genotype can produce different phenotypes at different times of year.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-14 -->

Explanation: Only day length was set to summer conditions, and the brown coat was kept despite winter temperatures, so day length acts as an environmental cue. The coat changes with the season each year, which rules out a mutation as the explanation.

**A614.** C. The genotype gives a similar phenotype across these farms, so the environment has only a small effect on milk yield.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-15 -->

Explanation: A narrow norm of reaction means the phenotype changes little between environments. The environment still has some effect because the range is not zero, and milk yield here is a continuous measure, not discrete categories.

**A615.** B. Genetically identical seedlings, all grown under the same conditions except for the fertiliser
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-16 -->

Explanation: Genetically identical seedlings keep genotype constant, and changing only the fertiliser isolates its effect on phenotype. The other designs allow genotype or other conditions to vary, so any height difference could have another cause.

**A616.** A. Several genes produce many allele combinations, and each gene adds a small effect to the phenotype.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-17 -->

Explanation: Polygenic traits combine many small effects, which produces many intermediate phenotypes. Sex linkage does not widen the range, and environment is only one source of variation, not the only one.

**A617.** C. A gene's product affects the phenotype, and the size of that effect can depend on environmental conditions.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-18 -->

Explanation: Genes code for proteins that influence traits, but environmental conditions can modify how strongly that influence is expressed. The other options wrongly claim fixed genetic control or no environmental role.

**A618.** D. A mutation in a sperm or egg cell
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-19 -->

Explanation: Only changes to the DNA in gametes can be inherited by offspring. A tan, a scar and weight gain are environmentally caused changes in phenotype that do not alter the DNA of gametes.

**A619.** B. The goitre is a phenotype produced by an environmental factor acting on a normal genotype.
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-20 -->

Explanation: Iodine deficiency is an environmental factor that can produce a goitre even when the genotype is normal. A goitre does not show a mutation or a particular allele, and diet does affect phenotype.

**A620.** A. Soil nitrogen availability
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-21 -->

Explanation: Soil nitrogen is an environmental factor that affects plant growth and leaf colour without changing the DNA. The other options are genetic factors, such as chromosome number, alleles and mutations.

**A621.** The plants have the same genotype, so genes do not explain the difference [1]; sunlight (the environment) acts on this genotype to produce a different phenotype [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-22 -->

Explanation:

**A622.** Other genes and environmental factors, such as diet, lifestyle or medical care, interact with the genotype [1]; so the same genotype can produce different phenotypes [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-23 -->

Explanation:

**A623.** Sunlight (UV exposure) increases melanin production, so the skin darkens [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-24 -->

Explanation:

**A624.** Environmental factors such as diet, temperature or light act on the genotype during development [1]; the genotype sets a range of possible phenotypes and the environment determines where in that range the organism falls [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-25 -->

Explanation:

**A625.** The claim is not valid [1]; environmental differences, such as diet, illness or lifestyle, can produce different phenotypes from the same genotype [1]; epigenetic changes or mutations that arise after the twins develop can also change gene expression [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-26 -->

Explanation:

**A626.** Partly incorrect: environmental factors change the phenotype without altering the DNA sequence in most cases [1]; but environmental mutagens such as UV radiation or chemicals can cause mutations that do change the genotype [1].
<!-- id: bio12a2-gpe-27 -->

Explanation:

#### Monohybrid crosses and test crosses

**A627.** B. 3 purple : 1 white
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-1 -->

Explanation: Pp × Pp gives 1 PP : 2 Pp : 1 pp. PP and Pp are both purple, so the phenotypic ratio is 3 purple : 1 white. A 1 : 1 ratio comes from Pp × pp, not from two heterozygotes.

**A628.** C. Cross the black guinea pig with a brown guinea pig
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-2 -->

Explanation: A test cross pairs the individual of unknown genotype with a homozygous recessive individual (bb, brown). Any recessive allele carried by the unknown parent will then show up in the offspring phenotype. Crossing with BB would give all black offspring whatever the unknown genotype is.

**A629.** B. Tt
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-3 -->

Explanation: About 1 : 1 tall : short is the result expected from Tt × tt. If the tall parent were TT, all offspring would be Tt and tall. The parent cannot be tt because it shows the dominant phenotype.

**A630.** B. 50%
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-4 -->

Explanation: CᴿCᵂ × CᴿCᵂ gives 1 CᴿCᴿ (red) : 2 CᴿCᵂ (pink) : 1 CᵂCᵂ (white), so half (50%) are pink. With incomplete dominance the heterozygote has its own intermediate phenotype, so the 3 : 1 pattern does not apply.

**A631.** A. Both parents must be heterozygous
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-5 -->

Explanation: A group O child is ii, so it received an i allele from each parent. The parents must therefore be Iᴬi and Iᴮi. An IᴮIᴮ parent could not pass on i, and no mutation is needed to explain the result.

**A632.** (a) Parents Bb (black) and bb (brown); Bb produces B and b gametes, bb produces only b gametes; (b) offspring genotypes 1 Bb : 1 bb; phenotypes 1 black : 1 brown (50% each).
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-6 -->

Explanation:

**A633.** Fertilisation is random, so each offspring has an independent 3/4 probability of being round and real counts deviate by chance from the predicted ratio; a larger sample (more offspring) or repeating the cross would make the results more reliable and closer to 3 : 1.
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-7 -->

Explanation:

**A634.** Genotype is the combination of alleles an organism has for a gene (e.g. Tt); phenotype is the observable characteristic produced by the genotype interacting with the environment (e.g. tall); homozygous means having two identical alleles for a gene (TT or tt); for example a TT plant and a Tt plant are both tall because T is dominant, so the same phenotype can come from different genotypes.
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-8 -->

Explanation:

**A635.** A. Alleles
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-9 -->

Explanation: Alleles are the alternative forms of a gene at the same locus on homologous chromosomes. Genotype is the allele combination an individual carries, phenotype is its observable trait, and gametes are sex cells, so none of these names the alternative forms.

**A636.** C. 50%
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-10 -->

Explanation: Hh × hh gives 1 Hh : 1 hh, so half the lambs are hh and polled. A 25% result applies to a cross between two heterozygotes, and 100% would require both parents to be polled.

**A637.** B. 3
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-11 -->

Explanation: The four squares of the Punnett square give RR, Rr, Rr and rr, which is three different genotypes. Four is the number of squares rather than genotypes, and 9 is the number of genotypes produced by a dihybrid cross.

**A638.** D. LL
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-12 -->

Explanation: An LL parent gives only Ll offspring, all long-rooted. An Ll parent would give about half round-rooted offspring, so 20 long-rooted offspring would be extremely unlikely.

**A639.** A. 25%
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-13 -->

Explanation: Ff × Ff gives 1 FF : 2 Ff : 1 ff, so 1 in 4 children is ff and affected. The 50% figure applies to a carrier crossed with an affected parent, and 75% is the chance that a child is unaffected.

**A640.** B. It produces two types, A and a, in a 1 : 1 ratio
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-14 -->

Explanation: Segregation places one allele into each gamete, so the A and a gametes form in equal numbers. An AA plant would produce only A gametes, and the 1 : 2 ratio confuses gametes with the genotypes of offspring.

**A641.** C. AA × aa
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-15 -->

Explanation: AA × aa gives only Aa offspring, so all (100%) show the dominant phenotype. Aa × Aa gives 75% dominant, Aa × aa gives 50%, and aa × aa gives none.

**A642.** D. It has two copies of the recessive allele
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-16 -->

Explanation: A recessive phenotype appears only when no dominant allele is present, so the genotype must be homozygous recessive. A heterozygote carries the recessive allele but shows the dominant phenotype, and a single dominant allele would be expressed, so neither can produce the recessive trait.

**A643.** C. 50%
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-17 -->

Explanation: Aa × aa gives 1 Aa : 1 aa, so half the children inherit the dominant allele and have achondroplasia. A 25% chance applies to two carriers of a recessive disorder, and 100% would require every child to inherit the dominant allele, which this cross cannot give.

**A644.** A. They separate, so each gamete receives one allele
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-18 -->

Explanation: During meiosis the two alleles separate into different gametes, so each gamete carries one allele. A gamete carrying both alleles would not be a normal haploid gamete, and the other options do not describe gamete formation.

**A645.** B. Both parents must be heterozygous (Bb)
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-19 -->

Explanation: A brown offspring (bb) must receive a b allele from each parent, so both black parents carry b and must be Bb. Homozygous dominant parents would produce only black offspring, and no mutation is needed to explain the result.

**A646.** D. It has two different alleles for the gene
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-20 -->

Explanation: A heterozygote carries two different alleles at the locus, one dominant and one recessive, and under complete dominance it shows the dominant phenotype. It is not homozygous, and it produces two types of gamete.

**A647.** C. 1
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-21 -->

Explanation: The four squares are DD, Dd, Dd and dd, so only one contains DD. Two squares contain Dd, which is the count of heterozygous squares, and three would match the count of dominant-phenotype squares rather than DD.

**A648.** D. A cross between individuals that differ in one characteristic controlled by a single gene
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-22 -->

Explanation: A monohybrid cross follows the inheritance of one characteristic controlled by one gene. Crosses involving two characteristics are dihybrid crosses, and a monohybrid cross does not require homozygous parents or any particular dominance pattern.

**A649.** The homozygous recessive partner contributes only recessive alleles, so each offspring's phenotype directly reflects the allele passed on by the unknown parent [1]; crossing with another dominant-phenotype individual can hide a recessive allele, because heterozygotes and homozygous dominant individuals look the same [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-23 -->

Explanation:

**A650.** 1 TT : 2 Tt : 1 tt [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-24 -->

Explanation:

**A651.** Probability that one child is aa = 1/4 [1]; both children aa = 1/4 × 1/4 = 1/16 (6.25%) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-25 -->

Explanation:

**A652.** (a) rr; produces only r gametes [1]; (b) RR gives all rough offspring (Rr) [1]; Rr gives about 1 rough : 1 smooth offspring [1]; (c) each smooth offspring must have received r from the unknown parent, since the smooth parent contributes only r [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-26 -->

Explanation:

**A653.** It has one copy of the recessive allele, which it can pass on to its offspring [1]; it shows the dominant phenotype because the dominant allele masks the recessive one [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-27 -->

Explanation:

**A654.** (a) 1/2 (two of four squares are Pp) [1]; (b) 3/4 (PP or Pp are unaffected) [1]; (c) each conception is an independent event, so earlier children do not change the chance for later ones [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-28 -->

Explanation:

**A655.** (a) Rr [1]; (b) 3 red : 1 white [1]; (c) homozygous for the trait, so it breeds true and produces offspring with the same phenotype for that trait when self-pollinated [1]; (d) the R allele is dominant, so the heterozygous Rr plants express red [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-29 -->

Explanation:

**A656.** Bb [1]; the white offspring (bb) must have received a b allele from the black parent, so that parent carries b, and as it shows black it must be heterozygous [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-mono-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Dihybrid crosses and linked genes

**A657.** A. 9 : 3 : 3 : 1
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-1 -->

Explanation: Two unlinked genes assort independently, so each heterozygous parent produces YR, Yr, yR and yr gametes in equal proportions. The 16-box Punnett square gives 9 yellow round : 3 yellow wrinkled : 3 green round : 1 green wrinkled. 1 : 1 : 1 : 1 is the ratio from a dihybrid test cross.

**A658.** C. AB, Ab, aB and ab in equal proportions
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-2 -->

Explanation: Each gamete gets one allele of each gene. With independent assortment, any A allele can go with any B allele, giving AB, Ab, aB and ab at 25% each. 'AB and ab only' is what you would see with complete linkage and no crossing over.

**A659.** B. The genes are linked, and the smaller classes are recombinants produced by crossing over
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-3 -->

Explanation: Unlinked genes would give about 1 : 1 : 1 : 1. Here the two parental combinations (GL and gl) are much more common than the two recombinant combinations (Gl and gL), which shows the genes are on the same chromosome. The recombinant classes exist, so crossing over between the loci must have happened in some cells.

**A660.** B. 17%
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-4 -->

Explanation: Recombination frequency = recombinant offspring ÷ total offspring × 100 = 170 ÷ 1000 × 100 = 17%. 83% is the proportion of parental types, not recombinants.

**A661.** C. show a higher recombination frequency, approaching that of unlinked genes
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-5 -->

Explanation: The further apart two loci are, the more likely a crossover will occur between them in prophase I, so more recombinant gametes are made. Genes very far apart can show close to 50% recombination, which looks like independent assortment. Close genes are the ones rarely separated.

**A662.** (a) BbRr produces BR, Br, bR and br gametes in equal proportions; bbrr produces only br gametes; (b) offspring 1 black rough (BbRr) : 1 black smooth (Bbrr) : 1 white rough (bbRr) : 1 white smooth (bbrr), i.e. 1 : 1 : 1 : 1.
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-6 -->

Explanation:

**A663.** Genes on different (non-homologous) chromosomes assort independently because the orientation of each homologous pair at metaphase I is random; this gives all allele combinations in gametes in equal proportions; linked genes are on the same chromosome and tend to be inherited together, so parental combinations of alleles are more common in gametes and new combinations only arise when crossing over occurs between the loci.
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-7 -->

Explanation:

**A664.** The 1 : 1 : 1 : 1 ratio is consistent with independent assortment, so the genes are probably unlinked; however genes very far apart on the same chromosome can also recombine at close to 50%, so the result alone does not prove they are on different chromosomes.
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-8 -->

Explanation:

**A665.** C. 1/16
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-9 -->

Explanation: The probability of aa from Aa × Aa is 1/4 and the probability of bb from Bb × Bb is also 1/4, so aabb = 1/4 × 1/4 = 1/16. 1/4 is the chance for only one gene, and 3/16 is the chance of a phenotype such as A_bb.

**A666.** A. 3/8
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-10 -->

Explanation: A_ from Aa × Aa is 3/4 and bb from Bb × bb is 1/2, so A_bb = 3/4 × 1/2 = 3/8. The answer 3/4 ignores the probability for gene B, and 1/8 wrongly multiplies 1/4 by 1/2.

**A667.** B. 9
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-11 -->

Explanation: Each gene gives three genotypes (AA, Aa, aa), so 3 × 3 = 9 distinct genotypes. The 16 is the number of boxes in the Punnett square rather than the number of distinct genotypes, and 4 is the number of gamete types from one parent.

**A668.** D. 60
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-12 -->

Explanation: Recombination frequency of 12% means 12% of 500 = 60 recombinant offspring across both recombinant classes combined. The answer 12 is the percentage rather than the count, 120 counts the recombinants twice, and 250 is the number expected for unlinked genes (50%).

**A669.** C. Prophase I
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-13 -->

Explanation: Crossing over occurs in prophase I while homologous chromosomes are paired and form chiasmata. Metaphase I is when homologous pairs line up at the equator, and interphase is when DNA replicates before meiosis begins.

**A670.** A. 40% AB, 40% ab, 10% Ab and 10% aB
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-14 -->

Explanation: The 80% parental gametes are split equally into AB and ab, and the 20% recombinant gametes are split equally into Ab and aB. The 25% option assumes unlinked genes, and the last option swaps the parental and recombinant classes.

**A671.** B. 75
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-15 -->

Explanation: Aa from Aa × aa is 1/2 and bb from Bb × bb is 1/2, so Aabb = 1/4 × 300 = 75. The answer 150 uses only one of the two probabilities, and 300 is the total number of offspring.

**A672.** D. 450
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-16 -->

Explanation: A_ is 3/4 and B_ is 3/4, so A_B_ = 9/16, and 9/16 × 800 = 450. The answer 200 is 1/4 of the offspring (the expected proportion for one recessive gene only), 400 is half, and 600 is the proportion for one dominant trait only.

**A673.** C. It exchanges segments between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes, producing chromosomes with new combinations of alleles
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-17 -->

Explanation: During prophase I, non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes exchange segments at chiasmata, so the chromosomes carry new allele combinations. The other options describe mitosis, the separation of homologues, and independent assortment, which are different processes.

**A674.** A. It contributes only recessive alleles, so each offspring's phenotype reveals the allele passed on by the unknown parent
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-18 -->

Explanation: The recessive parent gives only recessive alleles, so each offspring's phenotype directly shows which allele the unknown parent passed on. A test cross does not make offspring heterozygous, does not stop crossing over, and does not produce dominant phenotypes.

**A675.** B. 1/2
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-19 -->

Explanation: All offspring are Aa (AA × aa), and half are Bb from Bb × Bb, so AaBb = 1 × 1/2 = 1/2. The answer 1/4 comes from wrongly halving the Aa probability, and 1 ignores the probability for gene B.

**A676.** D. 1/2
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-20 -->

Explanation: A_ from Aa × aa is 1/2, and every offspring is B_ because the BB parent contributes a dominant allele, so A_B_ = 1/2 × 1 = 1/2. The answer 3/4 wrongly applies a 3 : 1 ratio to gene B.

**A677.** C. 8
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-21 -->

Explanation: Each heterozygous gene doubles the number of gamete types, so 2 × 2 × 2 = 8 (ABC, ABc, AbC, Abc, aBC, aBc, abC and abc). The answer 6 comes from adding rather than multiplying, 4 is the number of gamete types for only two genes, and 16 would come from four heterozygous genes.

**A678.** A. AaBb × aabb
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-22 -->

Explanation: A test cross of a heterozygote for two unlinked genes with a homozygous recessive gives four phenotypes in equal proportions. AaBb × AaBb gives 9 : 3 : 3 : 1, AABB × aabb gives only dominant offspring, and AaBB × AaBB gives a 3 : 1 ratio for gene A only.

**A679.** (a) Recombination frequency = 150 ÷ 1000 × 100 = 15% [1]; (b) the genes are linked because recombinant phenotypes make up only 15% (less than 50%), so the parental phenotypes are far more common [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-23 -->

Explanation:

**A680.** Crossing over occurs in prophase I [1]; homologous chromosomes pair and non-sister chromatids exchange equal segments at chiasmata [1]; the chromatids then carry new combinations of alleles, so the gametes formed from them are recombinant [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-24 -->

Explanation:

**A681.** The percentage of offspring (or gametes) that show recombinant phenotypes (or genotypes), calculated as recombinant ÷ total × 100 [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-25 -->

Explanation:

**A682.** (a) TtRr produces TR, Tr, tR and tr in equal proportions [1]; ttRr produces tR and tr in equal proportions [1]; (b) tall red : tall white : dwarf red : dwarf white = 3 : 1 : 3 : 1 [1]; (c) 1/2 × 1/4 = 1/8, so 1/8 × 320 = 40 tall white offspring [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-26 -->

Explanation:

**A683.** Independent assortment: random orientation of homologous pairs at metaphase I produces different combinations of maternal and paternal chromosomes in gametes [1]; crossing over: exchange of segments between non-sister chromatids in prophase I produces new combinations of alleles on the same chromosome [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-27 -->

Explanation:

**A684.** (a) 35% of gametes are AB (70% parental, split equally) [1]; (b) 30% × 500 = 150 recombinant offspring [1]; (c) ab gametes are 35%, so 35% × 500 = 175 ab/ab offspring [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-28 -->

Explanation:

**A685.** Probability of aa from Aa × Aa = 1/4 [1]; probability of B_ from Bb × Bb = 3/4, so aaB_ = 1/4 × 3/4 = 3/16 [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-29 -->

Explanation:

**A686.** ABC, ABc, AbC and Abc, each 25% [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-dihy-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Pedigree charts and modes of inheritance

**A687.** B. Autosomal recessive
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-1 -->

Explanation: Unaffected parents having an affected child means the trait is recessive (both parents are carriers). Because the affected child is a daughter with an unaffected father, it cannot be X-linked recessive: an affected daughter would need an affected father. So the trait is autosomal recessive.

**A688.** C. An affected female has an unaffected son
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-2 -->

Explanation: An X-linked recessive female (XᵃXᵃ) passes an Xᵃ to every son, and sons have only one X, so all her sons would be affected. An unaffected son of an affected mother rules it out. An affected father never passes his X to his sons, so an unaffected son is expected.

**A689.** C. 50%
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-3 -->

Explanation: Hh × hh gives 1 Hh : 1 hh, so each child has a 50% chance of inheriting the dominant allele and being affected.

**A690.** C. 1/2
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-4 -->

Explanation: The woman must have received Xᵇ from her colour-blind father, so she is XᴮXᵇ. A son receives his X from his mother, so he has a 1/2 chance of getting Xᵇ and being colour blind. The father's normal X goes only to daughters. 1/4 is the chance that any child (son or daughter) is colour blind.

**A691.** C. X-linked dominant
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-5 -->

Explanation: A trait seen in every generation, with affected children always having an affected parent, suggests dominance. An affected father gives his X to all daughters and his Y to all sons, so an X-linked dominant trait shows up in all his daughters and none of his sons. Y-linked traits pass from fathers to all sons instead.

**A692.** (a) Both parents are Ff; (b) Ff × Ff gives 1 FF : 2 Ff : 1 ff, so the probability of a carrier (Ff) child is 1/2; (c) the daughter is unaffected so she cannot be ff; of the remaining unaffected outcomes (1 FF : 2 Ff) two out of three are carriers, so her probability is 2/3.
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-6 -->

Explanation:

**A693.** Autosomal dominant traits usually appear in every generation and every affected person has at least one affected parent, whereas recessive traits can skip generations; two affected parents can have an unaffected child with a dominant trait, but two affected parents with a recessive trait (both homozygous recessive) will only have affected children.
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-7 -->

Explanation:

**A694.** Males have one X chromosome (XY), so a single recessive allele (XʰY) is enough to cause haemophilia; females have two X chromosomes and need two copies of the recessive allele (XʰXʰ), which is much less likely; a father passes his Y chromosome, not his X, to his sons, so sons get their X from their mother and cannot inherit the allele from their father.
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-8 -->

Explanation:

**A695.** A. An affected male
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-9 -->

Explanation: Squares represent males and circles represent females, and shading shows that the trait is expressed. A shaded square is therefore an affected male, whereas a shaded circle would be an affected female.

**A696.** B. 1/4
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-10 -->

Explanation: Dd × Dd gives 1 DD : 2 Dd : 1 dd, and only dd children are unaffected, so the probability is 1/4. The 3/4 is the probability of being affected, which is easy to confuse with the answer.

**A697.** C. 1/2
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-11 -->

Explanation: Aa × aa gives 1 Aa : 1 aa, so half the children are aa and affected. The 1/4 answer comes from the Aa × Aa cross, which does not apply because the mother is aa.

**A698.** D. 1/2
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-12 -->

Explanation: Sons inherit their X from their mother, who passes Xᴰ with probability 1/2, so half of sons are affected. The answer 1 wrongly assumes every son receives the dominant allele, and 0 would only apply if the mother had no dominant allele.

**A699.** A. Affected fathers pass the trait to all their sons and none of their daughters
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-13 -->

Explanation: The Y chromosome passes only from father to son, so every son of an affected father is affected and no daughter is. The half-of-sons pattern describes an autosomal dominant trait with a heterozygous father, which is a common mix-up.

**A700.** B. Both parents must be Dd
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-14 -->

Explanation: The unaffected child is dd, so each parent must have given a d allele. Both parents are affected, so each must also carry a D, which means both are Dd. A DD parent could not give a d allele, and a dd parent would be unaffected.

**A701.** C. Consanguineous mating, where the parents are related
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-15 -->

Explanation: A double line between partners shows consanguineous mating, meaning they are related, which increases the chance of a recessive trait. Twins are drawn as lines rising from a shared point, not as a double line between partners.

**A702.** A. 0
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-16 -->

Explanation: Daughters receive one X from their normal father, so they cannot receive two recessive alleles and cannot be affected. The 1/4 answer applies the autosomal Aa × Aa ratio, which does not apply to sex-linked genes.

**A703.** C. XᴬXᵃ
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-17 -->

Explanation: The affected son must have received Xᵃ from his mother, and an unaffected mother has a dominant Xᴬ, so she is XᴬXᵃ. XᵃXᵃ would be affected, XᴬXᴬ could not pass on Xᵃ, and XᵃY is male.

**A704.** D. X-linked recessive
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-18 -->

Explanation: An affected father passes his X to all daughters, who are unaffected carriers, and his Y to all sons, who are unaffected, which fits the male bias of X-linked recessive inheritance. X-linked dominant would show affected daughters, Y-linked would show affected sons, and autosomal dominant would give affected children of both sexes.

**A705.** C. 1/16
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-19 -->

Explanation: Each child has a 1/4 chance of being affected and the births are independent, so the chance that both are affected is 1/4 × 1/4 = 1/16. Using 1/4 alone ignores the second child, and 1/8 has no basis in this cross.

**A706.** D. 1
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-20 -->

Explanation: The daughter receives her father's Xʰ with certainty and her mother's Xᴴ, so she is a carrier. The 1/2 answer treats the daughter like a son, who inherits his X from the mother alone.

**A707.** B. Heterozygous
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-21 -->

Explanation: The affected child is aa and must receive an a allele from II-3, who is unaffected and so must be Aa. A homozygous dominant parent cannot pass on a, and a homozygous recessive parent would be affected, so the pedigree does determine the genotype.

**A708.** A carrier is a person who is heterozygous for the recessive allele, so is unaffected but can pass the allele to their offspring [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-22 -->

Explanation:

**A709.** The affected grandfather (XᵃY) passes Xᵃ to his daughter, who is an unaffected carrier (XᴬXᵃ) [1]; the dominant Xᴬ masks the recessive allele, so she does not show the trait and the generation is skipped [1]; her son receives Xᵃ with probability 1/2 and is affected, so the trait reappears in the next generation [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-23 -->

Explanation:

**A710.** Sons: probability 0, because sons receive Y from their father and Xᵈ from their unaffected mother [1]; daughters: probability 1, because every daughter receives Xᴰ from her affected father [1]; daughters are affected even though they are heterozygous (XᴰXᵈ), because the allele is dominant [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-24 -->

Explanation:

**A711.** (a) I-2 is Aa, because she must have given the recessive allele to the affected daughter II-2 [1]; II-1 is Aa, because he received a from his affected father and is unaffected [1]; (b) the probability is 1/4 (25%) [1], because Aa × Aa gives 1 AA : 2 Aa : 1 aa and only aa children are affected [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-25 -->

Explanation:

**A712.** She needs two copies of the recessive allele, so she must receive Xᵃ from her father as well as from her mother [1]; a father has only one X chromosome, so he must carry Xᵃ and be affected (XᵃY) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-26 -->

Explanation:

**A713.** Related parents are more likely to carry the same recessive allele, inherited from a common ancestor [1]; so the child is more likely to inherit two copies of that allele and show the condition [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-27 -->

Explanation:

**A714.** Yes, it rules out X-linked dominant inheritance [1]; an affected father with X-linked dominant inheritance passes the dominant allele to all his daughters, who would all be affected [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ped-28 -->

Explanation:

### Unit 2 AOS 2: Reproduction and adaptation

#### Asexual reproduction

**A715.** B. Strawberry plants producing new plants from runners
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-1 -->

Explanation: Runners (stolons) grow out from the parent strawberry plant and form new, genetically identical plants by mitosis. That is vegetative propagation. Seed production after pollination is sexual reproduction.

**A716.** B. budding
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-2 -->

Explanation: Budding produces a smaller outgrowth that gets a copy of the nucleus by mitosis and then separates. In binary fission (e.g. bacteria) a cell divides into two cells of roughly equal size. Fragmentation means a body breaks into pieces that each regrow.

**A717.** B. An unfertilised egg develops into a new individual
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-3 -->

Explanation: Parthenogenesis is when an unfertilised egg develops into an embryo. It happens in some insects, reptiles, fish and others. No sperm is involved, so stored sperm is not the explanation, and there is no father.

**A718.** B. A single new disease could infect and kill the whole crop because there is little genetic variation
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-4 -->

Explanation: Asexually propagated plants are clones. If one is susceptible to a pathogen, they all are, so the whole population is vulnerable (as seen with Panama disease in bananas). Asexual propagation usually gets plants to maturity faster, not slower.

**A719.** B. Asexual reproduction allows rapid population growth in a stable environment because every individual can produce offspring
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-5 -->

Explanation: Asexual reproduction needs only one parent, there is no need to find a mate, and every individual can reproduce. Populations can therefore grow quickly, which suits stable conditions. It uses mitosis (or binary fission), not meiosis and fertilisation, and gives little genetic diversity.

**A720.** The circular chromosome is replicated and the two copies attach to the plasma membrane; the cell elongates, separating the copies, and the membrane and cell wall pinch in across the middle to form two daughter cells; each daughter receives an identical copy of the replicated DNA, so they are genetically identical unless a mutation occurred during replication.
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-6 -->

Explanation:

**A721.** Advantages: no mate is needed so isolated individuals can reproduce; reproduction is fast and energy-efficient, allowing rapid population growth; (also accept: successful genotypes are passed on unchanged). Disadvantages: offspring have little genetic variation, so the population may not survive a change in the environment or a new disease; harmful mutations can build up in the lineage; offspring may compete closely with the parent for resources.
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-7 -->

Explanation:

**A722.** In spring and summer food is plentiful and conditions are stable, so asexual reproduction allows rapid population growth to exploit resources; in autumn conditions change and become harsher, so sexual reproduction produces genetically diverse offspring (and overwintering eggs), increasing the chance some will survive the changed conditions.
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-8 -->

Explanation:

**A723.** C. Mitosis of stem cells (neoblasts) that replace the missing tissues
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-9 -->

Explanation: Planarians have stem cells called neoblasts that divide by mitosis to replace missing tissues, producing genetically identical cells. Meiosis makes gametes, which do not regenerate body tissue, and fertilisation needs two gametes, so neither is involved. Binary fission is a reproductive process of single-celled organisms, not a way for specialised cells in a worm to divide.

**A724.** B. A rhizome
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-10 -->

Explanation: A rhizome is a horizontal underground stem with nodes that produce new shoots and roots, so each piece can grow into a genetically identical plant. A taproot is a root rather than a stem and does not normally produce new plants, while seeds and fruits are products of sexual reproduction.

**A725.** D. They are formed by mitosis, so they are genetically identical to the parent
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-11 -->

Explanation: Asexual spores of Rhizopus are produced by mitosis, so they are clones of the parent mycelium. Meiosis and fertilisation are sexual processes that produce variation, and binary fission is a method used by bacteria, not moulds.

**A726.** A. Sunflower seed formation after cross-pollination by bees
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-12 -->

Explanation: Sunflower seeds form when pollen fertilises an ovule, which involves the fusion of gametes from two sources, so this is sexual reproduction. Tubers, binary fission and root suckers each produce new individuals from one parent without the fusion of gametes.

**A727.** C. Sugarcane
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-13 -->

Explanation: Sugarcane is propagated from stem sections (setts) that each carry buds, and these grow into genetically identical plants. Wheat, maize and rice are normally grown from seed, which comes from sexual reproduction and is genetically varied.

**A728.** B. 64
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-14 -->

Explanation: Two hours is 120 minutes, which gives 120 ÷ 20 = 6 divisions. Each division doubles the cell number, so 2⁶ = 64 cells; answers of 6 or 12 wrongly treat the number of cells as increasing by a fixed amount, and 32 comes from miscounting the divisions as five.

**A729.** A. The mutation was in a cell whose mitotic descendants form only that branch, so the other parts of the tree did not inherit it
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-15 -->

Explanation: A mutation in a body cell is passed only to the cells that arise from that cell by mitosis, so only that branch shows it. Meiosis produces gametes rather than branches, and pollen cannot change the genes of the other branches, while the chromosome number is normally the same throughout the body.

**A730.** D. They are haploid, with half the chromosome number of the females
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-16 -->

Explanation: Drones develop from unfertilised eggs, so they have only one set of chromosomes and no father. Being diploid would require a fertilised egg, which is how workers develop, so the diploid option describes the wrong bee.

**A731.** C. Its own nucleus
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-17 -->

Explanation: The egg's own nucleus is removed so that the donor nucleus supplies all the nuclear DNA, while the egg's cytoplasm is kept to support development. The mitochondria and cell membrane are not removed in this process.

**A732.** A. The scion grows from cells produced by mitosis, so it is genetically identical to the donor cultivar, while the rootstock keeps its own genotype
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-18 -->

Explanation: Grafting is asexual: the scion is a clone of its donor because it grows by mitosis, and the rootstock keeps its own genotype. Seeds inside the apples are produced sexually and vary genetically, and no fertilisation occurs between scion and rootstock.

**A733.** B. It is genetically identical, because its cells are produced by mitosis from the parent's cells
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-19 -->

Explanation: The bud grows from repeated mitosis of the parent's body cells, so it is a clone of the parent. Meiosis and fertilisation are not involved, so the bud cannot be genetically different or contain genes from a second hydra.

**A734.** D. Spirogyra, a filamentous green alga
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-20 -->

Explanation: Spirogyra filaments break into pieces, and each piece grows into a new filament, so fragmentation is its asexual method. Escherichia coli divides by binary fission, yeast reproduces by budding, and wheat is propagated from seed.

**A735.** B. 14
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-21 -->

Explanation: Mitosis produces daughter cells with the same chromosome number as the parent cell, so each offspring cell keeps 14. Seven is the haploid number produced by meiosis in gametes, and 28 would require the chromosome number to double, as when two gametes fuse.

**A736.** A small piece of plant tissue (explant) is removed and placed on sterile nutrient medium containing plant hormones [1]; its cells divide by mitosis to form a mass of undifferentiated cells, which is then induced to form shoots and roots, producing plantlets [1]; the cells are produced by mitosis with no meiosis or fertilisation, so each plantlet has the same genotype as the parent plant [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-22 -->

Explanation:

**A737.** A group of genetically identical individuals produced asexually from one parent, by mitosis [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-23 -->

Explanation:

**A738.** Each eye contains a bud from which shoots grow, and the cells of the shoots are produced by mitosis from the parent's own cells [1]; no meiosis or fertilisation is involved, so no new combination of alleles is formed [1]; the new plants have the same genotype as the parent tuber, so they keep its desirable traits [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-24 -->

Explanation:

**A739.** Asexual reproduction involves no meiosis or fertilisation, so no new combinations of existing alleles are produced [1]; new alleles can arise only from mutations, which occur during DNA replication [1]; a beneficial mutation in one cell is passed to all of its clone offspring by mitosis [1]; if it improves survival or reproduction, natural selection causes the clone lineage carrying it to increase in frequency in the population [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-25 -->

Explanation:

**A740.** Buds form at the leaf margin, and their cells divide by mitosis to produce a small plant with roots and shoots [1]; the plantlet develops from the parent's own body cells, with no meiosis or fertilisation [1]; so it has the same genotype as the parent and is a clone [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-26 -->

Explanation:

**A741.** Gemmae are formed by mitosis in the parent plant, so no meiosis or fertilisation is involved [1]; each gemma grows into a new plant that is genetically identical to the single parent [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-27 -->

Explanation:

**A742.** Advantage: many genetically identical plants can be produced quickly from a small amount of tissue, keeping the desirable traits of the variety [1]; disadvantage: the plants have little genetic variation, so the whole group is at risk from a new disease or environmental change [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-28 -->

Explanation:

**A743.** Asexual reproduction in plants in which a new plant grows from a non-reproductive part of the parent, such as a stem, root or leaf [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-29 -->

Explanation:

**A744.** Cells in the fragment divide by mitosis and reorganise to form the body structures of a complete sponge [1]; the new sponge is genetically identical to the parent because no meiosis or fertilisation occurred [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-asex-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Sexual reproduction and genetic diversity

**A745.** A. Prophase I
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-1 -->

Explanation: Homologous chromosomes pair up (synapsis) in prophase I, and crossing over swaps segments between non-sister chromatids at chiasmata. Metaphase I is when independent assortment happens, as homologous pairs line up in random orientation.

**A746.** C. 16
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-2 -->

Explanation: The number of combinations is 2ⁿ, where n is the haploid number. Here n = 4, so 2⁴ = 16. Using 2n = 8 as the exponent would give 2⁸ = 256, which is wrong.

**A747.** B. Anaphase I of meiosis
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-3 -->

Explanation: In anaphase I the homologous chromosomes are pulled to opposite poles, which halves the chromosome number. In anaphase II and in mitotic anaphase, it is the sister chromatids that separate.

**A748.** B. 47 chromosomes (a trisomy)
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-4 -->

Explanation: A normal human gamete has 23 chromosomes. A gamete with 24 fused with a normal gamete gives 24 + 23 = 47 chromosomes, which is a trisomy (e.g. trisomy 21). A gamete with 22 chromosomes would give a monosomy with 45.

**A749.** A. Crossing over, independent assortment, random fertilisation
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-5 -->

Explanation: Crossing over (prophase I), independent assortment (metaphase I) and the random fusion of gametes at fertilisation all add genetic variation in sexual reproduction. Mitosis, binary fission and budding produce genetically identical cells.

**A750.** Crossing over: during prophase I homologous chromosomes pair and non-sister chromatids exchange segments at chiasmata; this produces recombinant chromatids with new combinations of alleles on the same chromosome. Independent assortment: during metaphase I each homologous pair lines up at the equator in a random orientation independent of other pairs; so maternal and paternal chromosomes are distributed to gametes in many different combinations (2ⁿ).
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-6 -->

Explanation:

**A751.** Genetic diversity means individuals carry different alleles, so some are more likely to have traits that let them survive a new selection pressure such as disease or climate change; these individuals survive and reproduce so the population persists; for example the Tasmanian devil has very low genetic diversity, which has made it highly vulnerable to devil facial tumour disease, which spreads between devils because their immune systems do not recognise the tumour cells as foreign.
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-7 -->

Explanation:

**A752.** In meiosis I homologous chromosomes separate, so the two cells produced are haploid (n) although each chromosome still has two chromatids; in meiosis II sister chromatids separate, like mitosis; this produces four haploid cells, each chromosome with a single chromatid, and the ploidy does not change in meiosis II.
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-8 -->

Explanation:

**A753.** A. 23 and 46
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-9 -->

Explanation: A human sperm is haploid with n = 23, and a body cell is diploid with 2n = 46. Reversing the two numbers is a common mix-up, and the option ending in 92 has no basis in the normal human life cycle.

**A754.** B. 6
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-10 -->

Explanation: Mitosis produces daughter cells with the same chromosome number as the parent cell, so each has 6 chromosomes. The answer 3 confuses mitosis with meiosis, and 12 wrongly assumes the chromosome number doubles.

**A755.** C. They are genetically identical to the parent, apart from any new mutations
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-11 -->

Explanation: Binary fission is asexual: one parent copies its DNA and divides, so the offspring are clones apart from rare mutations. Crossing over and fertilisation are features of sexual reproduction, so they do not apply to binary fission.

**A756.** D. 64
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-12 -->

Explanation: Each parent produces 2³ = 8 different gametes, so 8 × 8 = 64 different zygotes are possible. The answer 8 counts only one parent's gametes, and 16 comes from adding the two parents' numbers instead of multiplying them.

**A757.** A. One primary oocyte forms one functional ovum and polar bodies, while one primary spermatocyte forms four functional sperm
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-13 -->

Explanation: In oogenesis the cytoplasm divides unequally, so one large functional ovum forms and the polar bodies are small and degenerate. Spermatogenesis divides the cytoplasm equally to produce four functional sperm, so the claim that oogenesis yields four ova reverses the fact.

**A758.** B. Mutation
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-14 -->

Explanation: Mutations change the DNA sequence of a gene and so create new alleles. Crossing over, independent assortment and random fertilisation only reshuffle alleles that already exist into new combinations.

**A759.** A. A pathogen that infects one plant can spread to nearly all of them, destroying much of the crop
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-15 -->

Explanation: With almost no genetic variation, few or no plants are likely to carry resistance to a new pathogen, so one disease can spread through the whole plantation. The other options describe meiosis and fertilisation, which do not occur when a crop is propagated from suckers.

**A760.** D. 24, diploid
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-16 -->

Explanation: Fusion of two haploid gametes restores the diploid condition, so the zygote has 12 + 12 = 24 chromosomes and is diploid. The other options either halve the number instead of adding the two sets, or give the wrong ploidy.

**A761.** A. Two identical copies of one chromosome, joined at the centromere, formed by DNA replication
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-17 -->

Explanation: DNA replication in interphase copies each chromosome, and the two copies stay joined at the centromere until anaphase. Homologous chromosomes are a different pair, one from each parent, and the two cells formed at the end of meiosis I are daughter cells, not chromatids.

**A762.** B. The variety of alleles present among the individuals of the population
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-18 -->

Explanation: Genetic diversity is the range of alleles present across the individuals in a population, and a large population alone does not guarantee it. Every member of one species normally has the same chromosome number, so that option cannot describe diversity.

**A763.** C. Non-disjunction of the sex chromosomes during meiosis in one parent, producing a gamete with two sex chromosomes
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-19 -->

Explanation: Non-disjunction during meiosis in one parent produces a gamete with two sex chromosomes, which gives 47 chromosomes after fertilisation by a normal gamete. Crossing over does not occur in mitosis, and mitochondrial mutations do not change the number of nuclear chromosomes.

**A764.** B. 2
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-20 -->

Explanation: Meiosis halves the chromosome number, so each of the four haploid cells contains 2 chromosomes. The answer 4 is the diploid number of the parent cell, while 8 wrongly assumes the number doubles.

**A765.** C. 20
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-21 -->

Explanation: Oogenesis is a meiotic process, so polar bodies are haploid and contain 20 chromosomes. The answer 40 is the somatic number, while 10 wrongly halves the number twice.

**A766.** A haploid sperm nucleus fuses with a haploid ovum nucleus to form a diploid zygote [1]; this restores the diploid chromosome number that meiosis halved, so each generation keeps the same number [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-22 -->

Explanation:

**A767.** Meiosis II non-disjunction: sister chromatids of one chromosome fail to separate in anaphase II, so two of the four gametes are normal and two are abnormal, one with an extra chromosome and one missing a chromosome [1]; meiosis I non-disjunction: a homologous pair fails to separate in anaphase I, so all four gametes are abnormal, two with an extra chromosome and two missing one [1]; so meiosis II non-disjunction produces a mix of normal and abnormal gametes, whereas meiosis I non-disjunction makes every gamete abnormal [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-23 -->
