Explanation:

**A768.** Gametes are the cells that fuse to form a zygote, so a mutation in a gamete's DNA can be passed on to the offspring [1]; mutations in body (somatic) cells are not inherited by offspring, so their effects are limited to that individual [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-24 -->

Explanation:

**A769.** Mitosis has one division whereas meiosis has two divisions [1]; mitosis produces two daughter cells and meiosis produces four daughter cells [1]; mitosis daughter cells are diploid and meiosis daughter cells are haploid [1]; mitosis daughter cells are genetically identical to the parent cell, whereas meiosis daughter cells are genetically different from each other and from the parent [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-25 -->

Explanation:

**A770.** Any gamete from one parent can fuse with any gamete from the other, so each zygote combines a random gamete from each parent [1]; each gamete carries a random mix of maternal and paternal chromosomes from independent assortment, so each parent produces 2ⁿ different gametes [1]; the number of possible zygotes is therefore (2ⁿ) × (2ⁿ), a very large number of different combinations [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-26 -->

Explanation:

**A771.** Two sister chromatids, joined at the centromere [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-27 -->

Explanation:

**A772.** Females have two X chromosomes (XX) and males have one X and one Y chromosome (XY) [1]; each ovum carries an X chromosome, and the sex of the offspring depends on whether the sperm that fertilises it carries an X (female) or a Y (male) [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-sex-28 -->

Explanation:

#### Reproductive cloning

**A773.** B. an egg cell whose nucleus has been removed (enucleated)
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-1 -->

Explanation: In SCNT the donor nucleus goes into an enucleated egg cell. The egg is then stimulated (e.g. by an electric pulse) to start dividing. The nucleus has to be removed first so that the clone's nuclear DNA comes only from the donor.

**A774.** C. the Finn Dorset nucleus donor
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-2 -->

Explanation: The nucleus carries almost all the genes, so Dolly's nuclear DNA matched the Finn Dorset donor, and she looked like a Finn Dorset. Her mitochondrial DNA came from the egg donor's cytoplasm. The surrogate mother only provided the uterus.

**A775.** B. genetically identical to each other but not identical to either parent
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-3 -->

Explanation: The embryo started from fertilisation, so it has a new combination of alleles from both parents. The split embryos all come from that one zygote, so they are identical to each other (like identical twins) but not to either parent.

**A776.** B. Small pieces of tissue are grown on sterile nutrient media with plant hormones to form genetically identical plantlets
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-4 -->

Explanation: In tissue culture, explants (often meristem tissue) are grown on sterile media with nutrients and plant hormones such as auxins and cytokinins. The resulting plantlets come from mitosis, so they are clones of the parent plant. No pollination is involved, and meristem cells are diploid.

**A777.** B. Cloning adds no new genetic diversity to a population that already has little
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-5 -->

Explanation: Clones copy existing genotypes, so they do not raise the low genetic diversity that endangered populations usually suffer from. Cloned animals can generally reproduce, and not all clones have shortened lifespans.

**A778.** A somatic (body) cell is taken from the cow to be cloned and its nucleus extracted; an egg cell is taken from a donor cow and its nucleus removed (enucleated); the donor nucleus is inserted into (or the somatic cell fused with) the enucleated egg and an electric pulse or chemicals stimulate it to divide; the resulting embryo is grown in culture to an early embryo and implanted into the uterus of a surrogate cow, which gives birth to a calf genetically identical (in nuclear DNA) to the somatic cell donor.
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-6 -->

Explanation:

**A779.** Gene expression is affected by the environment and by random events in development (e.g. X-chromosome inactivation in female cats determines coat patches), so the same genotype can give a different phenotype; the clone also has mitochondrial DNA from the egg donor and develops in a different uterine environment from the donor.
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-7 -->

Explanation:

**A780.** Benefit: animals or plants with desirable traits (e.g. high milk yield, disease resistance) can be copied exactly and quickly; risk: herds or crops of clones have low genetic diversity, so a single disease or environmental change could devastate them (also accept low success rates and health problems in cloned animals, or ethical concerns about animal welfare).
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-8 -->

Explanation:

**A781.** C. the cytoplasm of the egg donor, which is not removed when the egg nucleus is removed
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-9 -->

Explanation: Mitochondria sit in the cytoplasm, not the nucleus, so they stay in the egg when its nucleus is removed. The clone's nuclear DNA matches the body cell donor, but its mitochondrial DNA does not. The surrogate and any sperm donor contribute no genetic material in SCNT.

**A782.** A. to activate the reconstructed egg so that it starts dividing as an embryo
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-10 -->

Explanation: The pulse or chemicals mimic fertilisation and trigger the cell to divide and form an embryo. SCNT uses no sperm, so fertilising the egg would give a mixed-parent embryo, and meiosis would halve the donor's chromosome number.

**A783.** D. the egg cytoplasm switches on genes that were silenced during differentiation, so the nucleus can direct development of a whole organism
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-11 -->

Explanation: A nucleus from a specialised cell has most of its genes switched off, and reprogramming switches the needed ones back on. Reprogramming changes which genes are active, not the DNA sequence itself, so the mutation option is wrong.

**A784.** B. embryo splitting produces offspring that are identical to each other, whereas SCNT produces offspring with the nuclear DNA of the body cell donor
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-12 -->

Explanation: Embryo splitting divides the cells of one early embryo, so the offspring match each other but not either parent. SCNT copies the nuclear DNA of a single adult body cell. The option saying both copy a parent is wrong, because embryo splitting begins with fertilisation.

**A785.** A. meristem tissue, because its cells divide actively and are often free of viruses
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-13 -->

Explanation: Meristem cells are actively dividing, undifferentiated cells, and the growing tip is often virus-free. Mature xylem cells are dead and cannot divide to form new plants. Plants grown from haploid pollen would not be clones of the parent.

**A786.** B. shoot formation
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-14 -->

Explanation: A high cytokinin to auxin ratio favours shoot growth, while a high auxin to cytokinin ratio favours root formation. Root formation is the result of the opposite ratio, which makes it a tempting mistake.

**A787.** D. the donor nucleus is not fully reprogrammed, so development is abnormal or stops early
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-15 -->

Explanation: Many transferred nuclei do not switch on the full set of genes needed for normal development, which is a main reason for the low success rate of SCNT. A nucleus is small enough to fit in the egg, and the enucleated egg supplies no chromosomes that could add faulty alleles.

**A788.** C. the clone's sex matches the nucleus donor, because the sex chromosomes are in the nucleus
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-16 -->

Explanation: Sex chromosomes are part of the nuclear DNA, so the clone is male, like the bull. The egg donor contributes only cytoplasm, and in mammals the sperm, not the egg, carries the sex chromosome that determines sex.

**A789.** C. 81
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-17 -->

Explanation: The number is multiplied by 3 each cycle, so 3 × 3 × 3 × 3 = 81. The answer 27 is the result after only three cycles, and 12 comes from 3 × 4, which ignores that the number multiplies each cycle.

**A790.** D. repeated subculturing can cause mutations, leading to variation among the plantlets
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-18 -->

Explanation: Repeated subculturing can introduce mutations, a problem known as somaclonal variation. Tissue culture needs no pollination or seeds and can produce many plantlets from a small piece of tissue, so the other options are wrong.

**A791.** B. vegetative reproduction, such as by runners or tubers, which gives genetically identical plants by mitosis
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-19 -->

Explanation: Runners and tubers produce new plants by mitosis from parts of the parent, just as tissue culture does, so the offspring are clones. Pollination, seed dispersal and meiosis all involve sexual reproduction or new gene combinations.

**A792.** A. 60
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-20 -->

Explanation: The donor nucleus supplies a full set of 60 chromosomes, and the enucleated egg contributes none. The answer 30 is the gamete number, and 120 wrongly adds two complete sets together.

**A793.** Early embryo cells are undifferentiated and totipotent, so each can develop into any cell type [1]; each separated group of cells can therefore develop into a complete embryo [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-21 -->

Explanation:

**A794.** Sterile conditions prevent contamination by bacteria or fungi [1]; contaminants would compete with the explant for nutrients or kill the plant tissue [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-22 -->

Explanation:

**A795.** Collect DNA samples (e.g. from tissue or blood) from the clone and the donor [1]; compare DNA profiles or markers, such as microsatellites or sequence data, at several loci [1]; matching results at all loci indicate identical nuclear DNA [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-23 -->

Explanation:

**A796.** Only the cells of an early, undifferentiated embryo can each form a whole organism, and the embryo has a limited number of cells [1]; splitting it into too many pieces leaves each piece with too few cells to develop successfully [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-24 -->

Explanation:

**A797.** Micropropagation uses small pieces of plant tissue (explants, often meristem), whereas SCNT uses a body cell nucleus placed in an enucleated egg [1]; micropropagation grows explants on sterile nutrient media with hormones, whereas SCNT embryos are stimulated to divide and then implanted into a surrogate uterus [1]; both produce offspring genetically identical to the source of the nuclear DNA [1]; micropropagation involves no egg or sperm, whereas SCNT uses an egg but no sperm [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-25 -->

Explanation:

**A798.** Many cloned embryos fail or die before or soon after birth, so many animals suffer [1]; surviving clones may have health problems such as abnormal organ development or shortened lifespan [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-26 -->

Explanation:

**A799.** Working: 12 ÷ 250 × 100 [1]; answer: 4.8% of transferred embryos produced a live calf [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-27 -->

Explanation:

**A800.** Clones are genetically identical in nuclear DNA, so the same mutation is copied into every clone [1]; if the mutation is harmful, all of the clones are affected, so the problem is repeated across the population [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-clone-28 -->

Explanation:

#### Structural, physiological and behavioural adaptations

**A801.** B. A bearded dragon basking in the sun in the morning to raise its body temperature
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-1 -->

Explanation: A behavioural adaptation is something an organism does. Basking is a behaviour that warms an ectotherm. Thick fur and sunken stomata are structural adaptations, and producing concentrated urine is a physiological one.

**A802.** C. It is a structural adaptation that reduces heat gain from the environment and water loss
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-2 -->

Explanation: Body shape is a structural feature. A smaller SA:V ratio means less surface relative to volume, so less heat is absorbed from a hot environment and less water is lost across the surface. It does not increase heat loss, and it is not a behaviour or an internal process.

**A803.** A. Stomata mainly on the upper surface of floating leaves - allows gas exchange with the air
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-3 -->

Explanation: Floating leaves like water lily pads have stomata on the upper surface, which is the side in contact with air, so gas exchange can happen there. Thick cuticles, deep roots and sunken stomata are xerophyte features for dry conditions.

**A804.** B. aestivation
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-4 -->

Explanation: Aestivation is dormancy during hot, dry conditions. Australian water-holding frogs, for example, burrow and aestivate underground. Hibernation is long-term dormancy during cold winter conditions.

**A805.** B. 2 only
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-5 -->

Explanation: CAM photosynthesis is a physiological (biochemical) adaptation: stomata open at night, when it is cooler, to take up CO₂ and reduce water loss. Spines and root systems are structural adaptations.

**A806.** Structural adaptations are physical features of the body, e.g. the large ears of the bilby, which lose heat; physiological adaptations are internal functions or processes, e.g. the spinifex hopping mouse producing highly concentrated urine to conserve water; behavioural adaptations are actions an organism carries out, e.g. kangaroos licking their forearms so evaporation cools the blood in surface vessels.
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A807.** A thick waxy cuticle on leaves is waterproof, so less water evaporates through the leaf surface; sunken stomata or stomata in hair-lined pits trap a layer of humid air, which reduces the water vapour concentration gradient and slows transpiration (also accept: reduced leaf surface area/needle-like leaves lower the area for water loss; vertically hanging leaves, as in eucalypts, reduce heat absorbed in the midday sun; rolled leaves enclose stomata).
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A808.** Longer ears have a larger surface area with many blood vessels, which increases heat loss to the environment by radiation and convection; in hot climates rabbits with longer ears are more likely to avoid overheating, survive and reproduce, so the trait becomes more common, while in cooler regions shorter ears reduce heat loss.
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A809.** B. A camel's body temperature rising by several degrees during the day rather than being held constant
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-9 -->

Explanation: A physiological adaptation is an internal regulated process. A camel letting its body temperature rise during the day, so it does not have to sweat to stay cool, is one. Sleeping and burrowing are behaviours, and leaf hairs are a structural feature.

**A810.** A. structural
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-10 -->

Explanation: Feathers are a physical feature of the body, so the adaptation is structural. A physiological adaptation is an internal process, such as changing blood flow to the skin, while fluffing the feathers up on purpose would be a behaviour.

**A811.** C. It is a behavioural adaptation that reduces the risk of predation for the group
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-11 -->

Explanation: Posting a sentry is something the animals do, so it is a behavioural adaptation, and its function is to warn the group and lower each animal's risk. The structural and physiological options mislabel the category, and the chemical warning option describes a mechanism the sentry does not use.

**A812.** D. The 4 cm cube has the smaller ratio, at 1.5 cm⁻¹, which is half the 3 cm⁻¹ of the 2 cm cube
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-12 -->

Explanation: The 2 cm cube has 24 cm² of surface and 8 cm³ of volume, a ratio of 3 cm⁻¹, while the 4 cm cube has 96 cm² and 64 cm³, a ratio of 1.5 cm⁻¹. A larger body has a smaller ratio, so the common mistake is to compare absolute surface areas instead of the ratio.

**A813.** B. It insulates the living cambium beneath, protecting it from heat during bushfires
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-13 -->

Explanation: Thick bark is a structural adaptation that insulates the living tissue beneath it, so a bushfire heats the cambium more slowly. Bark does not store water or absorb carbon dioxide, since photosynthesis happens mainly in the leaves, and hormone production is a physiological process rather than a function of bark.

**A814.** A. Convoluted nasal passages that cool exhaled air, so water vapour condenses and is returned to the body
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-14 -->

Explanation: Convoluted nasal passages cool exhaled air so that water vapour condenses on them and is recovered, which is a structural feature that reduces water loss. Panting, a high breathing rate and sweating all increase water loss, so they are wrong even though they help with cooling.

**A815.** C. They project above the mud and take in oxygen for the submerged roots
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-15 -->

Explanation: Pneumatophores are structural adaptations that project above the mud and take in air, supplying oxygen to the buried root tissues. Anchoring is the role of other root types, and salt removal is a physiological process rather than the function of these roots.

**A816.** D. behavioural
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-16 -->

Explanation: Moving to follow unpredictable rain is an action taken by the animal, so it is behavioural. The structural and physiological options describe body features or internal processes, and reproductive describes a different biological function.

**A817.** C. It is a structural adaptation that lets the bird crack and husk hard seeds
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-17 -->

Explanation: A short, thick beak is a physical feature, so it is structural, and its shape lets the bird crack and husk hard seeds. The digestive, diet-choice and water-storage options either mislabel the category or describe a function the beak does not have.

**A818.** A. Shivering, which generates heat through rapid muscle contractions
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-18 -->

Explanation: Shivering is an internal process that generates heat through muscle contraction, so it is physiological. Sheltering from the heat and migrating are behaviours, and a dense coat is a structural feature.

**A819.** B. Salt glands near the eyes that secrete a concentrated salt solution
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-19 -->

Explanation: Salt glands are a physiological adaptation: they actively excrete concentrated salt, which lets the bird drink seawater safely. Plumage is a structural feature and diet choice is behavioural, and neither removes salt from the body; storing seawater in the stomach would add to the salt load.

**A820.** D. a behavioural adaptation that reduces heat stress
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-20 -->

Explanation: Choosing when to forage is an action the animal carries out, so it is behavioural, and it reduces heat stress. The other options name the wrong category, or describe a feature that the timing of foraging does not change.

**A821.** C. physiological, because it is a regulated internal process
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-21 -->

Explanation: Closing stomata is a regulated internal response that reduces transpiration, which makes it physiological. Stomata are structures, so calling the response structural confuses the feature with the process it carries out.

**A822.** A. It is a physiological adaptation, because it is an internal metabolic process that conserves water
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-22 -->

Explanation: Excreting uric acid is an internal process that saves water, so it is physiological. Calling it structural confuses an organ with the process it carries out, which is the common mistake in this question.

**A823.** An action or behaviour carried out by an organism [1] that improves its chance of survival or reproduction, e.g. an echidna curling into a ball with its spines facing outwards when threatened [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-23 -->

Explanation:

**A824.** Structural [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-24 -->

Explanation:

**A825.** Pale colours reflect a greater proportion of incoming solar radiation [1]; less radiant energy is absorbed by the skin [1]; so less heat is gained from the environment, and less evaporative cooling and water loss is needed [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-25 -->

Explanation:

**A826.** Vasoconstriction: blood vessels in the skin narrow [1], reducing blood flow to the surface so less heat is lost to the air [1]; shivering: rapid, involuntary muscle contractions [1] release heat from the metabolism of the muscles [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-26 -->

Explanation:

**A827.** Water evaporates from the skin surface [1], and the energy needed to change it from liquid to vapour (latent heat) is taken from the skin and blood, cooling the body [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-27 -->

Explanation:

**A828.** Huddling with other individuals [1], which reduces the surface area exposed to cold air per animal and so reduces heat loss [1]; sheltering in a nest or burrow lined with insulating material [1], which traps warm air and reduces exposure to wind and cold [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-28 -->

Explanation:

**A829.** Larger leaves provide more surface area for light capture in low light [1]; thin leaves shorten the diffusion path for CO₂ to reach the chloroplasts [1]; exposed, dry habitats favour smaller, thicker leaves, which reduce the surface area for water loss [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-29 -->

Explanation:

**A830.** Chloride cells in the gills actively pump excess salt out of the body [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-adapt-30 -->

Explanation:

#### Adaptations to Australian environments

**A831.** B. seeds are released after fire, when competition is reduced and nutrients from ash are available
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-1 -->

Explanation: Serotiny holds seeds in woody fruits until fire triggers their release. After a fire the ground is clear, light is plentiful, competition is low and ash adds nutrients, so seedlings have a better chance of establishing. Serotiny does not stop the parent plant from burning.

**A832.** B. epicormic buds
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-2 -->

Explanation: Epicormic buds sit under the bark on trunks and branches and sprout after fire to regrow the canopy. Lignotubers are woody swellings at or below ground level that can also resprout, but they are not on the trunk.

**A833.** B. It produces very concentrated urine, obtains metabolic water from food and is nocturnal, staying in humid burrows during the day
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-3 -->

Explanation: The hopping mouse has long loops of Henle and makes extremely concentrated urine. It gets water from seeds and from cellular respiration (metabolic water). By being nocturnal and sheltering in humid burrows it cuts evaporative water loss. Sweating would waste water.

**A834.** A. cluster (proteoid) roots that increase surface area and release compounds that free phosphorus from the soil
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-4 -->

Explanation: Cluster (proteoid) roots are dense mats of fine rootlets. They have a large surface area and release carboxylates that make bound phosphate soluble, so the plant can take up more phosphorus from poor soils. Pneumatophores are a mangrove adaptation to waterlogged, low-oxygen mud.

**A835.** B. structural
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-5 -->

Explanation: The grooves between the scales are a physical body feature, which makes this a structural adaptation. (The lizard may also behave in ways that help, such as standing on damp sand, but the grooves themselves are structural.)

**A836.** Structural: a dense network of blood vessels close to the skin of the forearms, which loses heat when licked; physiological: produces concentrated urine and can slow or pause embryo development (embryonic diapause) when conditions are poor; behavioural: rests in shade during the hottest part of the day and is mostly active at dawn, dusk and night, and licks forearms so evaporation cools the blood.
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-6 -->

Explanation:

**A837.** Elastic tendons in the hind legs (e.g. the Achilles tendon) store energy on landing and release it in the next hop, so above a certain speed the energy cost of hopping barely rises with speed; this lets kangaroos travel long distances between scattered food and water sources with relatively little energy use.
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-7 -->

Explanation:

**A838.** Vertically hanging leaves present their edge to the midday sun, so less light and heat are absorbed in the hottest part of the day, lowering leaf temperature and transpiration; the thick waxy cuticle is waterproof and reduces evaporation of water from the leaf surface.
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-8 -->

Explanation:

**A839.** A. electroreceptors in its bill that detect the weak electric fields produced by prey
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-9 -->

Explanation: The platypus bill contains thousands of electroreceptors that detect the weak electrical signals produced by prey muscles. Echolocation is not used by platypuses, and a lateral line belongs to fish; the nostrils close underwater, so smell is not the main sense used there.

**A840.** C. secreting salt through salt glands on their leaves, where it crystallises and is shed
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-10 -->

Explanation: Grey mangroves have salt glands on their leaves that secrete excess salt, which then crystallises on the leaf surface and can be shed. Pneumatophores take in oxygen in waterlogged mud and do not remove salt, and the roots are not waterproof, so some salt is taken up.

**A841.** D. urinary bladder
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-11 -->

Explanation: The water-holding frog stores large volumes of water in its urinary bladder, which it draws on during dry periods. The gall bladder stores bile, and the lungs and stomach do not act as water stores.

**A842.** B. seedlings germinate only when there is enough water to grow, flower and set seed before the soil dries out
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-12 -->

Explanation: Germinating after heavy rain gives seedlings enough moisture to complete their short life cycle before drought returns. Germinating in the dry season would usually lead to death, and seed coats do not store water for the adult plant.

**A843.** C. warmer and less windy, which reduces heat loss and water loss
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-13 -->

Explanation: A dense, low cushion creates a warmer, more sheltered microclimate, reducing heat loss and drying out. Higher winds increase water loss and do not improve photosynthesis, and the cushion shape does not help disperse seeds further.

**A844.** D. shed rainwater quickly, so the leaf dries and fungi and algae cannot grow on it
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-14 -->

Explanation: Drip tips channel water off the leaf quickly, so leaves dry faster and epiphylls (fungi and algae) are less likely to grow. Leaves do not absorb water through their upper surface, and the tip does not change light absorption much.

**A845.** A. gulps air at the surface and takes up oxygen through a single lung
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-15 -->

Explanation: Queensland lungfish have a single lung-like organ and gulp air at the surface when dissolved oxygen falls. Burrowing into mud is a trait of African lungfish, not this species, and gills cannot extract oxygen from air.

**A846.** C. a behavioural adaptation for regulating body temperature
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-16 -->

Explanation: Basking is an action the lizard performs to raise its body temperature, so it is behavioural. Structural adaptations are physical body features, and basking does not conserve water.

**A847.** A. modified hairs
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-17 -->

Explanation: Echidna spines are modified hairs made of keratin that protect the animal from predators. Scales and feathers are different structures found in reptiles and birds, and the spines are not claws.

**A848.** D. convert nitrogen gas (N₂) from the air into ammonia that the plant can use
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-18 -->

Explanation: Rhizobia bacteria in root nodules fix atmospheric nitrogen into ammonia, which the plant uses to make proteins and other compounds. The bacteria do not dissolve phosphate, nitrate in roots is not a water store, and nitrogen gas is not used directly to make chlorophyll.

**A849.** B. avoid the high temperatures and dry conditions of the lowland plains, saving water and energy
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-19 -->

Explanation: Bogong moths rest in cool caves over summer, which avoids the heat and drought of the lowlands and saves energy and water. The moths are already adults when they migrate, so the caves do not promote growth.

**A850.** A. three times greater
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-20 -->

Explanation: For similar shapes, surface area scales with length squared and volume with length cubed, so the ratio falls as length increases; here it is 15 ÷ 5 = 3 times greater for the small rodent. Squaring the length ratio (nine times) or cubing it (27 times) gives the wrong answer.

**A851.** Structural: a very long caecum (around 2 m) that houses microbes which ferment cellulose and help break down toxic compounds [1]; behavioural: sleeps for around 18 hours or more each day, which reduces energy use on a low-energy diet [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-21 -->

Explanation:

**A852.** (i) behavioural: an action the emu performs to find food and water [1]; (ii) structural: a physical feature of the body covering [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-22 -->

Explanation:

**A853.** Gliding lets it cross between widely spaced trees with much less energy than travelling along the ground [1]; this allows it to reach scattered feeding sites, such as flowering eucalypts and trees with sap or insects, more quickly [1]; it avoids ground predators during travel, so more time and energy go into finding food [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-23 -->

Explanation:

**A854.** The mother can bask and move between sunny and shaded sites to keep herself and her developing embryos warmer than the cold surroundings [1]; the young develop inside the mother, so they are protected from frost and cold, and are born early enough in the season to feed and grow before winter [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-24 -->

Explanation:

**A855.** An animal whose body temperature depends mainly on external sources of heat, such as the sun, rather than on heat produced by its own metabolism [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-25 -->

Explanation:

**A856.** Structural: tough, armoured skin of bony plates (scutes) that reduces water loss and protects against injury [1]; behavioural: basks on riverbanks in the morning to warm up before hunting [1]; behavioural: builds mound nests from vegetation whose decomposition generates heat for the eggs [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-26 -->

Explanation:

**A857.** Fleshy leaves store water and have a thick cuticle that reduces water loss [1]; a low, trailing growth habit reduces exposure to wind and heat [1]; stems root at the nodes, so the plant anchors itself in shifting sand [1]; it spreads vegetatively along the ground, allowing it to colonise and hold dune sand after disturbance [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-27 -->

Explanation:

**A858.** The frog sheds layers of skin that harden into a cocoon around its body while it sits in mud [1]; the cocoon reduces evaporative water loss from the skin, so the water stored in its body lasts longer [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-28 -->

Explanation:

**A859.** C. structural
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-29 -->

Explanation: The hairs and the silvery surface are physical features of the leaf, so they are structural. The reflected light and trapped humid air reduce heat gain and water loss. Physiological adaptations are internal processes such as producing concentrated urine, and behavioural adaptations are actions such as sheltering.

**A860.** A. lose body heat from the blood vessels in the ears, helping the bilby regulate its body temperature
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-30 -->

Explanation: Large, well-vascularised ears act as radiators, releasing heat when the bilby is hot. Hearing is a real function of the ears, but the blood vessels point to heat exchange. Blood vessels in the ears do not store water, and display is not the main role of these ears.

**A861.** Torpor lowers the animal's body temperature and metabolic rate, so it uses much less energy [1]; this allows it to survive on stored body fat and limited food through the cold winter months when food is scarce [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-aus-31 -->

Explanation:

#### Interdependencies and keystone species

**A862.** B. has a disproportionately large effect on ecosystem structure relative to its abundance
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-1 -->

Explanation: A keystone species has an effect on community structure that is far larger than its numbers would suggest. Removing it causes big changes. Keystone species are often predators but do not have to be: ecosystem engineers and pollinators can also be keystone species.

**A863.** C. mutualism
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-2 -->

Explanation: Both species benefit (+/+), so this is mutualism. In commensalism one species benefits and the other is unaffected (+/0). In parasitism one benefits at the other's expense (+/−).

**A864.** A. a trophic cascade
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-3 -->

Explanation: A trophic cascade happens when a change at one trophic level (fewer predators) spreads through the food web: urchins increase and graze down the kelp. Biomagnification is the build-up of toxins up a food chain, which is a different process.

**A865.** C. parasitism
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-4 -->

Explanation: Mistletoe takes water and minerals through haustoria while the host gets no benefit, so it is parasitism (+/−). Mistletoe is a hemiparasite because it still photosynthesises, but its relationship with the host is still parasitic. Its berries and nectar do support many birds and insects.

**A866.** B. Dingoes suppress fox and cat numbers, reducing predation pressure on small native mammals
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-5 -->

Explanation: Dingoes kill and scare away foxes and cats (mesopredators). When dingoes are removed, mesopredators are released, they increase and they prey more heavily on small native mammals. This is mesopredator release, a form of trophic cascade.

**A867.** Intraspecific competition is competition for limited resources between individuals of the same species, e.g. male red kangaroos competing for mates, or eucalypt seedlings of one species competing for light; interspecific competition is competition between individuals of different species for the same resource, e.g. introduced European honeybees and native bees competing for nectar and tree hollows, or rabbits and native herbivores competing for grasses.
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-6 -->

Explanation:

**A868.** Flying foxes pollinate the flowers of many native trees (e.g. eucalypts) as they feed on nectar; they disperse seeds over long distances, often tens of kilometres, which helps regenerate forests and maintain gene flow between fragmented patches; because many plant species depend on them and, in turn, many other animals depend on those plants, losing flying foxes would have a disproportionately large effect on forest structure.
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-7 -->

Explanation:

**A869.** The skink population would decrease; grasshoppers are the skinks' food source, so less food would lead to lower survival and reproduction of skinks (and they may face greater predation pressure as kookaburras have less alternative prey).
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-8 -->

Explanation:

**A870.** B. commensalism
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-9 -->

Explanation: The barnacles benefit while the whale is unaffected, which is commensalism (+/0). Mutualism would require the whale to benefit too, and parasitism would require the whale to be harmed.

**A871.** C. amensalism
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-10 -->

Explanation: One species is harmed while the other is unaffected, which is amensalism (−/0). Commensalism is the reverse pattern, where one species benefits and the other is unaffected (+/0).

**A872.** D. mutualism
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-11 -->

Explanation: Both partners benefit (+/+), which is mutualism. Parasitism would mean one partner benefits at the other's expense, and commensalism would mean only one partner benefits.

**A873.** A. parasitism
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-12 -->

Explanation: The cuckoo benefits at the thornbill's expense (+/−), which is brood parasitism, a form of parasitism. Competition would involve both species using the same limited resource, which is not the main pattern here.

**A874.** B. predation
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-13 -->

Explanation: The owl kills and eats the possum, so the owl benefits and the possum is harmed (+/−), which is predation. Parasitism involves a parasite living on or in a host that is usually not killed quickly.

**A875.** C. The presence or absence of an indicator species reflects environmental conditions, while a keystone species has a large effect on community structure relative to its abundance
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-14 -->

Explanation: An indicator species shows the condition of an environment, such as lichens reflecting air quality, but removing it does not usually reshape the community. The other statements wrongly link these terms to abundance, feeding role or range.

**A876.** D. a wombat whose burrows provide shelter for many other species
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-15 -->

Explanation: Ecosystem engineers physically change the habitat, and the wombat's burrows create shelter used by many other species. Feeding on leaves or hunting does not change the habitat, and abundance alone does not make a species an ecosystem engineer.

**A877.** A. a fungus breaking down a fallen eucalypt log
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-16 -->

Explanation: Decomposers such as fungi and bacteria break down dead organic matter and return nutrients to the soil. The turtle, koala and dingo all eat living organisms, so they are consumers.

**A878.** C. cannot coexist indefinitely in the same place, and one is eventually eliminated
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-17 -->

Explanation: Two species using exactly the same resources compete directly, so the less competitive species is eventually eliminated. Niche partitioning, which evolves over time, is how species avoid exclusion, so it is not the prediction of the principle.

**A879.** D. reduced competition through resource partitioning
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-18 -->

Explanation: Feeding in different parts of the trees reduces the overlap in the resource used, which is resource partitioning, and this lowers competition. Extinction is not described, the birds are not benefiting each other, and no predator is changing lower trophic levels.

**A880.** A. 200 kJ
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-19 -->

Explanation: About 10% is passed on at each level, so 20 000 kJ becomes 2 000 kJ for the grasshoppers and then 200 kJ for the lizards. The value of 2 000 kJ is only one transfer, and 20 kJ wrongly applies a third 10% transfer.

**A881.** B. primary consumer
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-20 -->

Explanation: A primary consumer eats producers, so a bird that feeds on plant nectar and pollen is a primary consumer. Producers make their own food, secondary consumers eat other animals, and decomposers break down dead matter.

**A882.** C. one species benefits and the other is unaffected
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-21 -->

Explanation: The + shows that one species gains a benefit and the 0 shows that the other is unaffected, which is commensalism. A (+/−) pattern means one species benefits while the other is harmed, and a (+/+) pattern means both benefit.

**A883.** The position an organism occupies in a food chain or food web, based on its feeding relationship and source of energy [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-22 -->

Explanation:

**A884.** At each trophic level about 90% of the energy is lost, mainly as heat from respiration, and so is not passed on to the next level [1]; by the top level too little energy remains to support a viable population of consumers [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-23 -->

Explanation:

**A885.** The fungal hyphae extend the tree's root system, increasing the absorption of water and mineral nutrients such as phosphorus for the tree [1]; in return the tree supplies the fungus with sugars produced by photosynthesis [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-24 -->

Explanation:

**A886.** A keystone species has a disproportionately large effect on community structure relative to its abundance, so a common species is only a keystone if its removal causes major changes to the community [1]; many abundant species can be replaced by other species, so their loss would have only small effects on other organisms [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-25 -->

Explanation:

**A887.** In predation, the predator kills and eats its prey, so the prey is harmed immediately [1]; in parasitism, the parasite lives on or in the host and gains nutrients from it while usually not killing it quickly [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-26 -->

Explanation:

**A888.** Quolls that eat cane toads are poisoned and die, so the quoll population declines [1]; with fewer quolls, predation pressure on the quoll's prey, such as insects and small vertebrates, is reduced, so prey numbers are likely to increase [1]; changes in the prey population may then affect other species that depend on the prey or compete with it [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-27 -->

Explanation:

**A889.** Termites physically change the environment by moving soil and building mounds and tunnels, which alters soil structure, drainage and the distribution of nutrients [1]; these structures create shelter and habitat for other invertebrates and small animals [1]; if termites were removed, these species would lose their shelter and could decline [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-28 -->

Explanation:

**A890.** Water fleas receive 10% of 60 000 kJ = 6000 kJ [1]; small fish receive 10% of 6000 kJ = 600 kJ [1]; kingfisher receives 10% of 600 kJ = 60 kJ [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-29 -->

Explanation:

**A891.** Dugongs and green turtles lose their main food source, so their survival and reproduction decline [1]; juvenile fish and prawns lose their shelter and nursery habitat, so their numbers fall [1]; larger predators of these fish and prawns lose prey and may also decline [1]; reduced seagrass means less sediment is trapped and stabilised, increasing turbidity and harming other organisms such as filter feeders and corals [1]
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-30 -->

Explanation:

**A892.** D. mutualism, because the wasp and the fig both benefit and depend on each other to reproduce
<!-- id: bio12a3-inter-31 -->

Explanation: The fig provides a brood site and the wasp pollinates the fig, so both partners benefit (+/+), which is mutualism. Parasitism and predation would each harm one partner, and commensalism would leave the fig unaffected.

#### Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander ecological knowledge

**A893.** B. small, cool, patchy fires lit at particular times of year, creating a mosaic of burnt and unburnt areas
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-1 -->

Explanation: Cultural burning uses low-intensity, cool, patchy fires, often in the cooler months or early dry season, guided by Country, plants and animals. The resulting mosaic cuts fuel loads, protects fire-sensitive species and supports regrowth of food plants.

**A894.** B. It reduces fuel loads and provides refuges and a range of habitat ages for different species
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-2 -->

Explanation: Patches of different ages break up continuous fuel, so the risk of large, intense wildfires goes down. They also give animals unburnt refuges to shelter in and fresh regrowth to feed on, which supports biodiversity.

**A895.** B. detailed observation of local indicators such as weather, flowering of plants and animal behaviour
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-3 -->

Explanation: These calendars come from long-term observation of local environmental cues, such as winds, rain, plants flowering and animals breeding or migrating. They often recognise more than four seasons, and the cues signal when to hunt, gather or burn.

**A896.** B. used detailed knowledge of the short-finned eel's life cycle and seasonal movements to build sustainable aquaculture systems
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-4 -->

Explanation: The Budj Bim system of channels, weirs and ponds was built to trap and hold short-finned eels (kooyang) during their seasonal migrations. It is among the world's oldest aquaculture systems and reflects detailed ecological knowledge. Budj Bim is World Heritage listed.

**A897.** C. Working with Traditional Owners combines long-term, place-based knowledge with scientific methods, for example in ranger programs and fire management
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-5 -->

Explanation: Traditional knowledge is built from observing particular places over thousands of years. Combined with scientific monitoring in partnership with Traditional Owners (for example Indigenous ranger programs and savanna burning), it improves land and sea management. Using this knowledge needs consultation and respect for cultural and intellectual property.

**A898.** Cool, low-intensity burns at the right time remove understorey fuel such as dry grass and leaf litter, so later fires have less fuel and are less intense; burning in small patches creates a mosaic of different-aged vegetation that acts as firebreaks and gives animals refuges; regrowth after burning provides fresh food for herbivores and promotes germination of fire-adapted plants, maintaining a diversity of habitats and species.
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-6 -->

Explanation:

**A899.** Example: in parts of south-eastern Australia the flowering of certain wattles signals that fish such as mullet are running or that eels are moving (accept other valid examples, e.g. flowering of a plant indicating turtles or dugong are fat and ready to hunt); such indicators are reliable because both species respond to the same seasonal environmental cues (e.g. temperature, day length, rainfall), so their life cycle events happen at consistent times relative to each other.
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-7 -->

Explanation:

**A900.** Knowledge of breeding seasons, feeding grounds and population changes guides when and where hunting occurs, avoiding times when animals are breeding; community-based management plans and cultural rules limit how many animals are taken and which individuals (e.g. avoiding pregnant females or young), helping keep populations stable.
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-8 -->

Explanation:

**A901.** A. knowledge built over many generations through close observation of particular places and passed on through language, stories and practice
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-9 -->

Explanation: Traditional Ecological Knowledge is cumulative and place-based, built up over many generations and transmitted through language, stories and practice. The idea that it is purely spiritual ignores its detailed, practical information about species and seasons, and it is not a fixed list recorded by early explorers.

**A902.** B. reduce the fuel that would otherwise feed larger, hotter late-season wildfires and their greenhouse gas emissions
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-10 -->

Explanation: Early dry season fires are cooler and patchy, so they leave less fuel for the hotter, larger wildfires that tend to occur late in the season. The idea that the hottest fires clear ground best is a common mistake, because intense fire damages habitat and is not the goal of cultural burning.

**A903.** C. remove water-soluble toxins before the seed was eaten
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-11 -->

Explanation: The toxins in cycad seeds dissolve in water, so leaching removes them and makes the seed safe to eat. Fermentation and softening can be part of other food preparation, but neither removes the toxins, which is why they are not the purpose here.

**A904.** D. land or sea country that Traditional Owners have voluntarily dedicated for conservation, managed under their own plans and knowledge
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-12 -->

Explanation: Indigenous Protected Areas are voluntarily dedicated by Indigenous landowners and managed for conservation and culture, often in partnership with governments. The national park option is wrong because these areas actively include cultural land management such as cultural burning.

**A905.** A. the remaining seed allows the plant population to regenerate and still feeds wildlife
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-13 -->

Explanation: Leaving seed lets plants regenerate and provides food for birds and other animals. The claims that plants cannot be harmed, or that damage boosts seed production, are not sound grounds for sustainable harvest.

**A906.** B. obligations to care for those species, which can limit when and how much of them is taken
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-14 -->

Explanation: Kinship and totem relationships can carry responsibilities to care for and protect particular species, so people may limit when and how much they take. The idea that species are bought and sold describes a commercial system that is not part of traditional management.

**A907.** C. replanting parts of the tuber and returning later, so the patch can supply food in future seasons
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-15 -->

Explanation: Tending a patch by replanting and returning to it increases future harvests and keeps the food supply sustainable. Clearing a patch completely undermines future yield, and importing non-native crops is not part of traditional management.

**A908.** D. heat and smoke can trigger germination or resprouting, and reduced competition gives these species room to grow
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-16 -->

Explanation: Smoke and heat can break seed dormancy or stimulate resprouting in many fire-adapted species, and reduced competition helps seedlings establish. The claims that fire kills all seeds or changes soil acidity are common misconceptions and do not describe how these species respond.

**A909.** B. hot fire can kill fire-sensitive rainforest plants and the animals that depend on them
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-17 -->

Explanation: Rainforest edges contain many fire-sensitive species, so a hot fire can destroy them and the animals that rely on them. The idea that rainforest is fire-resistant is a common mistake, because its margins are often damaged by fire.

**A910.** B. these birds must drink daily, so they fly to water in the early morning and late afternoon along predictable paths
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-18 -->

Explanation: Many seed-eating birds need to drink every day, so their flights to water at dawn and dusk show the direction and distance to water. The claims about storing water in crops, or nesting only near water, are not supported as reasons for this method.

**A911.** C. watching the foraging bees and following their flight lines back to the hollow
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-19 -->

Explanation: Foraging worker bees fly in fairly direct lines between flowers and their nest, so following their flight paths can lead to the hollow. Cutting into trees harms the colony, and the other options are guesses that do not track the bees.

**A912.** A. whether the edges of the print are sharp or crumbled, and whether wind, rain or dew has altered it
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-20 -->

Explanation: Sharp, unweathered edges and an undisturbed surface suggest a recent track, while wind, rain or dew soften and fill it in over time. Stride length mainly indicates the animal's size and gait, not its age, and the number of tracks says nothing about when a single track was made.

**A913.** A. the knowledge belongs to the people who hold it, and some may be culturally restricted or sacred
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-21 -->

Explanation: Traditional Owners hold cultural and intellectual property rights over their knowledge, and some of it may be restricted by cultural law. The idea that knowledge is protected only when written down is a misconception, since much traditional knowledge is oral and still belongs to its holders.

**A914.** D. the lowest tides expose intertidal shellfish that are otherwise underwater and hard to reach
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-22 -->

Explanation: Low tides expose intertidal shellfish, making them accessible and reducing the effort needed to collect them. The claims about high tides pushing shellfish into shallow water, or about breeding timing, are misconceptions about how shellfish behave.

**A915.** The ongoing responsibility of Traditional Owners to look after land, sea, plants, animals and cultural places, using their knowledge, law and practices [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-23 -->

Explanation:

**A916.** It is based on long-term, direct observation of changes in species, seasons and water that short-term records would miss [1]; it captures local species interactions and environmental cues, so management can be tailored to that specific area [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-24 -->

Explanation:

**A917.** Consult the Traditional Owners and use their knowledge of timing, weather and seasonal indicators to choose when to burn [1]; assess fuel loads, wind and humidity, prepare firebreaks, then burn cool and in small patches [1]; monitor the results and plan a mosaic of burns of different ages over time to maintain habitat diversity [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-25 -->

Explanation:

**A918.** Sea Country includes the reefs, waters and animals that are part of people's culture and identity, so people hold detailed local knowledge of changes [1]; involving them gives management a basis in their rights, responsibilities and long-term care, which supports compliance and continuity [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-26 -->

Explanation:

**A919.** Early dry season burns are cooler and patchy, so they reduce the fuel available for later fires [1]; this reduces the area burnt by late-season wildfires, which are hotter and more extensive and release more greenhouse gases in total [1]; savanna burning projects led by Traditional Owners can earn Australian Carbon Credit Units under the Emissions Reduction Fund for emissions avoided [1]; patchy burning also protects large trees and other vegetation that store carbon, so less carbon is lost [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-27 -->

Explanation:

**A920.** C. underground water flows beneath the dry creek bed, close enough to the surface to reach by digging
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-28 -->

Explanation: Water moves through the sand and gravel beneath dry creek beds, so digging reaches it where it lies near the surface. Dew and rain stored in sand are not reliable sources, and floodwater does not stay in a hollow for long.

**A921.** A fire moving into the wind spreads slowly with a low flame height, so it burns at lower intensity and is easier to control [1]; the cooler fire consumes less of the canopy and understorey, leaving unburnt patches and reducing the risk of large, hot wildfires later [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-29 -->

Explanation:

**A922.** The stone rows (weirs) funnelled fish into pools and enclosures as water levels fell, so fish could be caught when they were concentrated [1]; openings in the traps let smaller fish and many others pass upstream, which supports continued breeding and a sustainable catch [1].
<!-- id: bio12a3-ik-30 -->

Explanation:

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A923.** Glucose: membranes, insulin and diabetes
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** The phospholipid bilayer has a hydrophobic (non-polar) interior. Glucose is a large, polar molecule, so it cannot pass through the bilayer and needs a carrier protein. Oxygen is small and non-polar, so it diffuses straight through the bilayer. *(2 marks)*

1. Glucose is large and polar, so it cannot cross the hydrophobic interior of the phospholipid bilayer and needs a carrier protein. *(1 mark)*
2. Oxygen is a small, non-polar molecule, so it can diffuse directly through the bilayer. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying glucose is 'too big for the pores'. The key reason is that the inside of the bilayer is hydrophobic and glucose is polar.

Topic: Plasma membrane structure (vce-bio12-plasma-membrane-structure)

**b.** It moves glucose down its concentration gradient, from high to low concentration, so it is passive. *(1 mark)*

1. Facilitated diffusion moves molecules down their concentration gradient, so no energy from ATP is needed. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Confusing it with active transport. Active transport moves substances against their concentration gradient and uses ATP.

Topic: Passive transport across membranes (vce-bio12-passive-transport-across-membranes)

**c.** Stimulus: blood glucose rises. Beta cells in the pancreas detect the rise and secrete insulin. Insulin causes body cells (such as liver and muscle cells) to take up glucose, and the liver converts it to glycogen, so blood glucose falls back to normal. *(2 marks)*

1. The rise in blood glucose (stimulus) is detected by beta cells in the pancreas, which respond by secreting insulin into the blood. *(1 mark)*
2. Insulin makes target cells such as liver and muscle cells take up glucose, and the liver stores it as glycogen, so blood glucose falls. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming glucagon. Glucagon is released when blood glucose is low and raises it.

Topic: Blood glucose regulation (vce-bio12-blood-glucose-regulation)

**d.** The response (a fall in blood glucose) counteracts the original stimulus (a rise in blood glucose), bringing the level back towards normal. *(1 mark)*

1. The response reverses the change that triggered it: high blood glucose leads to a response that lowers blood glucose. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying it is negative because something decreases. Negative feedback means the response opposes the original change, whichever direction it was.

Topic: Stimulus-response model and negative feedback (vce-bio12-stimulus-response-model-and-negative-feedback)

**e.** With no beta cells, little or no insulin is produced. Without insulin, body cells do not take up glucose and the liver does not convert it to glycogen, so glucose stays in the blood. *(2 marks)*

1. Destroyed beta cells mean little or no insulin is secreted. *(1 mark)*
2. Without insulin, cells do not take up glucose from the blood and the liver does not store it as glycogen, so blood glucose remains high. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing type 2 diabetes. In type 2, insulin is produced but cells respond poorly to it; in type 1, insulin is not produced.

Topic: Malfunctions of homeostasis (vce-bio12-malfunctions-of-homeostasis)

**f.** Ribosomes (on the rough endoplasmic reticulum) make insulin. It is packaged by the Golgi apparatus into vesicles, which fuse with the plasma membrane and release it by exocytosis. *(2 marks)*

1. Ribosomes, attached to the rough endoplasmic reticulum, synthesise insulin. *(1 mark)*
2. Vesicles from the Golgi apparatus carry it to the plasma membrane and release it by exocytosis. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying insulin leaves by diffusion. Proteins are too large; they are secreted in vesicles by exocytosis.

Topic: Active transport, endocytosis and exocytosis (vce-bio12-active-transport-endocytosis-and-exocytosis)

**A924.** Coat colour in guinea pigs
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** bb *(1 mark)*

1. White is the recessive phenotype, so it only appears when both alleles are recessive: bb. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing Bb. A guinea pig with even one B allele is black.

Topic: Genomes, genes and alleles (vce-bio12-genomes-genes-and-alleles)

**b.** 3 black : 1 white *(2 marks)*

1. Each parent is Bb and makes B and b gametes. The Punnett square gives BB, Bb, Bb and bb in the ratio 1 : 2 : 1. *(1 mark)*
2. BB and Bb are black and bb is white, so the expected phenotype ratio is 3 black : 1 white. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving the genotype ratio (1 : 2 : 1) when the question asks for phenotypes.

Topic: Monohybrid crosses and test crosses (vce-bio12-monohybrid-crosses-and-test-crosses)

**c.** Cross it with a white (bb) guinea pig. If any offspring are white, the black parent is Bb (about half the offspring would be white). If all of a large number of offspring are black, the parent is very likely BB. *(2 marks)*

1. Cross the black guinea pig with a homozygous recessive (white, bb) guinea pig. *(1 mark)*
2. If any white offspring appear, the parent must be Bb (expected ratio 1 black : 1 white); if all of many offspring are black, it is most likely BB. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Crossing it with another black guinea pig. A test cross uses the homozygous recessive so the unknown parent's alleles show directly in the offspring.

Topic: Monohybrid crosses and test crosses (vce-bio12-monohybrid-crosses-and-test-crosses)

**d.** Crossing over: in prophase I, non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes exchange segments, giving new combinations of alleles. Independent assortment: in metaphase I, each pair of homologous chromosomes lines up in a random orientation, so gametes get different mixes of maternal and paternal chromosomes. *(2 marks)*

1. Crossing over in prophase I exchanges sections between non-sister chromatids of homologous chromosomes, recombining alleles. *(1 mark)*
2. Independent assortment in metaphase I: homologous pairs line up randomly, so each gamete receives a different combination of chromosomes. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming mutation or fertilisation. The question asks how meiosis itself creates variation.

Topic: Meiosis and variation (vce-bio12-meiosis-and-variation)

**e.** 32 *(1 mark)*

1. Gametes are haploid (n), with half the diploid number: 64 ÷ 2 = 32. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving 64 or 128. Meiosis halves the chromosome number.

Topic: Chromosomes and karyotypes (vce-bio12-chromosomes-and-karyotypes)

**f.** A black coat is a physical feature that camouflages the guinea pig against the dark rocks. Predators are less likely to see it, so it is more likely to survive and reproduce. *(2 marks)*

1. Coat colour is a physical (structural) feature; a black coat blends in with the dark rocks. *(1 mark)*
2. This camouflage makes black guinea pigs harder for predators to see, increasing their chance of surviving and reproducing. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the guinea pigs turned black in order to blend in. Individuals do not change their coat colour to suit the environment; the feature helps those that have it survive.

Topic: Structural, physiological and behavioural adaptations (vce-bio12-structural-physiological-and-behavioural-adaptations)

**A925.** Cells that divide, specialise and die
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** The cell grows, makes organelles and proteins, and replicates its DNA (S phase), preparing to divide. *(2 marks)*

1. The cell grows and produces organelles and proteins (G1 and G2). *(1 mark)*
2. DNA is replicated during the S phase, so each chromosome consists of two sister chromatids. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling interphase a resting phase. The cell is very active, especially in copying its DNA.

Topic: The eukaryotic cell cycle and mitosis (vce-bio12-the-eukaryotic-cell-cycle-and-mitosis)

**b.** Prophase, metaphase, anaphase, telophase. In metaphase, chromosomes line up along the equator of the cell, attached to spindle fibres. *(2 marks)*

1. Order: prophase, metaphase, anaphase, telophase. *(1 mark)*
2. Metaphase: chromosomes line up along the cell's equator, with spindle fibres attached at the centromeres. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing anaphase (chromatids separating) as metaphase.

Topic: The eukaryotic cell cycle and mitosis (vce-bio12-the-eukaryotic-cell-cycle-and-mitosis)

**c.** Apoptosis removes damaged or abnormal cells by programmed death. If the signals fail, damaged cells survive and keep dividing, forming a tumour. *(2 marks)*

1. Apoptosis is programmed cell death that removes damaged or unneeded cells. *(1 mark)*
2. If apoptosis fails, abnormal cells survive and divide uncontrollably, which can form a tumour (cancer). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying cancer cells die more often. Cancer involves cells that avoid death and keep dividing.

Topic: Apoptosis and cancer (vce-bio12-apoptosis-and-cancer)

**d.** Embryonic stem cells are pluripotent (can become almost any cell type) and come from early embryos; adult stem cells are multipotent (limited to related cell types) and are found in tissues such as bone marrow. *(2 marks)*

1. Embryonic stem cells: pluripotent, from early embryos. *(1 mark)*
2. Adult stem cells: multipotent, found in tissues such as bone marrow, giving rise to a limited range of cells. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing only one type. A comparison needs both.

Topic: Stem cells and specialisation (vce-bio12-stem-cells-and-specialisation)

**e.** 64 *(1 mark)*

1. 2 hours = 6 divisions, so the number of cells is 2⁶ = 64. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Adding instead of doubling (1 + 6 = 7). Each division doubles the number of cells.

Topic: Binary fission (vce-bio12-binary-fission)

**f.** Bacteria have no nucleus and usually a single circular chromosome, so there is no need for the stages of mitosis or a spindle. *(1 mark)*

1. Prokaryotes have a single circular chromosome and no nucleus, so the DNA is simply copied and separated as the cell splits. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying bacteria have no DNA. They have DNA, just not inside a nucleus.

Topic: Binary fission (vce-bio12-binary-fission)

**A926.** Staying warm and staying hydrated
<!-- id: vce-bio12-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 10 marks*

**a.** Stimulus: falling body temperature. Receptors: thermoreceptors in the skin. Control centre: the hypothalamus. Effectors: skeletal muscles (shivering) and arterioles in the skin (vasoconstriction). Response: heat is produced and conserved, so body temperature rises back to normal. *(3 marks)*

1. Stimulus (drop in temperature) detected by thermoreceptors in the skin. *(1 mark)*
2. The hypothalamus, the control centre, sends signals to effectors. *(1 mark)*
3. Effectors respond: skeletal muscles shiver to produce heat, and skin arterioles constrict to reduce heat loss. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming the brain as the receptor. Receptors detect the stimulus; the hypothalamus processes it.

Topic: Thermoregulation (vce-bio12-thermoregulation)

**b.** Less blood flows through capillaries near the skin surface, so less heat is lost to the surroundings. *(1 mark)*

1. Narrowed arterioles send less warm blood to the skin surface, reducing heat loss by radiation and conduction. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the blood vessels move away from the skin. They narrow; they do not move.

Topic: Thermoregulation (vce-bio12-thermoregulation)

**c.** Ectotherms rely on external heat to control body temperature. Basking is an action that raises the lizard's body temperature so it can become active. *(2 marks)*

1. An ectotherm gains most of its heat from the environment rather than from metabolism. *(1 mark)*
2. Basking is a behaviour (an action), and it warms the lizard so that its enzymes and muscles work efficiently. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling it a structural adaptation. Structural adaptations are physical features; basking is a behaviour.

Topic: Structural, physiological and behavioural adaptations (vce-bio12-structural-physiological-and-behavioural-adaptations)

**d.** Osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus detect the high solute concentration of the blood; the posterior pituitary releases ADH, which makes the kidney's collecting ducts more permeable to water, so more water is reabsorbed and less, more concentrated urine is produced. *(2 marks)*

1. Dehydration raises blood solute concentration, detected by osmoreceptors in the hypothalamus, and the posterior pituitary releases ADH. *(1 mark)*
2. ADH increases the permeability of the collecting ducts to water, so more water is reabsorbed into the blood and urine is concentrated. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying ADH increases urine production. ADH reduces it by increasing reabsorption.

Topic: Water balance (vce-bio12-water-balance)

**e.** For example: a thick waxy cuticle; sunken or fewer stomata; small or needle-like leaves. *(2 marks)*

1. First adaptation, for example a thick, waxy cuticle that reduces evaporation. *(1 mark)*
2. Second adaptation, for example sunken stomata, fewer stomata, or reduced leaf surface area. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing behavioural or physiological responses. The question asks for structural features.

Topic: Adaptations to Australian environments (vce-bio12-adaptations-to-australian-environments)

## VCE Units 3 & 4

### Unit 3 AOS 1: Nucleic acids and proteins

#### Nucleic acid structure

**A927.** B. A phosphate group, a deoxyribose sugar and a nitrogenous base
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-1 -->

Explanation: A DNA nucleotide consists of a phosphate group, the pentose sugar deoxyribose and one of four nitrogenous bases (A, T, C or G). Ribose is the sugar in RNA nucleotides, not DNA.

**A928.** B. 28%
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-2 -->

Explanation: By complementary base pairing, G = C = 22%, so G + C = 44%. The remaining 56% is A + T, and since A = T, thymine is 28%. 56% is the combined A + T value, not thymine alone.

**A929.** C. phosphodiester bonds between the sugar of one nucleotide and the phosphate of the next.
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-3 -->

Explanation: The sugar-phosphate backbone is held together by covalent phosphodiester bonds linking the 3′ carbon of one sugar to the 5′ phosphate of the next nucleotide. Hydrogen bonds join the two strands to each other, not nucleotides within a strand.

**A930.** A. The base uracil
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-4 -->

Explanation: RNA contains uracil in place of thymine. Both DNA and RNA have a sugar-phosphate backbone, contain cytosine and are built in the 5′ to 3′ direction.

**A931.** D. Fragment X separates into single strands at a higher temperature because G–C pairs form three hydrogen bonds.
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-5 -->

Explanation: G–C pairs are held by three hydrogen bonds while A–T pairs are held by two, so DNA rich in G–C needs more energy (a higher temperature) to separate. Length is equal here, so base composition decides it.

**A932.** The two strands run in opposite directions; one strand runs 5′ to 3′ while its complementary strand runs 3′ to 5′ (the 5′ phosphate end of one strand lies opposite the 3′ hydroxyl end of the other).
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-6 -->

Explanation:

**A933.** Complementary strand: 3′-TACGGCAA-5′ (equivalently written 5′-AACGGCAT-3′); the strands are held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary bases.
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-7 -->

Explanation:

**A934.** Both are single-stranded RNA polymers made of ribonucleotides containing ribose and A, U, C, G; mRNA is a linear molecule that carries a copy of the genetic instructions (codons) from the DNA to the ribosome; tRNA folds into a cloverleaf shape held by internal base pairing, carries an anticodon at one end and a specific amino acid at the 3′ end, delivering amino acids to the ribosome during translation.
<!-- id: bio34a1-nas-8 -->

Explanation:

**A935.** C. Single-stranded RNA
<!-- id: vce-bio34-nas-c1 -->

Explanation: Uracil means RNA. In a double strand, A would equal U and G would equal C; here they do not, so it is single-stranded.

#### Transcription and RNA processing

**A936.** B. binds to the promoter and synthesises RNA in the 5′ to 3′ direction using the template strand.
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-1 -->

Explanation: RNA polymerase binds to the promoter upstream of the gene, unwinds the DNA and adds complementary ribonucleotides to the 3′ end of the growing RNA, reading the template strand 3′ to 5′. Start codons are recognised by ribosomes in translation, not by RNA polymerase.

**A937.** C. 5′-AUGCCUAAG-3′
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-2 -->

Explanation: mRNA is complementary and antiparallel to the template strand, with U replacing T: template 3′-TACGGATTC-5′ gives mRNA 5′-AUGCCUAAG-3′. Option A contains thymine, so it is DNA, not RNA.

**A938.** A. Addition of a poly-A tail
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-3 -->

Explanation: A poly-A tail (a long chain of adenine nucleotides) is added to the 3′ end. The methyl-guanine cap is added to the 5′ end, not the 3′ end. Splicing removes introns, not exons.

**A939.** D. It may contain different combinations of exons in different cells due to alternative splicing.
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-4 -->

Explanation: Introns are removed and exons are joined. Through alternative splicing, some exons may be included or excluded, so one gene can produce several mature mRNAs and therefore several proteins. Removing introns usually makes the mature mRNA much shorter than the gene.

**A940.** B. introns, which make up most of the gene, were removed from the pre-mRNA during splicing.
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-5 -->

Explanation: Eukaryotic genes often contain long introns that are transcribed into pre-mRNA and then spliced out, leaving only the exons in mature mRNA. Translation occurs in the cytoplasm, not before export, and only the template strand is ever transcribed.

**A941.** A 5′ methyl-guanine (methylated G) cap is added to the 5′ end, which protects the mRNA and helps ribosome attachment; a poly-A tail is added to the 3′ end, which protects the mRNA from degradation and aids export; introns are removed and exons are joined together by spliceosomes (splicing).
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A942.** During splicing, different combinations of exons from the same pre-mRNA can be retained or removed; this produces different mature mRNAs from one gene, each translated into a different polypeptide, so the number of proteins exceeds the number of genes.
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A943.** Prokaryotes lack a membrane-bound nucleus, so ribosomes can access mRNA as soon as it is made (eukaryotes separate transcription in the nucleus from translation in the cytoplasm); as a consequence, prokaryotic mRNA generally does not undergo splicing, capping and tailing, whereas eukaryotic pre-mRNA is processed in the nucleus before export.
<!-- id: bio34a1-trp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A944.** D. 5′-AUG CCU AAG-3′
<!-- id: vce-bio34-trp-c1 -->

Explanation: mRNA is complementary and antiparallel to the template strand, with U replacing T. Read 5′ to 3′: AUG CCU AAG.

#### Translation and the genetic code

**A945.** C. most amino acids are coded for by more than one codon.
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-1 -->

Explanation: Degeneracy (redundancy) means that more than one codon can specify the same amino acid, e.g. GGU, GGC, GGA and GGG all code for glycine. Option B describes universality, and the code is non-overlapping.

**A946.** A. 5′-AUG-3′
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-2 -->

Explanation: The anticodon pairs complementarily and antiparallel with the codon: 3′-UAC-5′ pairs with 5′-AUG-3′, the start codon for methionine. ATG contains thymine, which is not found in mRNA.

**A947.** D. 299
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-3 -->

Explanation: 900 nucleotides = 300 codons. The final codon is a stop codon, which does not code for an amino acid, leaving 299 amino acids (the start codon AUG codes for methionine and is counted).

**A948.** B. Peptide bonds form between adjacent amino acids delivered by tRNA.
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-4 -->

Explanation: tRNAs bring specific amino acids to the ribosome, where anticodons pair with mRNA codons and peptide bonds form between adjacent amino acids by condensation. The ribosome reads mRNA 5′ to 3′, and splicing happens in the nucleus.

**A949.** D. Translation stops early, producing a shortened, likely non-functional polypeptide.
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-5 -->

Explanation: UAA is a stop codon, so this is a nonsense mutation that terminates translation early. A substitution does not shift the reading frame, which is what an insertion or deletion would do.

**A950.** Each tRNA molecule binds (is charged with) a specific amino acid at its 3′ end; it carries this amino acid to the ribosome; its anticodon pairs complementarily with the matching mRNA codon, ensuring amino acids are added in the order specified by the mRNA.
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-6 -->

Explanation:

**A951.** Met–Ala–Trp–Lys; UGA is a stop codon, which has no matching tRNA, so a release factor binds instead and translation terminates, releasing the polypeptide.
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-7 -->

Explanation:

**A952.** The genetic code is universal; the same codons code for the same amino acids in almost all organisms, so bacterial ribosomes and tRNAs translate the jellyfish codons into the same amino acid sequence.
<!-- id: bio34a1-tgc-8 -->

Explanation:

**A953.** A. 1
<!-- id: vce-bio34-tgc-c1 -->

Explanation: Each amino acid here has only one codon, so only AUG UGG AUG works. For most amino acids degeneracy would allow several sequences.

#### Gene regulation and the trp operon

**A954.** A. corepressor that binds to the repressor protein, allowing it to bind the operator.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-1 -->

Explanation: When tryptophan levels are high, tryptophan binds to the inactive repressor, changing its shape so that it binds the operator and blocks RNA polymerase. Removing a repressor in response to a molecule describes an inducible operon such as lac, not trp.

**A955.** B. Regulatory genes code for proteins such as repressors that control the expression of structural genes.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-2 -->

Explanation: Regulatory genes (such as trpR) code for proteins that switch other genes on or off. Structural genes (such as trpE–trpA) code for proteins with other cell functions, such as enzymes. Regulatory genes are transcribed to produce their proteins.

**A956.** C. the structural genes continue to be transcribed even though tryptophan is abundant.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-3 -->

Explanation: Without bound tryptophan, the repressor stays inactive and cannot bind the operator, so RNA polymerase continues to transcribe the structural genes (attenuation may reduce, but not fully stop, transcription). The repressor could only bind the operator when tryptophan is bound.

**A957.** D. the ribosome moves quickly through the leader sequence, allowing a terminator hairpin (regions 3–4) to form so transcription stops.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-4 -->

Explanation: With plenty of tryptophan-carrying tRNA, the ribosome passes the two trp codons rapidly and covers region 2, so regions 3 and 4 pair to form a terminator hairpin and RNA polymerase detaches. Stalling and antiterminator formation occur when tryptophan is low.

**A958.** A. the added tryptophan acted as a corepressor and also enabled attenuation, both reducing transcription of the structural genes.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-5 -->

Explanation: Added tryptophan binds the repressor so it blocks the operator, and high tryptophan also causes early termination by attenuation; both reduce transcription quickly. Genes are not deleted and the repressor is a protein coded by trpR, not made from tryptophan.

**A959.** When tryptophan is low, no tryptophan binds to the repressor (coded by trpR), so the repressor stays inactive and cannot bind to the operator; RNA polymerase can therefore bind to the promoter and transcribe the structural genes that code for the enzymes that synthesise tryptophan.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-6 -->

Explanation:

**A960.** Making tryptophan-synthesis enzymes requires energy and resources (ATP, amino acids, nucleotides); by switching off the operon when tryptophan is already available, the bacterium avoids wasting resources and can direct them to other processes such as growth and reproduction.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-7 -->

Explanation:

**A961.** Transcription and translation are coupled in bacteria, so a ribosome begins translating the leader (trpL) sequence while it is being transcribed; when tryptophan (and tRNA carrying tryptophan) is abundant, the ribosome moves quickly past the trp codons in region 1 and covers region 2; this allows regions 3 and 4 to pair, forming a terminator hairpin loop that causes RNA polymerase to detach before the structural genes are transcribed.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gro-8 -->

Explanation:
