**A962.** B. The structural genes are still transcribed, wasting energy
<!-- id: vce-bio34-gr-c1 -->

Explanation: Tryptophan activates the repressor, but the repressor cannot bind the mutated operator, so RNA polymerase still transcribes trpE–trpA.

#### Protein structure and the proteome

**A963.** C. the sequence of amino acids in the polypeptide chain.
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-1 -->

Explanation: Primary structure is the linear sequence of amino acids joined by peptide bonds. α-helices and β-pleated sheets are secondary structure, 3D shape is tertiary structure and multiple polypeptides is quaternary structure.

**A964.** B. Hydrogen bonds between atoms of the polypeptide backbone
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-2 -->

Explanation: α-helices and β-pleated sheets are stabilised by hydrogen bonds between the C=O and N–H groups of the backbone. Disulfide bridges and other R-group interactions are mainly associated with tertiary structure.

**A965.** D. Quaternary
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-3 -->

Explanation: Quaternary structure is the association of two or more polypeptide chains into one functional protein. Myoglobin is a single polypeptide, so its highest level is tertiary structure.

**A966.** A. the complete set of proteins expressed by a cell, tissue or organism at a particular time.
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-4 -->

Explanation: The proteome is the full set of proteins expressed by a cell, tissue or organism at a given time under given conditions. The complete set of genes is the genome. Unlike the genome, the proteome varies between cell types and over time.

**A967.** C. The bacterial enzyme has more stabilising bonds (such as disulfide bridges and ionic bonds) maintaining its tertiary structure.
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-5 -->

Explanation: Heat disrupts the weaker bonds that hold tertiary structure, changing the active site (denaturation). Extra stabilising interactions help the bacterial enzyme keep its shape. Peptide bonds are not broken by denaturation, and the genetic code is universal.

**A968.** A condensation reaction occurs between the carboxyl group of one amino acid and the amino group of the next; a molecule of water is released and a covalent peptide bond (C–N) forms between them.
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A969.** The change alters the primary structure of the polypeptide; this changes the interactions between R groups (e.g. hydrophobic interactions, ionic bonds) and therefore the way the polypeptide folds into its tertiary structure; if the shape of the active site changes, the substrate may no longer be complementary, so enzyme activity is reduced or lost.
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A970.** Different genes are switched on (expressed) or off in different cell types due to gene regulation; in addition, alternative splicing and post-translational modification can differ between cells, so each cell type produces a different set of proteins.
<!-- id: bio34a1-psp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A971.** D. Alternative splicing of pre-mRNA and post-translational modification
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ps-c1 -->

Explanation: Different combinations of exons can be joined from one gene, and proteins can be modified after translation, so the proteome is larger than the genome.

#### Protein secretory pathway

**A972.** B. Rough endoplasmic reticulum → transport vesicle → Golgi apparatus → secretory vesicle → plasma membrane
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-1 -->

Explanation: Secreted proteins are made by ribosomes on the rough ER, enter the ER lumen, travel in transport vesicles to the Golgi apparatus for modification and packaging, and leave in secretory vesicles that fuse with the plasma membrane (exocytosis).

**A973.** C. modify, sort and package proteins into vesicles.
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-2 -->

Explanation: The Golgi apparatus receives proteins from the rough ER, modifies them (for example by adding carbohydrate groups), sorts them and packages them into vesicles. Polypeptide synthesis happens at ribosomes, and ATP comes mainly from mitochondria.

**A974.** A. exocytosis.
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-3 -->

Explanation: Exocytosis releases vesicle contents to the outside as the vesicle membrane fuses with the plasma membrane. Endocytosis and phagocytosis bring material into the cell.

**A975.** D. Rough ER, then Golgi apparatus, then secretory vesicles, then outside the cell
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-4 -->

Explanation: The labelled amino acid is built into proteins at ribosomes on the rough ER first, then moves to the Golgi, then into secretory vesicles, and finally outside the cell. Proteins are not made in the nucleus.

**A976.** A. the secreted protein accumulates in the rough ER and is not secreted.
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-5 -->

Explanation: Without vesicles to carry proteins from the rough ER to the Golgi, the protein builds up in the ER and does not reach the plasma membrane. Transcription in the nucleus is not affected by vesicle transport.

**A977.** Ribosomes attached to the rough ER translate the mRNA, and the growing polypeptide enters the ER lumen; within the ER the polypeptide is folded and may be modified, then packaged into transport vesicles that carry it to the Golgi apparatus.
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-6 -->

Explanation:

**A978.** Extensive rough ER provides many ribosomes and space to synthesise and fold large amounts of protein for secretion; many mitochondria supply ATP needed for protein synthesis and for vesicle transport and exocytosis.
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-7 -->

Explanation:

**A979.** The gene is transcribed into mRNA in the nucleus, which is processed and exported to the cytoplasm; the mRNA is translated by ribosomes on the rough ER and the polypeptide enters the ER lumen where it folds, then travels in a transport vesicle to the Golgi apparatus; the Golgi modifies and packages the protein into a secretory vesicle, which moves to and fuses with the plasma membrane, releasing the protein by exocytosis.
<!-- id: bio34a1-sec-8 -->

Explanation:

**A980.** A. Rough ER → transport vesicle → Golgi apparatus → secretory vesicle → plasma membrane
<!-- id: vce-bio34-psp-c1 -->

Explanation: Secreted proteins are made by ribosomes on the rough ER, carried in vesicles to the Golgi to be modified and packaged, then exported by exocytosis.

### Unit 3 AOS 1: DNA manipulation

#### Enzymes for DNA manipulation

**A981.** B. sticky ends with single-stranded 5′-AATT overhangs.
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-1 -->

Explanation: Cutting G^AATTC on both antiparallel strands leaves staggered cuts with complementary 5′-AATT single-stranded overhangs (sticky ends). Blunt ends occur when both strands are cut at the same position, e.g. SmaI.

**A982.** C. Reverse transcriptase
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-2 -->

Explanation: Reverse transcriptase synthesises complementary DNA (cDNA) from an mRNA template. Since mature mRNA has had its introns removed, the cDNA lacks introns, which is useful for expressing human genes in bacteria.

**A983.** D. join DNA fragments by forming phosphodiester bonds in the sugar-phosphate backbone.
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-3 -->

Explanation: DNA ligase catalyses formation of phosphodiester bonds between adjacent nucleotides, sealing the backbone of joined fragments. Cutting at recognition sites is done by restriction enzymes; copying is done by DNA polymerase.

**A984.** A. Linear: 4 fragments; circular: 3 fragments
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-4 -->

Explanation: Cutting a linear molecule n times produces n + 1 fragments (4), whereas cutting a circular molecule n times produces n fragments (3), because there are no free ends to begin with.

**A985.** B. Using the same enzyme produces complementary sticky ends that can base pair with each other.
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-5 -->

Explanation: Cutting both with the same restriction enzyme produces complementary sticky ends, so the gene and plasmid ends can base pair by hydrogen bonding before ligase seals the backbone. Restriction enzymes do not join DNA.

**A986.** DNA polymerase synthesises a new DNA strand by adding free nucleotides one at a time, complementary to a template strand, to the 3′ end of a growing strand/primer; DNA ligase joins two existing DNA fragments together by forming a phosphodiester bond between them, and does not use a template.
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-6 -->

Explanation:

**A987.** Restriction enzymes evolved as a defence against bacteriophages, cutting invading viral DNA at specific recognition sites; the bacteria's own DNA is protected because its recognition sites are chemically modified (methylated), so the enzyme cannot cut them.
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-7 -->

Explanation:

**A988.** Probability = (1/4)⁶ = 1/4096, so on average once every 4096 bp; a four-base-pair site has a probability of (1/4)⁴ = 1/256, occurring once every 256 bp on average; it therefore occurs more often, cutting the DNA at more sites and producing more, smaller fragments.
<!-- id: bio34a1-edm-8 -->

Explanation:

**A989.** B. The gene can only insert in one orientation and the plasmid cannot rejoin itself
<!-- id: vce-bio34-edm-c1 -->

Explanation: The two different sticky ends are not complementary to each other, which stops self-ligation and forces directional insertion. Ligase is still needed to seal the backbone.

#### CRISPR-Cas9

**A990.** C. provide an adaptive defence against bacteriophages by recognising and cutting viral DNA.
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-1 -->

Explanation: CRISPR-Cas is a bacterial immune system: fragments of phage DNA are stored as spacers in the CRISPR array, and on reinfection the transcribed crRNA guides Cas9 to cut the matching viral DNA. It is not involved in operon regulation or conjugation.

**A991.** D. base pair with a complementary target DNA sequence and direct Cas9 to that site.
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-2 -->

Explanation: The sgRNA contains a spacer sequence (about 20 nucleotides) complementary to the target DNA and binds Cas9, guiding it to the correct location. Cas9 is the endonuclease that cuts DNA; repair is done by the cell.

**A992.** A. protospacer adjacent motif (PAM).
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-3 -->

Explanation: Cas9 must recognise a PAM (5′-NGG-3′ for S. pyogenes Cas9) adjacent to the target before it cuts. This prevents bacteria from cutting the spacers in their own CRISPR array, which lack PAMs.

**A993.** B. small insertions or deletions that can shift the reading frame and knock out the gene.
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-4 -->

Explanation: NHEJ joins broken ends without a template and is error-prone, often adding or deleting a few nucleotides. These indels can cause a frameshift and a non-functional protein (a gene knockout). Precise insertion requires homology-directed repair with a template.

**A994.** D. Cas9 will not cut the target, even though the sgRNA is complementary, because no PAM is present.
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-5 -->

Explanation: Cas9 requires a PAM adjacent to the target sequence to cut. Without one, no double-stranded break is made at that site. Cas9 does not cut randomly; it is directed by sgRNA complementarity plus the PAM.

**A995.** When a bacteriophage injects its DNA, Cas proteins (Cas1 and Cas2) cut out a short segment of the viral DNA; this segment is inserted as a new spacer into the bacterium's CRISPR array between repeat sequences; on a later infection the array is transcribed into crRNA, which guides Cas9 to the matching viral DNA so it is cut and destroyed.
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-6 -->

Explanation:

**A996.** After Cas9 makes a double-stranded break at the target site, scientists supply a DNA template containing the desired sequence flanked by regions homologous to either side of the break; the cell uses homology-directed repair to copy the template into the break, inserting the corrected sequence.
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-7 -->

Explanation:

**A997.** Advantage: it is cheaper, faster and more precise/easily targeted, since only the sgRNA sequence needs to be changed to target a new gene; concern: off-target cuts could cause harmful mutations, and edits to embryos are heritable (germline) and passed on to future generations without their consent.
<!-- id: bio34a1-cas-8 -->

Explanation:

**A998.** C. There is no PAM sequence next to the target
<!-- id: vce-bio34-cr-c1 -->

Explanation: Cas9 needs a short protospacer-adjacent motif (PAM, such as NGG) next to the target sequence before it will cut.

#### PCR and gel electrophoresis

**A999.** C. Denaturation 95 °C; annealing 55 °C; extension 72 °C
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-1 -->

Explanation: DNA strands separate at about 95 °C, primers anneal at about 50–65 °C (e.g. 55 °C), and Taq polymerase extends the primers at its optimum of about 72 °C.

**A1000.** A. is stable at the high temperatures used to denature DNA.
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-2 -->

Explanation: Taq polymerase comes from the thermophilic bacterium Thermus aquaticus, so it does not denature at around 95 °C and is not destroyed in each cycle. It still requires primers.

**A1001.** D. 5120
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-3 -->

Explanation: Copies = initial × 2ⁿ = 5 × 2¹⁰ = 5 × 1024 = 5120. 1024 is the result from a single starting copy.

**A1002.** B. the phosphate groups in the DNA backbone are negatively charged.
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-4 -->

Explanation: DNA carries a negative charge from its phosphate groups, so it migrates towards the positive electrode (anode). Fragment size affects speed through the gel matrix, not the direction of movement.

**A1003.** C. 200 bp
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-5 -->

Explanation: Smaller fragments travel further through the gel. A band between the 300 bp and 100 bp markers is between those sizes, so about 200 bp. 400 bp would lie between the 300 and 500 bp bands.

**A1004.** Primers are short single-stranded DNA sequences complementary to the ends of the target region, providing a 3′ end from which DNA polymerase can begin adding nucleotides; two primers are needed because each binds to a different strand (forward and reverse) at opposite ends of the target, so both strands are copied and the region between them is amplified.
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1005.** PCR with primers flanking specific STR loci is used to amplify these regions from the small amounts of DNA in each sample; because individuals have different numbers of repeats, the amplified fragments differ in length; the fragments are separated by size using gel electrophoresis and the banding patterns of the crime scene sample and suspect are compared (matching bands at all loci indicate a match).
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1006.** Without a ladder of known fragment sizes, the sizes of the sample fragments cannot be determined (only their relative positions); a negative control (PCR reaction with no template DNA) should also be run to check that reagents are not contaminated with DNA.
<!-- id: bio34a1-pcr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1007.** A. 3072
<!-- id: vce-bio34-pcr-c1 -->

Explanation: Copies = starting number × 2ⁿ = 3 × 2¹⁰ = 3 × 1024 = 3072.

#### Recombinant plasmids and transformation

**A1008.** D. are small circular DNA molecules that replicate independently and can carry inserted genes.
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-1 -->

Explanation: Plasmids are small, circular, double-stranded DNA molecules with their own origin of replication, so they copy independently of the chromosome and can carry a gene of interest into a host cell.

**A1009.** A. Treating cells with calcium chloride and applying a heat shock
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-2 -->

Explanation: Calcium chloride treatment followed by a brief heat shock (e.g. 42 °C) makes the bacterial membrane more permeable to plasmids. Electroporation is another method. Restriction enzymes cut DNA, not cells.

**A1010.** B. have taken up a plasmid, which may or may not contain the gene of interest.
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-3 -->

Explanation: Only bacteria containing a plasmid (with its ampicillin-resistance gene) survive. Both recombinant and non-recombinant plasmids carry the resistance gene, so a second marker (e.g. lacZ) is needed to identify colonies with the insert.

**A1011.** C. took up a recombinant plasmid in which the insert disrupted lacZ.
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-4 -->

Explanation: Inserting the gene into lacZ disrupts it, so no functional β-galactosidase is made and X-gal is not broken down into a blue product, leaving white colonies. Blue colonies contain plasmids without the insert. Bacteria with no plasmid do not grow on ampicillin.

**A1012.** D. The human gene contains introns, and bacteria cannot remove introns from mRNA.
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-5 -->

Explanation: Bacteria lack the spliceosomes needed to remove introns, so a genomic eukaryotic gene would be translated incorrectly. cDNA (from mature mRNA) or synthetic DNA lacks introns. The genetic code is universal, so the codons themselves are not the problem.

**A1013.** The gene of interest and the plasmid are cut with the same restriction enzyme to produce complementary sticky ends; the gene and plasmid are mixed so their sticky ends base pair by hydrogen bonding; DNA ligase is added to form phosphodiester bonds, sealing the gene into the plasmid to form a recombinant plasmid.
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1014.** Transformation is inefficient because plasmids must cross the cell membrane and wall, which only happens in a small fraction of cells even after heat shock or electroporation; a selectable marker such as an antibiotic-resistance gene on the plasmid allows only transformed bacteria to grow on agar containing that antibiotic.
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1015.** Any two: the gene was inserted in the reverse orientation relative to the promoter, so it is not transcribed correctly; the plasmid lacks a bacterial promoter or ribosome binding site upstream of the gene; the gene contains introns that bacteria cannot remove; the protein is degraded by bacterial proteases or requires post-translational modification the bacteria cannot perform.
<!-- id: bio34a1-rpt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1016.** C. White, because the inserted gene disrupts lacZ
<!-- id: vce-bio34-rp-c1 -->

Explanation: Only transformed bacteria grow on ampicillin. A working lacZ breaks down X-gal to give a blue colour; an insert in lacZ disrupts it, so recombinant colonies are white.

#### GMOs and transgenic organisms in agriculture

**A1017.** A. A transgenic organism contains a gene that has been transferred from a different species.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-1 -->

Explanation: All transgenic organisms are GMOs, but a GMO is only transgenic if it contains DNA from another species. Switching off an organism's own gene (e.g. with CRISPR) produces a GMO that is not transgenic.

**A1018.** B. produces a protein that is toxic to certain caterpillar pests, reducing the need for insecticide sprays.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-2 -->

Explanation: The Bt gene codes for a Cry protein that is toxic to particular insect larvae such as the cotton bollworm, so growers spray less insecticide. Glyphosate tolerance is a different trait found in herbicide-tolerant crops such as some canola.

**A1019.** C. synthesise β-carotene, a precursor of vitamin A.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-3 -->

Explanation: Genes from daffodil (or maize) and a soil bacterium were inserted so that rice endosperm produces β-carotene, which the body converts to vitamin A. Herbicide resistance and salt tolerance are different traits.

**A1020.** D. naturally transfers a section of DNA from its Ti plasmid into the plant cell's genome.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-4 -->

Explanation: Agrobacterium transfers T-DNA from its Ti plasmid into plant chromosomes. Scientists replace tumour-causing genes in the T-DNA with the gene of interest so it is integrated into the plant genome.

**A1021.** A. repeated spraying selected for ryegrass plants that carried variations giving glyphosate resistance, which survived and reproduced.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-5 -->

Explanation: Glyphosate acts as a selection pressure: resistant ryegrass individuals survive and reproduce, so resistance increases in the population over time. Organisms do not change their genes deliberately, and gene flow to every weed plant is very unlikely.

**A1022.** The gene of interest (with a marker gene) is inserted into the T-DNA region of a Ti plasmid, replacing the tumour-causing genes, using restriction enzymes and ligase; the recombinant plasmid is put into Agrobacterium, which is used to infect plant cells or tissue, transferring the T-DNA into the plant genome; transformed cells are selected (e.g. on antibiotic or herbicide media) and grown by tissue culture into whole plants that carry the gene.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1023.** Benefit: reduced use of broad-spectrum insecticide sprays, protecting non-target insects and reducing costs while increasing yield; risk: target pests may evolve resistance to the Bt toxin through natural selection (managed using refuge crops), or the transgene may spread to related wild plants by cross-pollination.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1024.** For: GM crops can increase yields, resist pests or drought, or improve nutrition (e.g. Golden Rice), helping feed growing populations; against: patented GM seed may need to be bought each season, increasing costs and dependence of farmers on a few large companies, and some consumers and export markets reject GM products.
<!-- id: bio34a1-gmo-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1025.** A. Every transgenic organism is a GMO, but not every GMO is transgenic
<!-- id: vce-bio34-gmo-c1 -->

Explanation: A GMO has had its genome altered in any way (including editing its own genes); a transgenic organism contains DNA from a different species.

### Unit 3 AOS 2: Biochemical pathways

#### Enzymes and coenzymes

**A1026.** D. The active site changes shape slightly as the substrate binds, forming a tighter enzyme–substrate complex
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-1 -->

Explanation: In the induced fit model the active site is complementary but flexible, moulding around the substrate as it binds. The rigid, exact-match idea describes the older lock-and-key model, and enzymes are not consumed or permanently changed.

**A1027.** A. lowering the activation energy of the reaction
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-2 -->

Explanation: Enzymes are biological catalysts that provide an alternative pathway with a lower activation energy. They do not supply energy and do not change the final position of equilibrium; they only speed up attainment of it.

**A1028.** C. NADP⁺ → NADPH, produced in the light-dependent stage of photosynthesis
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-3 -->

Explanation: NADP⁺ accepts electrons and a proton to become NADPH in the light-dependent stage, and NADPH is unloaded in the Calvin cycle. NADH is made in cellular respiration, not the light-independent stage, and the Calvin cycle uses ATP rather than producing it.

**A1029.** B. a non-protein organic molecule that assists an enzyme, often by carrying electrons, protons or chemical groups between reactions
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-4 -->

Explanation: Coenzymes are organic, non-protein molecules (e.g. NAD⁺, NADP⁺, FAD, ATP/ADP) that bind temporarily and shuttle electrons, protons or phosphate groups between reactions. Inorganic helpers such as Mg²⁺ are cofactors but not coenzymes.

**A1030.** D. B
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-5 -->

Explanation: E2 converts B into C. Without functional E2, B cannot be converted, so B builds up while C and D are depleted. A still converts to B because E1 is functional.

**A1031.** Enzymes are proteins whose tertiary structure is determined by the sequence of amino acids (primary structure); folding produces an active site with a specific three-dimensional shape and chemical properties (charge, hydrophobicity); only a substrate with a complementary shape and chemistry can bind to the active site to form an enzyme–substrate complex, so each enzyme is specific to one substrate/reaction.
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1032.** Energy released from catabolic reactions such as cellular respiration is used to add an inorganic phosphate (Pi) to ADP, forming ATP; ATP diffuses to sites where energy is needed; hydrolysis of ATP to ADP + Pi releases energy that drives anabolic or energy-requiring processes such as active transport, muscle contraction or protein synthesis; ADP + Pi are then recycled back to respiration to be reloaded.
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1033.** Unloaded coenzymes (NAD⁺) accept electrons and hydrogen ions released during glycolysis and the Krebs cycle, becoming loaded (NADH); loaded NADH carries these electrons and protons to the electron transport chain on the inner mitochondrial membrane, where it is unloaded (returning to NAD⁺) and the energy is used to produce ATP.
<!-- id: bio34a2-enz-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1034.** B. NADP⁺ accepts electrons and H⁺ in the light-dependent stage, and NADPH donates them in the Calvin cycle
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ec-c1 -->

Explanation: Coenzymes cycle between loaded and unloaded forms, carrying electrons and protons from one stage to the next.

#### Factors affecting enzymes and inhibition

**A1035.** C. non-competitive inhibitor
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-1 -->

Explanation: Binding at a site other than the active site (an allosteric site) and altering active site shape is non-competitive inhibition. Competitive inhibitors and substrate analogues bind the active site itself.

**A1036.** A. the enzyme was denatured, permanently changing the shape of its active site
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-2 -->

Explanation: High temperatures break the hydrogen bonds and other interactions holding the tertiary structure, denaturing the enzyme. The change is irreversible here, so cooling does not restore activity. Peptide bonds (primary structure) are not broken by denaturation.

**A1037.** D. Enzyme concentration (all active sites were occupied)
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-3 -->

Explanation: At the plateau all active sites are saturated, so adding substrate has no effect; the rate is limited by the number of enzyme molecules. Adding more enzyme provides more active sites and raises the maximum rate, confirming enzyme concentration was limiting.

**A1038.** B. increasing the concentration of substrate
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-4 -->

Explanation: Competitive inhibitors compete with the substrate for the active site. Raising substrate concentration increases the likelihood that substrate, rather than inhibitor, occupies the active site. This does not overcome non-competitive inhibition.

**A1039.** C. the change in pH alters the charges on amino acid R groups, disrupting the bonds that maintain the active site shape
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-5 -->

Explanation: Changes in pH alter the ionisation of R groups, disrupting ionic and hydrogen bonds that hold the tertiary structure, so the active site loses its complementary shape. Each enzyme has an optimum pH matched to its environment.

**A1040.** An irreversible inhibitor forms strong (often covalent) bonds with the enzyme, permanently inactivating it; a reversible inhibitor binds with weak bonds and can detach, so enzyme activity can be restored; because the insect's acetylcholinesterase is permanently inactivated, enzyme activity cannot recover (new enzyme must be synthesised), so nerve function fails and the insect dies.
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1041.** At 10 °C the enzyme and substrate molecules have low kinetic energy, so there are fewer successful collisions between substrate and active site and fewer enzyme–substrate complexes form per unit time, giving low activity; the enzyme is not denatured at 10 °C, so activity would increase if warmed; at 60 °C heat disrupts the hydrogen bonds maintaining tertiary structure, so the enzyme denatures, the active site loses its shape and substrate can no longer bind, so activity is near zero.
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1042.** The final product of a pathway binds to (often allosterically inhibits) an enzyme early in the pathway, reducing its activity when product levels are high; this prevents over-production of the product and avoids wasting substrate and energy, with the pathway resuming when product levels fall.
<!-- id: bio34a2-inh-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1043.** D. Competitive, binding to the active site
<!-- id: vce-bio34-fe-c1 -->

Explanation: Extra substrate out-competes a competitive inhibitor for the active site. A non-competitive inhibitor changes the active site's shape, so more substrate cannot help.

#### Photosynthesis

**A1044.** A. thylakoid membranes of the chloroplast
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-1 -->

Explanation: Chlorophyll and the electron transport proteins of the light-dependent stage are embedded in the thylakoid membranes (stacked into grana). The stroma is the site of the light-independent stage (Calvin cycle).

**A1045.** D. Inputs: CO₂, NADPH, ATP. Outputs: glucose, NADP⁺, ADP + Pi
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-2 -->

Explanation: The Calvin cycle fixes CO₂ using energy from ATP and electrons/protons from NADPH, producing glucose and returning NADP⁺ and ADP + Pi to the light-dependent stage. The option with H₂O, NADP⁺ and ADP + Pi as inputs describes the light-dependent stage.

**A1046.** D. Oxygen gas released from the leaf
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-3 -->

Explanation: In the light-dependent stage, water is split (photolysis) to provide electrons and H⁺; the oxygen atoms from water are released as O₂. The oxygen atoms in glucose come from CO₂.

**A1047.** A. the supply of ATP and NADPH from the light-dependent stage stops
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-4 -->

Explanation: The Calvin cycle does not use light directly, but it depends on ATP and NADPH from the light-dependent stage. In darkness these are no longer produced, so the light-independent reactions slow and stop once existing supplies are used.

**A1048.** C. CO₂ concentration
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-5 -->

Explanation: At the plateau, increasing light has no effect, so light is not limiting. Because adding CO₂ increased the rate, CO₂ concentration was the factor limiting the Calvin cycle at that point.

**A1049.** Rubisco is the enzyme in the stroma that catalyses carbon fixation, combining CO₂ with a 5-carbon compound (RuBP) at the start of the Calvin cycle; like other enzymes it has an optimum temperature; below the optimum, fewer successful collisions occur so the rate is slower, and above the optimum the enzyme begins to denature (and binds O₂ more readily), reducing the rate of carbon fixation.
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1050.** 6CO₂ + 12H₂O → C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ + 6H₂O (in the presence of light and chlorophyll; 6CO₂ + 6H₂O → C₆H₁₂O₆ + 6O₂ also accepted); water is split in the light-dependent stage in the thylakoid membranes (grana); carbon dioxide is fixed in the light-independent stage (Calvin cycle) in the stroma.
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1051.** The light-dependent stage produces ATP and NADPH, which are required to power and reduce CO₂ in the light-independent stage; the light-independent stage regenerates ADP + Pi and NADP⁺, which must return to the thylakoids for the light-dependent stage to continue, so neither stage can continue for long without the other.
<!-- id: bio34a2-ps-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1052.** D. Oxygen gas released by the plant
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ph-c1 -->

Explanation: Water is split in the light-dependent stage, and its oxygen atoms are released as O₂. The oxygen in glucose comes from CO₂.

#### C3, C4 and CAM plants

**A1053.** B. rubisco binds oxygen instead of carbon dioxide, reducing the efficiency of carbon fixation
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-1 -->

Explanation: Rubisco can bind O₂ as well as CO₂. When O₂ binds, energy and fixed carbon are wasted and CO₂ is released, lowering photosynthetic efficiency. PEP carboxylase does not bind O₂, which is why C4 plants use it.

**A1054.** D. spatially separating initial CO₂ fixation in mesophyll cells from the Calvin cycle in bundle sheath cells
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-2 -->

Explanation: In C4 plants PEP carboxylase fixes CO₂ into a 4-carbon compound in mesophyll cells; this is moved to bundle sheath cells, where CO₂ is released at high concentration around rubisco. Separating stages in time (night/day) describes CAM plants.

**A1055.** C. CO₂ is fixed into a 4-carbon acid at night and released to rubisco for the Calvin cycle during the day
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-3 -->

Explanation: Pineapple is a CAM plant. At night, PEP carboxylase fixes CO₂ into organic acids (malate) stored in vacuoles. During the day, with stomata closed, CO₂ is released internally and enters the Calvin cycle, which needs ATP and NADPH from light.

**A1056.** A. A hot, arid desert with very low water availability
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-4 -->

Explanation: CAM plants keep stomata closed during the hot day, greatly reducing water loss while still fixing carbon. This trade-off is most beneficial in hot, dry environments. In cool, moist conditions C3 plants are generally more efficient.

**A1057.** D. at lower temperatures photorespiration in C3 plants is low, so the extra ATP cost of the C4 pathway is not offset
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-5 -->

Explanation: Concentrating CO₂ in C4 plants costs extra ATP. When it is cool, rubisco binds O₂ less often and stomata can stay open, so C3 plants lose little to photorespiration and avoid this energy cost.

**A1058.** In hot, dry conditions C3 plants close their stomata to reduce water loss by transpiration; this prevents CO₂ from diffusing into the leaf while O₂ produced by the light-dependent stage builds up, so the ratio of O₂ to CO₂ around rubisco increases; rubisco therefore binds O₂ more often (and its affinity for O₂ relative to CO₂ increases at higher temperatures), increasing photorespiration and reducing glucose production.
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1059.** Similarity: both first fix CO₂ using PEP carboxylase (which does not bind O₂) into a 4-carbon compound, later releasing CO₂ to rubisco for the Calvin cycle; difference: C4 plants separate the two steps spatially (mesophyll and bundle sheath cells) during the day, whereas CAM plants separate them in time (CO₂ fixed at night, Calvin cycle during the day) within the same cell.
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1060.** Recommend the C4 crop; in hot, sunny, dry conditions stomata may partially close, and a C3 crop would suffer high photorespiration, reducing yield; the C4 crop concentrates CO₂ around rubisco in bundle sheath cells, so photorespiration is minimised and it can photosynthesise efficiently with stomata only partly open, giving higher yield and better water use efficiency.
<!-- id: bio34a2-c4-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1061.** A. C4 plants separate initial CO₂ fixation and the Calvin cycle in space; CAM plants separate them in time
<!-- id: vce-bio34-c4-c1 -->

Explanation: C4: CO₂ is fixed in mesophyll cells and released to rubisco in bundle-sheath cells. CAM: CO₂ is fixed at night (stomata open) and used in the Calvin cycle by day.

#### Cellular respiration

**A1062.** B. cytosol
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-1 -->

Explanation: Glycolysis occurs in the cytosol and does not need oxygen. The Krebs cycle occurs in the mitochondrial matrix and the electron transport chain on the inner mitochondrial membrane (cristae).

**A1063.** C. 2 pyruvate, 2 ATP and 2 NADH
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-2 -->

Explanation: Glycolysis splits glucose into 2 pyruvate, producing a net 2 ATP (4 made, 2 used) and 2 NADH. CO₂ is released in the link reaction and Krebs cycle, not glycolysis.

**A1064.** A. is the final electron acceptor at the end of the electron transport chain, combining with electrons and H⁺ to form water
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-3 -->

Explanation: At the end of the electron transport chain, O₂ accepts electrons and H⁺ to form H₂O. Without O₂ the chain stops, NADH cannot be unloaded and most ATP production ceases. The CO₂ released in the Krebs cycle comes from the carbon skeletons of the fuel molecules.

**A1065.** D. The concentration of NADH would increase and ATP production would fall sharply
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-4 -->

Explanation: If the chain is blocked, NADH and FADH₂ cannot be unloaded, so NADH accumulates and NAD⁺ is depleted. The electron transport chain produces most ATP, so ATP levels fall sharply. Glycolysis does not need oxygen directly.

**A1066.** D. Electron transport chain, on the inner mitochondrial membrane (cristae)
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-5 -->

Explanation: Glycolysis and the Krebs cycle each produce only 2 ATP per glucose. The electron transport chain on the cristae uses energy from loaded coenzymes to produce the large majority of ATP (approximately 26–34 ATP).

**A1067.** Location: mitochondrial matrix; inputs: acetyl CoA (derived from 2 pyruvate), NAD⁺, FAD, ADP + Pi; outputs: CO₂ (released as waste), 2 ATP, loaded coenzymes NADH and FADH₂ (which carry electrons and H⁺ to the electron transport chain).
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1068.** More mitochondria means more sites for the Krebs cycle and electron transport chain (more cristae surface area), so the rate of aerobic respiration increases; more ATP can be produced aerobically to supply energy for muscle contraction during sustained exercise, reducing reliance on anaerobic respiration and lactate build-up.
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1069.** The electron transport chain proteins and ATP synthase are embedded in the inner mitochondrial membrane; folding into cristae greatly increases the surface area of this membrane, so more electron transport chains and ATP synthase enzymes are present and more ATP can be produced per mitochondrion.
<!-- id: bio34a2-cr-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1070.** D. The Krebs cycle, because NAD⁺ and FAD are no longer regenerated
<!-- id: vce-bio34-cr2-c1 -->

Explanation: The ETC unloads NADH and FADH₂. Without it, NAD⁺ and FAD run out and the Krebs cycle stops. Glycolysis can continue through fermentation.

#### Anaerobic respiration and fermentation

**A1071.** A. lactate (lactic acid) and 2 ATP
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-1 -->

Explanation: Anaerobic respiration in animals converts glucose to lactate, with a net yield of 2 ATP from glycolysis. Ethanol and CO₂ are the products in yeast (alcoholic fermentation). No CO₂ is produced in lactic acid fermentation.

**A1072.** C. regenerate NAD⁺ so that glycolysis can continue
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-2 -->

Explanation: Glycolysis requires NAD⁺. Without oxygen the electron transport chain cannot unload NADH, so pyruvate is reduced to lactate or ethanol, unloading NADH back to NAD⁺. No extra ATP is made in this step.

**A1073.** B. cytosol
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-3 -->

Explanation: Both glycolysis and the fermentation step that produces ethanol and CO₂ occur in the cytosol. Mitochondria are not used because the Krebs cycle and electron transport chain depend on oxygen.

**A1074.** D. respiring anaerobically, producing ethanol and CO₂
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-4 -->

Explanation: Once O₂ is used up, yeast switches to anaerobic respiration (alcoholic fermentation), which still releases CO₂ along with ethanol. Lactate is produced by animal cells and some bacteria, not yeast.

**A1075.** C. produces much less ATP because glucose is only partially broken down
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-5 -->

Explanation: Anaerobic respiration yields only 2 ATP per glucose because much of the chemical energy remains in lactate or ethanol. Aerobic respiration fully oxidises glucose to CO₂ and H₂O, producing far more ATP.

**A1076.** Yeast breaks down glucose by glycolysis in the cytosol into 2 pyruvate, producing 2 ATP and 2 NADH; in the absence of oxygen pyruvate is converted into ethanol and CO₂, which regenerates NAD⁺ so glycolysis can continue; advantage: it is a renewable fuel, and the CO₂ released when it burns was recently absorbed by the crop during photosynthesis, so net CO₂ emissions can be lower than for fossil fuels.
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1077.** Anaerobic respiration produces only 2 ATP per glucose, which cannot meet a high energy demand for long and rapidly uses up glucose; lactate (and associated H⁺) builds up in the muscle, lowering pH, which affects enzyme function and contributes to fatigue, so oxygen is needed to recover.
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1078.** Dough rises due to CO₂ released by yeast respiration (including fermentation); at 30 °C, closer to the optimum temperature of yeast enzymes, molecules have more kinetic energy and enzyme–substrate collisions are more frequent, so the rate of respiration and CO₂ production is faster than at 15 °C.
<!-- id: bio34a2-an-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1079.** Converting pyruvate to lactate regenerates NAD⁺ from NADH. Glycolysis needs NAD⁺, so it can continue and keep producing 2 ATP per glucose.
<!-- id: vce-bio34-an-c1 -->

Explanation: Without NAD⁺ regeneration glycolysis would stop; the lactate step itself makes no ATP.

#### Biotechnology to improve yield

**A1080.** A. Rubisco with a reduced tendency to bind O₂ relative to CO₂
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-1 -->

Explanation: Reducing rubisco's tendency to bind O₂ would lower photorespiration, so more carbon is fixed into glucose, increasing growth and yield. Increased O₂ binding would worsen photorespiration.

**A1081.** D. direct Cas9 to a specific target DNA sequence by complementary base pairing
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-2 -->

Explanation: The guide RNA has a sequence complementary to the target DNA, directing Cas9 to the correct site. Cas9 is the endonuclease that makes the double-stranded cut, adjacent to a PAM sequence.

**A1082.** B. Inserting genes for the C4 pathway enzymes and bundle sheath anatomy so CO₂ is concentrated around rubisco
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-3 -->

Explanation: Research projects aim to engineer C4-like features into rice so CO₂ is concentrated around rubisco, reducing photorespiration and increasing photosynthetic efficiency. The other options would reduce photosynthesis.

**A1083.** C. does not compete directly with food production for crops
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-4 -->

Explanation: Using agricultural waste and non-food biomass avoids diverting food crops (and the land they need) to fuel production. All bioethanol releases CO₂ when burned, and breaking down cellulose requires enzymes (cellulases) before fermentation.

**A1084.** A. yeast can only ferment simple sugars such as glucose, and cellulose is a polysaccharide
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-5 -->

Explanation: Cellulose is a polymer of glucose. Yeast lacks cellulases, so enzymes (or other pretreatments) must hydrolyse cellulose into glucose that yeast can take up and ferment to ethanol and CO₂.

**A1085.** A guide RNA complementary to the target gene is designed and delivered with Cas9 into plant cells; Cas9 is guided to the target sequence (next to a PAM) and makes a double-stranded cut; the cell repairs the break by non-homologous end joining, often introducing insertions/deletions that disable the gene; e.g. knocking out a gene that makes the plant susceptible to a disease, or a gene that limits grain size/number, could increase yield.
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1086.** Factor: photorespiration / the inefficiency of rubisco, which binds O₂ as well as CO₂; approach: gene editing (e.g. CRISPR-Cas9) or genetic modification to produce a rubisco with greater specificity for CO₂, or insert a CO₂-concentrating mechanism (e.g. C4 pathway genes); this would increase the rate of carbon fixation in the Calvin cycle, producing more glucose for growth and increasing yield.
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1087.** Advantage: it is renewable and the CO₂ released was recently absorbed during photosynthesis, so it can reduce net greenhouse gas emissions compared with fossil fuels; disadvantage: growing sugarcane for fuel uses land, water and fertiliser that could be used for food production, and processing requires energy inputs that reduce the net benefit.
<!-- id: bio34a2-bt-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1088.** C. Less photorespiration, so more carbon is fixed per unit of water lost
<!-- id: vce-bio34-by-c1 -->

Explanation: In heat, C3 plants close their stomata and rubisco binds O₂ more often. A C4 pathway concentrates CO₂ around rubisco.

### Unit 4 AOS 1: Responding to pathogens

#### Pathogens and physical and chemical barriers

**A1089.** D. A prion
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-1 -->

Explanation: Prions are misfolded proteins and viruses are also non-cellular. Bacteria, fungi and protozoa are cellular pathogens.

**A1090.** D. chemical barrier that breaks down bacterial cell walls
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-2 -->

Explanation: Lysozyme is an enzyme that hydrolyses peptidoglycan in bacterial cell walls, making it a chemical barrier in the first line of defence. Physical barriers include skin and mucus; microbiological barriers are normal flora.

**A1091.** A. killed many of the normal bacterial flora that usually outcompete fungi
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-3 -->

Explanation: Normal flora act as a microbiological barrier by competing for space and nutrients. Antibiotics kill bacteria but not fungi, so removing the normal flora allows fungi such as Candida to overgrow.

**A1092.** C. A waxy cuticle on the leaf surface
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-4 -->

Explanation: The waxy cuticle physically blocks pathogens and prevents water films that help fungal spores germinate. Saponins, chitinases and hydrogen peroxide are chemical defences.

**A1093.** B. Pathogens trapped in mucus are less effectively moved out of the airways, increasing the risk of respiratory infection
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-5 -->

Explanation: Mucus traps pathogens and cilia sweep the mucus up and out of the airways (the mucociliary escalator). Damaged cilia mean mucus and trapped pathogens accumulate, increasing infection risk.

**A1094.** A pathogen is an agent that causes disease, e.g. the influenza virus or the bacterium Mycobacterium tuberculosis; an antigen is a molecule (often a protein or glycoprotein) that is recognised by the immune system and can trigger an immune response, e.g. the haemagglutinin protein on the surface of the influenza virus.
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1095.** Physical: thick bark or a waxy cuticle that blocks entry of fungal hyphae, and/or thick cellulose cell walls that are hard to penetrate; chemical: production of antimicrobial/antifungal compounds such as essential oils, or enzymes such as chitinase that break down fungal cell walls (any two, with at least a correct description of each).
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1096.** Gastric juice contains hydrochloric acid giving a pH of about 1.5–3.5; this low pH denatures proteins (including enzymes) of many ingested microorganisms, killing them before they can reach and colonise the intestines.
<!-- id: bio34a2-pb-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1097.** D. Contain no nucleic acid; they are misfolded proteins that cause normal proteins to misfold
<!-- id: vce-bio34-pb-c1 -->

Explanation: Bacteria, fungi and protozoa are cellular; viruses contain nucleic acid; prions are protein only.

#### Innate immunity

**A1098.** D. It is non-specific, responds rapidly and does not produce immunological memory
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-1 -->

Explanation: The innate response (second line of defence) responds to general features shared by many pathogens, acts within minutes to hours and does not create memory. Specificity and memory are features of adaptive immunity carried out by lymphocytes.

**A1099.** C. cause vasodilation and increased permeability of capillaries
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-2 -->

Explanation: Histamine causes vasodilation (redness, heat) and increased capillary permeability, allowing fluid, complement proteins and phagocytes to move into the infected tissue (swelling). Natural killer cells and cytotoxic T cells release perforin.

**A1100.** A. cells that have abnormal or reduced MHC class I markers, such as virus-infected or cancerous cells
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-3 -->

Explanation: NK cells detect cells with reduced or abnormal MHC I (common in virus-infected and cancer cells) and induce apoptosis by releasing perforin and granzymes. They are part of innate immunity and do not need prior sensitisation.

**A1101.** D. virus-infected cells, and they signal neighbouring cells to increase their resistance to viral replication
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-4 -->

Explanation: Interferons are cytokines released by virus-infected host cells. They signal neighbouring uninfected cells to produce antiviral proteins, slowing the spread of the virus. They are not antibodies.

**A1102.** B. Higher temperature can slow bacterial reproduction and speed up some immune processes such as phagocytosis
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-5 -->

Explanation: A moderate fever, triggered by chemicals acting on the hypothalamus, creates conditions less favourable for many pathogens to reproduce and enhances some immune responses. Very high fevers can be harmful, but 39 °C does not denature most human enzymes.

**A1103.** The neutrophil is attracted to the infection site by chemical signals (chemotaxis) and recognises the pathogen (e.g. via receptors that bind pathogen surface molecules or complement/antibody); it engulfs the pathogen by extending its membrane around it, forming a phagosome (endocytosis); the phagosome fuses with a lysosome and hydrolytic enzymes digest the pathogen, with waste removed by exocytosis.
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1104.** Damaged cells and mast cells release histamine and other chemicals; histamine causes vasodilation, increasing blood flow (redness and heat), and increases capillary permeability so fluid leaks into tissue (swelling, pressing on nerves to cause pain); increased permeability allows phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages) and complement proteins to move into the tissue and destroy pathogens, with clotting factors helping to wall off the area.
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1105.** Complement proteins can form a membrane attack complex that creates pores in the bacterial membrane, causing lysis; they can coat (opsonise) bacteria, making them easier for phagocytes to recognise and engulf; they also act as chemical signals that attract phagocytes to the site (any two).
<!-- id: bio34a2-inn-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1106.** B. Mast cells release histamine → blood vessels dilate and become more permeable → phagocytes move into the tissue
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ii-c1 -->

Explanation: Histamine causes vasodilation (redness, heat) and leaky capillaries (swelling), letting phagocytes and complement reach the site.

#### Antigen presentation and the lymphatic system

**A1107.** C. all nucleated body cells
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-1 -->

Explanation: MHC I markers are present on all nucleated cells and display fragments of proteins made inside the cell (self or, if infected, viral peptides). MHC II is restricted to antigen-presenting cells such as dendritic cells, macrophages and B cells. Mature red blood cells lack a nucleus and MHC I.

**A1108.** A. present antigen fragments on MHC II to T helper cells with complementary receptors
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-2 -->

Explanation: Dendritic cells are antigen-presenting cells that carry antigen through lymphatic vessels to lymph nodes, where many naive T cells are concentrated. There, a T helper cell with a complementary receptor binds the MHC II–antigen complex and is activated.

**A1109.** D. Returning excess tissue fluid to the blood and providing sites where lymphocytes encounter antigens
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-3 -->

Explanation: The lymphatic system collects excess tissue fluid (lymph) and returns it to the bloodstream, and its lymph nodes and spleen are secondary lymphoid tissues where lymphocytes are activated. It has no pump and does not make red blood cells.

**A1110.** D. lymphocytes in the nodes are undergoing clonal expansion after encountering antigen
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-4 -->

Explanation: When lymphocytes with receptors complementary to an antigen are activated in a lymph node, they proliferate rapidly (clonal expansion), increasing the size of the node. Swollen nodes are a sign that the adaptive response is under way.

**A1111.** A. B cells in the bone marrow; T cells in the thymus
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-5 -->

Explanation: Both B and T cells originate from stem cells in bone marrow. B cells mature in the bone marrow, while T cells migrate to and mature in the thymus. Both are primary lymphoid organs.

**A1112.** MHC I is found on all nucleated cells, whereas MHC II is found only on antigen-presenting cells (dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells); MHC I presents peptides from proteins made inside the cell (endogenous, e.g. viral proteins in an infected cell), which are recognised by cytotoxic T cells; MHC II presents peptides from material engulfed from outside the cell (exogenous, e.g. phagocytosed bacteria), which are recognised by T helper cells.
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1113.** Cells of the donor organ carry MHC markers (self-markers) that differ from those of the recipient; the recipient's immune system recognises the donor MHC as non-self/foreign (antigens), activating T cells (and antibodies) that attack and destroy the transplanted tissue.
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1114.** Bacteria and/or antigen-presenting cells such as dendritic cells that have engulfed the bacteria travel in lymph through lymphatic vessels; they reach a nearby lymph node, where the dendritic cell presents antigen on MHC II to T helper cells, and B cells encounter antigen; lymphocytes with complementary receptors are selected and undergo clonal expansion in the node, after which effector cells and antibodies leave via lymph and blood to the site of infection.
<!-- id: bio34a2-ap-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1115.** C. Mature red blood cells have no nucleus and do not display MHC class I
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ap-c1 -->

Explanation: Cytotoxic T cells recognise antigen presented on MHC I, which nucleated cells display. Mature red blood cells lack nuclei and MHC I.

#### Humoral immunity

**A1116.** C. pathogens and antigens in extracellular fluids such as blood and lymph
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-1 -->

Explanation: The humoral response produces antibodies that circulate in body fluids and act on extracellular pathogens and toxins. Infected and cancerous cells are targeted by cell-mediated immunity (cytotoxic T cells).

**A1117.** B. a T helper cell recognises the antigen presented on the B cell's MHC II and releases cytokines
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-2 -->

Explanation: The B cell internalises the antigen and presents fragments on MHC II. A T helper cell with a complementary receptor binds and releases cytokines (e.g. interleukins), stimulating the B cell to undergo clonal expansion and differentiation.

**A1118.** D. variable region at the tips of the heavy and light chains
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-3 -->

Explanation: Each antibody has two antigen-binding sites formed by the variable regions of a heavy and a light chain. Differences in amino acid sequence here give each antibody its specificity. The constant region determines the antibody class and its interactions with other immune components.

**A1119.** C. neutralisation
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-4 -->

Explanation: Neutralisation occurs when antibodies cover the parts of a pathogen or toxin needed to bind host cells. Agglutination is the clumping of pathogens by antibodies; opsonisation is coating pathogens to enhance phagocytosis.

**A1120.** A. memory B cells produced during the first exposure rapidly differentiating into plasma cells
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-5 -->

Explanation: During the primary response, clonal expansion produces memory B cells as well as plasma cells. On re-exposure, the larger population of memory B cells responds quickly, differentiating into plasma cells that produce more antibody, sooner.

**A1121.** A naive B cell with B cell receptors (membrane-bound antibodies) complementary to the antigen binds it (clonal selection) and presents antigen fragments on MHC II; a T helper cell that has been activated against the same antigen binds the B cell and releases cytokines; the B cell is stimulated to undergo clonal expansion (rapid mitosis), producing many identical cells; these differentiate into plasma cells, which secrete large quantities of specific antibodies, and memory B cells, which persist and provide a faster secondary response.
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1122.** Agglutination: each antibody has (at least) two binding sites, so antibodies bind several bacteria at once, clumping them together so they cannot spread and are more easily engulfed by phagocytes; opsonisation: antibodies coat the bacteria and phagocytes recognise the constant region of bound antibodies, which increases the rate of phagocytosis.
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1123.** Antibodies are specific: the variable region of each antibody has a shape complementary to one specific antigen (epitope); antigens on the chickenpox virus have a different shape from measles antigens, so measles antibodies cannot bind to them, and no memory cells specific for chickenpox exist.
<!-- id: bio34a2-hum-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1124.** D. Both antibody production and cytotoxic T cell responses are greatly weakened
<!-- id: vce-bio34-hi-c1 -->

Explanation: Helper T cells release cytokines that activate the selected B cells (clonal expansion into plasma and memory cells) and cytotoxic T cells.

#### Cell-mediated immunity

**A1125.** B. Cytotoxic T cell
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-1 -->

Explanation: Cytotoxic T cells recognise antigen presented on MHC I of infected cells and induce apoptosis. Plasma cells secrete antibodies (humoral immunity) and helper T cells coordinate the response but do not kill infected cells themselves.

**A1126.** C. viral antigen fragments on MHC class I markers.
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-2 -->

Explanation: Almost all nucleated cells display endogenous (internal) antigen fragments on MHC I, which is recognised by the T cell receptor and CD8 of cytotoxic T cells. MHC II is found on professional antigen-presenting cells and is recognised by helper T cells.

**A1127.** A. undergo apoptosis.
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-3 -->

Explanation: Perforin forms pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes to enter and activate caspases, which trigger apoptosis (programmed cell death). Interferon is released by infected cells, not as a result of granzyme action.

**A1128.** B. Neither clonal expansion of B cells nor activation of cytotoxic T cells occurs effectively.
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-4 -->

Explanation: Helper T cells release cytokines (interleukins) that stimulate clonal expansion and differentiation of both selected B cells and cytotoxic T cells, so both arms of adaptive immunity are weakened (as in advanced HIV infection). Innate barriers and MHC expression are not dependent on helper T cells.

**A1129.** D. memory T cells.
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-5 -->

Explanation: Memory T cells are produced during clonal expansion and persist long-term, enabling a faster, larger secondary response. Plasma cells are antibody-secreting B cell derivatives and naive T cells have not yet encountered their antigen.

**A1130.** Dendritic cell presents viral antigen on MHC II to a helper T cell with a complementary T cell receptor (and antigen on MHC I to a naive cytotoxic T cell with a complementary receptor); the activated helper T cell releases cytokines/interleukins; cytokines stimulate clonal selection and clonal expansion of the specific cytotoxic T cells (and production of memory T cells); cytotoxic T cells leave the lymph node, travel to the infection site, bind infected cells displaying the antigen on MHC I and release perforin and granzymes, causing apoptosis.
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1131.** Antibodies are found in body fluids (plasma, lymph) and can only bind antigens/pathogens outside cells, so they cannot reach viruses replicating inside cells; cytotoxic T cells recognise viral antigen displayed on MHC I of infected cells and destroy the whole infected cell, removing the site of viral replication.
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1132.** Donor cells display MHC markers that differ from the recipient's (non-self); recipient cytotoxic T cells (activated with help from helper T cells) would recognise these as foreign and destroy the transplanted tissue, causing rejection; suppressing T cells reduces rejection but increases the risk of infections (or certain cancers) because the recipient's adaptive immune response is weakened.
<!-- id: bio34a3-cmi-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1133.** The infected cell presents viral antigen on MHC I. A cytotoxic T cell with a complementary receptor binds it and releases perforin and granzymes, triggering apoptosis. Antibodies act only outside cells, and viruses replicating inside cells are hidden from them.
<!-- id: vce-bio34-cm-c1 -->

Explanation: Mention MHC I, receptor specificity, apoptosis, and the location of the virus.

#### Types of immunity and vaccination

**A1134.** B. natural passive immunity.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-1 -->

Explanation: The antibodies are received naturally (not by medical intervention) and made by another individual, so it is natural passive immunity. Active immunity requires the person's own B cells to make antibodies and memory cells.

**A1135.** C. It provides immediate but short-term protection.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-2 -->

Explanation: Antivenom contains pre-made antibodies, giving artificial passive immunity: it acts immediately but no memory cells are formed, and the antibodies are broken down within weeks. The bushwalker's own B cells are not involved.

**A1136.** A. To trigger a secondary response that produces more memory cells and a higher antibody level.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-3 -->

Explanation: A booster exposes memory cells to the antigen again, producing a faster, larger secondary response with higher antibody titres and more memory cells, extending protection. Vaccines produce active, not passive, immunity.

**A1137.** D. causing the recipient's cells to translate a viral antigen, which then stimulates an adaptive immune response.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-4 -->

Explanation: mRNA in lipid nanoparticles enters cells and is translated into a viral protein (antigen), which is presented to lymphocytes, stimulating antibody production and memory cells. mRNA does not enter the nucleus or integrate into DNA, and it is not an attenuated or passive vaccine.

**A1138.** B. the chain of transmission is broken, as most contacts cannot become infected.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-5 -->

Explanation: This is herd immunity: when a high proportion of the population is immune, the pathogen cannot spread efficiently, protecting those who cannot be vaccinated. Antibodies are not transferred by casual contact.

**A1139.** Active immunity: the individual's own immune system is exposed to an antigen and produces antibodies (via plasma cells) and memory cells; protection is long-lasting because of memory cells (but takes time to develop); passive immunity: antibodies made by another organism are received, giving immediate protection; it is short-lived because no memory cells are made and the antibodies are degraded.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1140.** The first dose triggered a primary response: naive B cells had to undergo clonal selection and expansion, so there was a lag and a small peak; memory B cells (and memory helper T cells) were produced during the primary response; at the booster, these memory cells recognised the antigen and rapidly differentiated into many plasma cells, producing a faster and much larger secondary response.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1141.** If fewer people are immune than the threshold, an infected person is likely to contact enough susceptible people that each case leads to more than one new case; the pathogen can then spread through clusters of susceptible (unvaccinated or non-responding) individuals, causing an outbreak.
<!-- id: bio34a3-tiv-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1142.** A. About 93%
<!-- id: vce-bio34-tv-c1 -->

Explanation: 1 − 1/15 ≈ 0.93. Highly infectious diseases need very high vaccination coverage.

#### Emerging diseases and immunotherapy

**A1143.** B. Measles returning to a region after vaccination rates fall
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-1 -->

Explanation: A re-emerging disease is a known disease that had declined but is increasing again, e.g. measles after falling vaccination rates. A new coronavirus jumping species is an emerging disease; genetic and lifestyle conditions are not infectious diseases.

**A1144.** A. International air travel
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-2 -->

Explanation: Air travel allows infected people to move between continents within the incubation period, spreading the pathogen rapidly, as occurred with COVID-19. The other options reduce disease spread.

**A1145.** C. fusing a specific plasma (B) cell with a myeloma (cancer) cell to form a hybridoma that is cloned.
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-3 -->

Explanation: A hybridoma combines the ability of a plasma cell to make one specific antibody with the ability of a myeloma cell to divide indefinitely; clones produce identical (monoclonal) antibodies. Pooled donor antibodies would be polyclonal.

**A1146.** B. prevents PD-L1 from binding, so cytotoxic T cells remain active against the cancer cells.
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-4 -->

Explanation: This is a checkpoint inhibitor: blocking PD-1 stops the cancer cell's PD-L1 from inactivating the T cell, so the patient's cytotoxic T cells can recognise and destroy the cancer. The antibody targets PD-1 on T cells, not PD-L1 on cancer cells, and does not itself kill cells.

**A1147.** C. partner with Traditional Owners, gain free, prior and informed consent and share benefits.
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-5 -->

Explanation: Ethical research respects Indigenous cultural and intellectual property, involving genuine partnership, informed consent and benefit-sharing with the communities who hold the knowledge.

**A1148.** Factor 1: international travel/globalisation; infected people (including asymptomatic carriers) moved between countries before detection, seeding new outbreaks; factor 2: high population density/urbanisation or a lack of prior immunity in the population; crowded conditions increase contact rates for airborne/droplet spread, and with no existing immunity (novel virus) almost everyone was susceptible. (Accept also asymptomatic transmission, long incubation period.)
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1149.** The monoclonal antibody is specific/complementary to the antigen (e.g. HER2) on the cancer cell, so it binds only cancer cells and not healthy cells; binding may block the receptor and stop signals that stimulate uncontrolled division; it can also tag the cell for destruction by immune cells (e.g. NK cells/phagocytes), or carry an attached drug/radioactive substance directly to the cancer cell.
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1150.** A pathogen that normally infects animals (reservoir host) is transmitted to humans through close contact, e.g. habitat destruction, wildlife trade or farming; mutation(s) may allow the pathogen to bind human cells and spread from human to human, and because humans have no prior immunity it can spread rapidly.
<!-- id: bio34a3-edi-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1151.** A. Blocking PD-1 so T cells stay active and attack the cancer cells
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ed-c1 -->

Explanation: Checkpoint inhibitors release the brakes on the patient's own T cells. This is immunotherapy, a form of passive treatment, not vaccination.

### Unit 4 AOS 2: Change over time

#### Mutations, gene flow and genetic drift

**A1152.** A. Mutation
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-1 -->

Explanation: Mutation changes DNA sequences and creates new alleles. Gene flow can introduce alleles that are new to a particular population, but they already existed elsewhere; drift and selection only change existing allele frequencies.

**A1153.** C. causes a frameshift that alters every codon downstream.
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-2 -->

Explanation: Deleting one base shifts the reading frame, so all downstream codons are changed, often producing a non-functional protein or an early stop codon. A substitution affects at most one codon.

**A1154.** C. a bottleneck effect.
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-3 -->

Explanation: A bottleneck occurs when a population is drastically reduced, so the surviving gene pool is a small, random sample of the original, reducing diversity. The founder effect involves a small group colonising a new area.

**A1155.** B. A population of 25 rock-wallabies isolated on an island
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-4 -->

Explanation: Genetic drift is random change in allele frequency and has the greatest effect in small, isolated populations, where chance events strongly change the proportions of alleles passed on. Gene flow counteracts drift.

**A1156.** D. gene flow, which makes the gene pools of the two populations more similar.
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-5 -->

Explanation: Movement of alleles between populations via gametes (pollen) is gene flow; it tends to make populations more genetically similar. No new mutation occurs in this process.

**A1157.** Founder effect: a small group of individuals leaves a population to colonise a new area, so the new population's gene pool reflects only the founders' alleles; bottleneck effect: a large population is drastically reduced in size by a chance event (e.g. bushfire, disease), and survivors carry only a subset of alleles; similarity: both are forms of genetic drift that reduce genetic diversity and can change allele frequencies by chance.
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1158.** Genetic drift (a bottleneck effect); because the deaths were random rather than related to phenotype, the 20 survivors happened by chance to carry fewer B alleles, so the allele frequency changed without selection.
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1159.** A somatic mutation occurs in a body cell and is not passed to offspring, so it cannot change the population's gene pool; a germline mutation occurs in cells that produce gametes and can be inherited, introducing a new allele into the gene pool on which evolutionary mechanisms can act.
<!-- id: bio34a3-mgd-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1160.** B. The founder effect, a form of genetic drift
<!-- id: vce-bio34-mg-c1 -->

Explanation: A small founding group carries a non-representative sample of alleles by chance. Gene flow would make the frequencies more similar.

#### Natural selection and selective breeding

**A1161.** C. Individuals deliberately changing their traits to suit the environment
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-1 -->

Explanation: Natural selection acts on existing heritable variation; individuals cannot choose to acquire inherited traits. Variation, a selection pressure and differential reproduction are all required.

**A1162.** B. longer-legged toads disperse faster, reach new areas first and breed with other fast toads.
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-2 -->

Explanation: At the front, toads with heritable longer legs travel further and arrive first, so they breed together and pass on the alleles, increasing their frequency (spatial sorting/selection). Acquired characteristics are not inherited, and mutations do not arise in response to need.

**A1163.** A. involves humans choosing which individuals reproduce, often reducing genetic diversity.
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-3 -->

Explanation: In selective breeding, humans are the selecting agent and breed individuals with desired traits, which increases desired allele frequencies but narrows the gene pool, reducing diversity.

**A1164.** D. predation by birds that see contrasting moths more easily.
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-4 -->

Explanation: The selection pressure is the environmental factor that affects survival: birds preferentially eat moths that contrast with the bark. The dark colour is the advantageous phenotype, not the pressure.

**A1165.** C. Plants are genetically very similar, so the whole crop may be vulnerable to a single new disease.
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-5 -->

Explanation: Selective breeding narrows genetic diversity; with uniform genotypes, a pathogen able to infect one plant can infect most of the crop, risking total crop failure.

**A1166.** Variation: snails in the population vary in shell thickness due to different alleles (arising from mutation); selection pressure: crabs crush and eat thin-shelled snails more easily; selective advantage: thick-shelled snails are more likely to survive and reproduce; inheritance: they pass the thick-shell alleles to offspring, so over many generations the frequency of the thick-shell allele/phenotype increases in the population.
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1167.** Humans apply a strong, consistent and deliberate selection pressure, allowing only individuals with the desired trait to breed each generation; in natural selection, pressures vary and individuals without the advantageous trait still often reproduce, so allele frequencies change more slowly.
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1168.** Genetic diversity decreases because only a few sheep with particular alleles are used for breeding (and inbreeding increases); consequence: the flock has a reduced ability to adapt to environmental change/disease, or recessive harmful conditions become more common due to increased homozygosity.
<!-- id: bio34a3-nss-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1169.** A. Selection for a few traits reduces genetic diversity, so fewer individuals may carry resistance alleles
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ns-c1 -->

Explanation: Low diversity means one pathogen can affect most of the population, as in the Irish potato famine.

#### Antibiotic and antiviral resistance

**A1170.** C. Resistant bacteria already present survive the antibiotic and reproduce, increasing the frequency of resistance.
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-1 -->

Explanation: Resistance alleles arise by random mutation (or are acquired by horizontal gene transfer) before exposure; the antibiotic acts as a selection pressure, killing susceptible bacteria. Bacteria, not patients, become resistant.

**A1171.** B. transfer of plasmids by conjugation.
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-2 -->

Explanation: Horizontal gene transfer via plasmids (conjugation) spreads resistance genes between bacteria. Binary fission passes genes only to daughter cells (vertical transfer), and bacteria do not undergo meiosis.

**A1172.** A. It allows the more-resistant bacteria that survived early doses to multiply.
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-3 -->

Explanation: Early doses kill the most susceptible bacteria; stopping early leaves those with partial resistance alive to reproduce, so resistance alleles increase in frequency.

**A1173.** D. increase, as the drug selects for viruses with the mutation.
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-4 -->

Explanation: The drug is a selection pressure: susceptible viruses are inhibited while resistant variants replicate and spread, increasing their frequency. Viruses evolve rapidly because of high mutation rates and short replication times.

**A1174.** C. Prescribing antibiotics only for bacterial infections, using narrow-spectrum drugs where possible
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-5 -->

Explanation: Reducing unnecessary use lowers the selection pressure for resistance. Antibiotics are ineffective against viruses, and routine use in livestock and relying on one drug increase selection for resistance.

**A1175.** Variation: some bacteria possess a resistance allele/gene that arose by random mutation or was acquired by plasmid transfer; selection pressure: methicillin use kills susceptible bacteria; resistant bacteria survive and reproduce rapidly by binary fission, passing on the resistance gene (and may share it by conjugation); over many generations the frequency of resistant bacteria increases until most of the population is resistant.
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1176.** For a virus to survive, it would need resistance mutations to all three drugs at once, which is extremely unlikely; so combination therapy reduces the chance that resistant variants survive and are selected, slowing the evolution of resistance.
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1177.** Bacteria have very short generation times (can divide every 20–30 minutes) and huge population sizes, so mutations arise frequently and selection acts over many generations quickly; humans have long generation times and fewer offspring, so changes in allele frequency take much longer.
<!-- id: bio34a3-aar-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1178.** Random mutations already give a few bacteria some resistance. The antibiotic kills susceptible bacteria, so resistant ones survive and reproduce, passing on the allele (natural selection). Stopping early leaves the more resistant survivors to multiply.
<!-- id: vce-bio34-ar-c1 -->

Explanation: The antibiotic does not cause the mutation; it selects for it. Horizontal gene transfer (plasmids) can spread resistance too.

#### Speciation

**A1179.** D. can interbreed under natural conditions to produce viable, fertile offspring.
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-1 -->

Explanation: The biological species concept is based on reproductive compatibility producing viable, fertile offspring. Similar appearance and shared habitat are not sufficient, and individuals within a species have different DNA sequences.

**A1180.** A. Geographical isolation → different selection pressures and genetic drift → genetic divergence → reproductive isolation
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-2 -->

Explanation: A geographical barrier prevents gene flow; each population experiences different selection pressures, mutations and drift, so gene pools diverge until the populations can no longer interbreed even if brought together.

**A1181.** B. are separate species, as their hybrid offspring are not fertile.
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-3 -->

Explanation: Mules are infertile, so horses and donkeys cannot produce fertile offspring and are separate species (post-zygotic isolation). Their ability to hybridise reflects a relatively recent common ancestor.

**A1182.** C. temporal (seasonal) reproductive isolation.
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-4 -->

Explanation: Breeding at different times prevents interbreeding even though the populations share a habitat; this is a pre-zygotic temporal isolating mechanism, which can lead to sympatric speciation.

**A1183.** D. a polyploid plant cannot produce fertile offspring with the parent species, so it is instantly reproductively isolated.
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-5 -->

Explanation: A polyploid (e.g. tetraploid) crossed with a diploid parent produces triploid offspring that are usually sterile because chromosomes cannot pair in meiosis, so the polyploid is reproductively isolated in the same location.

**A1184.** Rising sea level created a geographical barrier, so gene flow between island and mainland populations stopped; the island population faced different selection pressures (e.g. climate, food, predators) and natural selection favoured different alleles; random mutations and genetic drift (small island population) further changed allele frequencies; over many generations the gene pools diverged so much that the populations would be reproductively isolated (unable to produce fertile offspring) if reunited, meaning a new species had formed (allopatric speciation).
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1185.** Allopatric speciation occurs when populations are separated by a geographical barrier that prevents gene flow; sympatric speciation occurs within the same geographical area, where reproductive isolation arises without a physical barrier (e.g. by polyploidy, behavioural or temporal isolation).
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-7 -->

Explanation:
