**A1186.** Observe (or allow) the two populations to interbreed under natural conditions; if they do not mate or their offspring are not viable/fertile (e.g. test whether hybrid offspring can produce their own offspring), they are reproductively isolated and are separate species; if fertile offspring are produced, speciation has not occurred.
<!-- id: bio34a3-spe-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1187.** D. Temporal (prezygotic) isolation, and they can be regarded as separate species
<!-- id: vce-bio34-sp-c1 -->

Explanation: The biological species concept asks whether populations interbreed in nature. Different breeding seasons are a prezygotic barrier.

#### Fossil record and dating

**A1188.** B. Rapid burial in fine sediment with low oxygen
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-1 -->

Explanation: Rapid burial in fine sediment (e.g. mud, volcanic ash) in anoxic conditions protects remains from scavengers, decomposers and oxygen, allowing mineralisation. Oxygen and decomposers break remains down.

**A1189.** B. Fossil X is older than fossil Y.
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-2 -->

Explanation: The law of superposition states that, in undisturbed strata, lower layers were deposited earlier and are older. This is relative dating, which gives order but not an absolute age.

**A1190.** C. 17 190 years.
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-3 -->

Explanation: One-eighth remaining = (1/2)³, so three half-lives have passed: 3 × 5730 = 17 190 years. Two half-lives (11 460 years) would leave one-quarter.

**A1191.** C. the half-life of carbon-14 is too short, so almost no carbon-14 would remain.
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-4 -->

Explanation: Carbon-14 is useful up to about 50 000 years; after that too little remains to measure. Older fossils are dated by measuring isotopes with long half-lives (e.g. potassium-40 → argon-40) in volcanic layers above and below the fossil.

**A1192.** A. they are formed by cyanobacteria, and fossil stromatolites are among the oldest evidence of life (about 3.5 billion years).
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-5 -->

Explanation: Stromatolites are layered structures built by cyanobacterial mats trapping sediment; fossil examples from the Pilbara are about 3.5 billion years old, and living Shark Bay stromatolites help scientists interpret them.

**A1193.** Fossilisation requires rare conditions (rapid burial, low oxygen), so most organisms decompose or are scavenged without forming fossils; soft-bodied organisms rarely fossilise; and many fossils are destroyed by erosion or geological activity, or have not yet been discovered. (Any two.)
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1194.** The volcanic ash layers were dated by absolute (radiometric) dating, e.g. potassium-argon dating, which measures the ratio of a parent isotope to its stable daughter product and uses the known half-life; sedimentary rock itself cannot easily be dated this way; by superposition, the fossil is younger than the lower layer and older than the upper layer, so it is between 3.2 and 3.6 million years old.
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1195.** An index fossil is from a species that was widespread geographically but existed for a relatively short period of time; if the same index fossil is found in rock layers at both sites, the layers can be inferred to be of similar age (relative dating/correlation).
<!-- id: bio34a3-frd-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1196.** B. 17 190 years
<!-- id: vce-bio34-fd-c1 -->

Explanation: 12.5% = (1/2)³, so three half-lives: 3 × 5730 = 17 190 years. Dinosaur fossils are far too old for ¹⁴C dating.

#### Evidence of relatedness and phylogenetic trees

**A1197.** C. homologous structures, showing divergent evolution from a common ancestor.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-1 -->

Explanation: Homologous structures share the same underlying structure inherited from a common ancestor but have been modified for different functions (divergent evolution). Analogous structures have similar functions but different origins.

**A1198.** B. analogous structures.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-2 -->

Explanation: Bird and insect wings both enable flight but have entirely different structures and evolutionary origins, so they are analogous, resulting from convergent evolution under similar selection pressures.

**A1199.** C. Humans share a more recent common ancestor with the rhesus monkey than with any of the other species listed.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-3 -->

Explanation: The fewer amino acid differences, the more recent the common ancestor. Rhesus monkey has only 1 difference. The data compare each species with humans, so they do not show how kangaroos relate to dogs.

**A1200.** B. a common ancestor from which two or more lineages diverged.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-4 -->

Explanation: Each node (branch point) represents a hypothetical common ancestor where lineages split. The tips of branches represent the taxa being compared.

**A1201.** D. DNA comparisons detect all changes, including silent mutations and non-coding regions that do not alter amino acids.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-5 -->

Explanation: Because the genetic code is degenerate, some DNA changes do not change the amino acid sequence; DNA also includes non-coding regions. DNA comparisons therefore reveal more differences and give finer resolution of relatedness.

**A1202.** Vestigial structures are reduced structures that have little or no function in the organism but were functional in its ancestors; example: the human coccyx (tailbone), or pelvic bones in whales/pythons; they provide evidence that the species shares a common ancestor with organisms in which the structure is functional, having been retained from that ancestor but reduced through evolution as it lost its selective advantage.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1203.** P and Q are most closely related (2 differences) and share the most recent common ancestor; R and S are more closely related to each other (4 differences) than to P or Q, forming a second group; the two groups (P+Q and R+S) diverged from an earlier common ancestor, at the base of the tree.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1204.** The number of DNA (or amino acid) differences between two species is counted; dividing by the known mutation rate (calibrated using fossils of known age) estimates the time since they shared a common ancestor; limitation: mutation rates can vary between genes, species and over time (e.g. due to generation time or selection), so estimates may be inaccurate.
<!-- id: bio34a3-erp-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1205.** A. A human arm and a whale flipper
<!-- id: vce-bio34-er-c1 -->

Explanation: Homologous structures share a bone pattern from a common ancestor but have different functions. The other pairs are analogous (convergent evolution).

#### Human evolution

**A1206.** B. A foramen magnum positioned centrally underneath the skull
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-1 -->

Explanation: A centrally placed foramen magnum allows the skull to balance on top of a vertical spine, indicating bipedalism. In quadrupedal apes it is positioned further back. The other features relate to diet or general morphology.

**A1207.** C. short, broad and bowl-shaped, supporting the internal organs and attachment of walking muscles.
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-2 -->

Explanation: The human pelvis is short and bowl-shaped, supporting abdominal organs during upright posture and positioning muscles for efficient bipedal walking. Chimpanzees have a long, narrow pelvis suited to quadrupedal locomotion.

**A1208.** B. Homo sapiens interbred with Neanderthals and Denisovans after migrating out of Africa.
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-3 -->

Explanation: Shared DNA segments indicate interbreeding (gene flow) between Homo sapiens and archaic hominins in Eurasia after the out-of-Africa migration. Homo sapiens evolved in Africa, not from Neanderthals.

**A1209.** C. 65 000 years.
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-4 -->

Explanation: Artefacts at Madjedbebe have been dated to around 65 000 years ago, supporting the long, continuous presence of Aboriginal peoples in Australia. Homo sapiens has existed for only about 300 000 years.

**A1210.** D. modern humans and extinct species more closely related to humans than to chimpanzees.
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-5 -->

Explanation: Hominins are the group consisting of modern humans and extinct relatives on the human lineage after its split from the chimpanzee lineage (e.g. Australopithecus, Homo erectus). Hominids include all great apes.

**A1211.** Valgus angle: the femurs angle inwards towards the knees, placing the feet under the body's centre of gravity for balance when walking; short, broad, bowl-shaped pelvis: supports the internal organs and provides attachment for muscles that stabilise the body on one leg during walking; (also accept S-shaped spine, which positions the centre of gravity over the hips and absorbs shock; or arched foot and non-opposable big toe in line with other toes, which act as a lever and absorb shock).
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-6 -->

Explanation:

**A1212.** mtDNA is inherited only through the maternal line and does not recombine, so changes accumulate only by mutation at a relatively constant rate; African populations show the greatest mtDNA diversity, indicating they are the oldest (more time to accumulate mutations); non-African populations carry a subset of African lineages, and molecular clock estimates trace all modern mtDNA to a common female ancestor in Africa, supporting a recent African origin and later migration.
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-7 -->

Explanation:

**A1213.** Scientific models are based on available evidence, and the fossil record is incomplete; a new species with an unexpected combination of features (small brain and body, yet recent age and tool use) shows that several hominin species co-existed and that the human family tree is more branched than previously thought, so models must be revised.
<!-- id: bio34a3-hev-8 -->

Explanation:

**A1214.** B. Walked upright (habitually bipedal)
<!-- id: vce-bio34-he-c1 -->

Explanation: A central foramen magnum balances the skull on a vertical spine. In quadrupeds it is towards the back of the skull.

### Challenge questions

#### Challenge set

**A1215.** C. Near the start of the coding sequence
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-1 -->

Explanation: A deletion shifts the reading frame for every codon after it, so an early deletion changes almost every amino acid. Introns are removed before translation.

**A1216.** STR regions are amplified by PCR and separated by size on a gel. A child inherits one allele at each STR locus from each parent, so every band in the child not from the mother must match a band of the biological father. One mismatch at several loci excludes him.
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-2 -->

Explanation: Mention PCR of STRs, separation by size, and the inheritance of one allele from each parent.

**A1217.** A. Not restore the maximum rate, because the active site's shape has changed
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-3 -->

Explanation: Non-competitive inhibitors change the active site from elsewhere; more substrate cannot outcompete them.

**A1218.** C. Make little difference, because light is the limiting factor
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-4 -->

Explanation: The rate is limited by whichever factor is in shortest supply. With little light, few ATP and NADPH are available for the Calvin cycle.

**A1219.** C. Glycolysis happens in the cytosol, so mitochondria cannot use glucose directly
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-5 -->

Explanation: Mitochondria take up pyruvate, not glucose. Supplying pyruvate would allow ATP production.

**A1220.** B. Artificial passive immunity, which is fast but short-lived with no memory cells
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-6 -->

Explanation: The patient's immune system did not make the antibodies, so no memory cells form.

**A1221.** B. The mRNA stays in the cytoplasm, where it is translated and then broken down, and there is no reverse transcription into DNA
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-7 -->

Explanation: The mRNA is translated by ribosomes into an antigen, then degraded; it does not enter the nucleus or become DNA.

**A1222.** D. A very short generation time, huge population sizes and a high mutation rate
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-8 -->

Explanation: Selection acts on variation each generation; many generations, many individuals and error-prone RNA replication mean resistant variants appear and spread quickly.

**A1223.** No effect: a silent mutation, because the genetic code is degenerate. Small effect: a missense mutation swaps one amino acid for a similar one or one away from the active site. Large effect: a nonsense mutation creates an early stop codon, or a missense change alters the active site.
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-9 -->

Explanation: Give a named mutation type and the reason for each outcome.

**A1224.** IV: temperature (for example 10, 20, 30, 40, 50 °C, using water baths). DV: rate of O₂ production (volume of gas or foam height per minute). Controlled: catalase concentration and volume, H₂O₂ concentration and volume, pH. Repeat each trial at least three times.
<!-- id: vce-bio34-challenge-10 -->

Explanation: A good design states measurable variables, a range of values, controls and repetition.

### Exam-style questions (mixed topics)

**A1225.** Human insulin, from gene to bacteria
<!-- id: vce-bio34-x1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** Any two: DNA is double-stranded and mRNA is single-stranded; DNA contains deoxyribose and mRNA contains ribose; DNA contains thymine and mRNA contains uracil instead. *(2 marks)*

1. First difference, for example: DNA is double-stranded, while mRNA is single-stranded. *(1 mark)*
2. Second difference, for example: DNA nucleotides contain the sugar deoxyribose and the base thymine, while mRNA nucleotides contain ribose and uracil. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving differences in function or location (such as 'DNA stays in the nucleus'). The question asks about structure.

Topic: Nucleic acid structure (vce-bio34-nucleic-acid-structure)

**b.** mRNA: 5′ AUG CCU AAG 3′; amino acids: Met–Pro–Lys *(2 marks)*

1. The mRNA is complementary to the template strand, with U in place of T: 3′ TAC GGA TTC 5′ gives 5′ AUG CCU AAG 3′. *(1 mark)*
2. Read the codons in order from the 5′ end: AUG = Met, CCU = Pro, AAG = Lys, so Met–Pro–Lys. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Writing T in the mRNA, or copying the template strand instead of pairing with it. mRNA contains uracil and is complementary to the template strand.

Topic: Translation and the genetic code (vce-bio34-translation-and-the-genetic-code)

**c.** It is made by ribosomes on the rough endoplasmic reticulum and enters the RER, travels in a transport vesicle to the Golgi apparatus where it is modified and packaged, then leaves in a secretory vesicle that fuses with the plasma membrane and releases it by exocytosis. *(2 marks)*

1. Ribosomes on the rough endoplasmic reticulum make the protein, which moves through the RER and then in a vesicle to the Golgi apparatus, where it is modified and packaged. *(1 mark)*
2. A secretory vesicle from the Golgi apparatus moves to the plasma membrane, fuses with it and releases insulin by exocytosis. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Leaving out the vesicles or the order of organelles. Proteins move between the RER, the Golgi and the membrane inside vesicles.

Topic: Protein secretory pathway (vce-bio34-protein-secretory-pathway)

**d.** The human gene contains introns. Bacteria cannot remove introns, but cDNA is made from mature mRNA, so it has no introns and the bacteria can express it correctly. *(1 mark)*

1. Human genes contain introns, which are removed from pre-mRNA by splicing. Bacteria cannot splice, but cDNA is copied from mature mRNA, so it has no introns. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying bacteria 'cannot read human DNA'. The genetic code is universal; the problem is the introns.

Topic: Transcription and RNA processing (vce-bio34-transcription-and-rna-processing)

**e.** The same enzyme gives both pieces complementary sticky ends, which base-pair with each other. DNA ligase then joins the sugar–phosphate backbones. *(2 marks)*

1. Cutting both with the same restriction enzyme produces complementary sticky ends, so the cDNA and the plasmid can base-pair at the cut site. *(1 mark)*
2. DNA ligase joins the sugar–phosphate backbones, sealing the gene into the plasmid. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming DNA polymerase. Ligase joins existing fragments; polymerase builds a new strand from nucleotides.

Topic: Enzymes for DNA manipulation (vce-bio34-enzymes-for-dna-manipulation)

**f.** Only bacteria that took up a plasmid have the ampicillin-resistance gene and survive. But a plasmid that closed up again without the insulin gene also gives resistance, so the colonies must be screened further (for example with a second marker gene or PCR). *(2 marks)*

1. Transformation is inefficient: only bacteria that took up a plasmid carry the ampicillin-resistance gene, so only they survive and form colonies. *(1 mark)*
2. Plasmids that rejoined without the insert also carry the resistance gene, so a resistant colony may not contain the insulin gene; further screening is needed. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Assuming every resistant colony has the recombinant plasmid. Resistance only shows that a plasmid was taken up.

Topic: Recombinant plasmids and transformation (vce-bio34-recombinant-plasmids-and-transformation)

**A1226.** A new virus: immunity, vaccines and resistance
<!-- id: vce-bio34-x2 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** S is an antigen: it is non-self and triggers an immune response. The few naive B cells whose receptors are complementary to S bind it (clonal selection) and, with cytokines from activated helper T cells, divide to form many identical plasma and memory B cells (clonal expansion). *(2 marks)*

1. S acts as an antigen. Only the B cells with receptors (membrane-bound antibodies) complementary to S bind it, which selects them (clonal selection). *(1 mark)*
2. Helped by cytokines from activated helper T cells, the selected B cells divide into clones of plasma cells and memory B cells (clonal expansion). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying B cells 'make the right antibody' after meeting the antigen. The specific B cells already exist; the antigen selects them.

Topic: Humoral immunity (vce-bio34-humoral-immunity)

**b.** Any two: neutralisation (antibodies block S so the virus cannot attach to and enter host cells); agglutination (clumping virus particles together); opsonisation (marking virus particles for phagocytosis); activating complement proteins. *(2 marks)*

1. First way, for example: neutralisation, where antibodies bind S and stop the virus attaching to host cells. *(1 mark)*
2. Second way, for example: agglutination or opsonisation, which clumps or tags virus particles so phagocytes engulf them more easily. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying antibodies 'kill' or 'destroy' the virus. Antibodies bind and mark it; other parts of the immune system remove it.

Topic: Humoral immunity (vce-bio34-humoral-immunity)

**c.** Viruses replicate inside host cells, where antibodies cannot reach them. Infected cells display viral antigens on MHC class I markers; cytotoxic T cells recognise these and cause the infected cells to die (apoptosis), stopping the virus multiplying. *(2 marks)*

1. Once inside host cells, the virus is out of reach of antibodies. *(1 mark)*
2. Infected cells present viral antigen on MHC class I markers; cytotoxic T cells recognise them and induce apoptosis in those cells. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying cytotoxic T cells attack free virus particles. They target infected body cells.

Topic: Cell-mediated immunity (vce-bio34-cell-mediated-immunity)

**d.** The vaccine triggers a primary immune response that produces memory B and T cells specific to S (artificial active immunity). On later exposure to the virus, the memory cells give a faster and larger secondary response, so the virus is removed before it causes disease. *(2 marks)*

1. The vaccine's S protein triggers a primary response, producing memory B cells and memory T cells specific to S. *(1 mark)*
2. If the virus is met later, memory cells produce a faster, stronger secondary response that clears it before symptoms develop. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the vaccine gives the person antibodies. Antibodies from a vaccine fade; long-term protection comes from memory cells.

Topic: Types of immunity and vaccination (vce-bio34-types-of-immunity-and-vaccination)

**e.** A random mutation in the gene for the enzyme had already produced resistant virus particles before the drug was used. The drug acted as a selection pressure: it stopped susceptible particles replicating, while resistant ones survived and replicated. Over many generations the resistance allele became more frequent in the virus population. *(3 marks)*

1. Variation: a random mutation in the viral gene for the enzyme produced resistant virus particles. The mutation was already present; the drug did not cause it. *(1 mark)*
2. Selection: the drug stopped susceptible particles replicating, but resistant particles survived and replicated, passing on the resistance allele. *(1 mark)*
3. Over many generations, the frequency of the resistance allele increased until the resistant strain was common. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the virus 'became resistant because of the drug' or 'adapted to the drug'. Resistance comes from a random mutation that the drug then selects for.

Topic: Antibiotic and antiviral resistance (vce-bio34-antibiotic-and-antiviral-resistance)

**A1227.** Photosynthesis, Rubisco and a gene-edited crop
<!-- id: vce-bio34-x3 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** In the stroma of the chloroplast; it uses ATP and NADPH. *(2 marks)*

1. Location: the stroma of the chloroplast. *(1 mark)*
2. Inputs from the light-dependent stage: ATP and NADPH. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming the thylakoid membranes (grana). They are the site of the light-dependent stage.

Topic: Photosynthesis (vce-bio34-photosynthesis)

**b.** Stomata close to save water, so CO₂ falls and O₂ builds up inside the leaf; Rubisco then binds O₂ more often (photorespiration), so less carbon is fixed. *(2 marks)*

1. The plant closes its stomata to reduce water loss, so CO₂ cannot enter and O₂ accumulates in the leaf. *(1 mark)*
2. With a higher O₂ to CO₂ ratio, Rubisco binds O₂ more often, increasing photorespiration and reducing carbon fixation. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying only that the enzyme denatures. The main effect at moderate heat is stomatal closure and photorespiration.

Topic: Factors affecting enzymes and inhibition (vce-bio34-factors-affecting-enzymes-and-inhibition)

**c.** They first fix CO₂ into a 4-carbon compound in mesophyll cells, then release it in bundle-sheath cells, keeping CO₂ concentration high around Rubisco. *(2 marks)*

1. In mesophyll cells, CO₂ is first fixed by an enzyme that does not bind O₂, forming a 4-carbon compound. *(1 mark)*
2. This compound is moved to bundle-sheath cells and releases CO₂ there, so Rubisco is surrounded by high CO₂ and rarely binds O₂. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Describing CAM plants, which separate the steps in time (night and day), not in space.

Topic: C3, C4 and CAM plants (vce-bio34-c3-c4-and-cam-plants)

**d.** The guide RNA is complementary to the target DNA and leads Cas9 to it; Cas9 then cuts both strands of the DNA at that site. *(2 marks)*

1. The guide RNA has a sequence complementary to the target gene, so it binds there and directs Cas9 to the correct site (next to a PAM sequence). *(1 mark)*
2. Cas9 is an endonuclease that cuts both strands of the DNA at the target site. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying Cas9 finds the gene by itself. The guide RNA provides the specificity.

Topic: CRISPR-Cas9 (vce-bio34-crispr-cas9)

**e.** When the cell repairs the break, bases are often inserted or deleted, which can knock out the gene; or a supplied DNA template can be inserted. *(1 mark)*

1. The cell repairs the double-strand break; errors in repair add or remove bases (disrupting the gene), or scientists supply a template so a new sequence is inserted. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying Cas9 inserts the new gene. Cas9 only cuts; the cell's repair process makes the change.

Topic: CRISPR-Cas9 (vce-bio34-crispr-cas9)

**f.** Benefit: higher, more reliable yields in dry conditions. Concern: for example, effects on ecosystems or reduced genetic diversity if one variety is grown widely. *(2 marks)*

1. A benefit, such as more reliable food production in drought, using less irrigation water. *(1 mark)*
2. A concern, such as reduced genetic diversity, unknown ecological effects, or unequal access for farmers. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Giving vague answers such as 'it is unnatural'. Name a specific biological, social or ethical issue.

Topic: Biotechnology to improve yield (vce-bio34-biotechnology-to-improve-yield)

**A1228.** Birds on two islands
<!-- id: vce-bio34-x4 -->
*Multi-part question · 9 marks*

**a.** The water was a geographical barrier that stopped gene flow. Different selection pressures, mutations and genetic drift changed allele frequencies differently on each island. Over time the populations became reproductively isolated, forming separate species (allopatric speciation). *(3 marks)*

1. A geographical barrier (the sea) separated the populations, so there was no gene flow between them. *(1 mark)*
2. Different selection pressures (for example different seeds), new mutations and genetic drift changed allele frequencies differently in each population. *(1 mark)*
3. Over many generations the populations became reproductively isolated, so they could no longer interbreed: separate species. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the birds changed in order to adapt to each island. Variation arises randomly; selection acts on it.

Topic: Speciation (vce-bio34-speciation)

**b.** A bottleneck effect (a type of genetic drift): the survivors carry only some of the original alleles, so genetic diversity falls and allele frequencies change by chance. *(2 marks)*

1. Bottleneck effect, a form of genetic drift. *(1 mark)*
2. The small surviving population carries only a random sample of alleles, so genetic diversity decreases and allele frequencies shift by chance, not by selection. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calling it natural selection. The storm killed birds at random, regardless of their traits.

Topic: Mutations, gene flow and genetic drift (vce-bio34-mutations-gene-flow-and-genetic-drift)

**c.i.** A and B *(1 mark)*

1. A and B have the fewest differences (4), so they share the most recent common ancestor. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Choosing the pair with the most differences.

Topic: Evidence of relatedness and phylogenetic trees (vce-bio34-evidence-of-relatedness-and-phylogenetic-trees)

**c.ii.** Mutations accumulate at a roughly constant rate, so more differences mean more time since the species shared a common ancestor (a molecular clock). *(1 mark)*

1. If mutations build up at an approximately constant rate, the number of differences acts as a molecular clock: more differences means a longer time since divergence. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying more differences mean the species are more advanced.

Topic: Evidence of relatedness and phylogenetic trees (vce-bio34-evidence-of-relatedness-and-phylogenetic-trees)

**d.** Between 2.1 and 2.4 million years old; the ash layers are dated by absolute (radiometric) dating, and the fossil's position between them uses relative dating (the law of superposition). *(2 marks)*

1. The fossil is between 2.1 and 2.4 million years old. *(1 mark)*
2. The ash layers are dated by radiometric (absolute) dating; the fossil's age comes from its position between them (relative dating, superposition). *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the fossil itself was radiometrically dated. Here its age comes from the layers around it.

Topic: Fossil record and dating (vce-bio34-fossil-record-and-dating)

**A1229.** Challenge: insulin from bacteria
<!-- id: vce-bio34-cx1 -->
*Multi-part question · 11 marks*

**a.** Bacteria cannot remove introns. mRNA has already been spliced, so cDNA contains only the exons. *(2 marks)*

1. Human genes contain introns that are removed by splicing in eukaryotes. *(1 mark)*
2. Bacteria lack splicing machinery, so cDNA (exons only) is needed. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying bacteria cannot read human DNA. The genetic code is universal.

Topic: Transcription and RNA processing (vce-bio34-transcription-and-rna-processing)

**b.** A restriction endonuclease cuts the plasmid (and the cDNA ends) to leave matching sticky ends; DNA ligase joins the sugar–phosphate backbones. *(2 marks)*

1. Restriction enzyme: cuts at a recognition site, giving complementary sticky ends. *(1 mark)*
2. DNA ligase: forms phosphodiester bonds to seal the DNA. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying ligase forms hydrogen bonds between bases.

Topic: Enzymes for DNA manipulation (vce-bio34-enzymes-for-dna-manipulation)

**c.** About 5.2 × 10⁷ copies; primers bind to each end of the target and give Taq polymerase a starting point. *(2 marks)*

1. 50 × 2²⁰ ≈ 5.2 × 10⁷ copies. *(1 mark)*
2. Primers are short sequences complementary to the ends of the target, where DNA polymerase starts adding nucleotides. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Using 50 × 20 or 2 × 50²⁰.

Topic: PCR and gel electrophoresis (vce-bio34-pcr-and-gel-electrophoresis)

**d.** Only bacteria with a plasmid grow on ampicillin. Of those, white colonies have the insert in lacZ (recombinant); blue colonies have a plasmid without the insert. *(3 marks)*

1. Ampicillin kills bacteria without a plasmid. *(1 mark)*
2. A plasmid without an insert has a working lacZ, which breaks down X-gal to give blue colonies. *(1 mark)*
3. The insert disrupts lacZ, so recombinant colonies are white and are selected. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Choosing blue colonies.

Topic: Recombinant plasmids and transformation (vce-bio34-recombinant-plasmids-and-transformation)

**e.** Rough ER: ribosomes make the polypeptide, which is folded and transported. Golgi: modifies and packages it into vesicles for secretion. Bacteria have no ER or Golgi, so the chains may need to be folded and joined after extraction. *(2 marks)*

1. Correct roles of rough ER and Golgi apparatus. *(1 mark)*
2. Bacteria lack these organelles, so post-translational processing must be done another way. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying bacteria have a Golgi apparatus.

Topic: Protein secretory pathway (vce-bio34-protein-secretory-pathway)

**A1230.** Challenge: responding to a new virus
<!-- id: vce-bio34-cx2 -->
*Multi-part question · 12 marks*

**a.** Physical: mucus traps particles and cilia sweep it away. Chemical: lysozyme in mucus, or antimicrobial secretions. *(2 marks)*

1. A correct physical barrier and how it works. *(1 mark)*
2. A correct chemical barrier and how it works. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Naming the skin. The question asks about the respiratory tract.

Topic: Pathogens and physical and chemical barriers (vce-bio34-pathogens-and-physical-and-chemical-barriers)

**b.** Viruses replicate inside cells, out of reach of antibodies. Infected cells display viral antigen on MHC I, and cytotoxic T cells recognise it and trigger apoptosis. *(2 marks)*

1. Antibodies cannot enter cells. *(1 mark)*
2. Cytotoxic T cells recognise antigen on MHC I and induce apoptosis. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying antibodies kill infected cells directly.

Topic: Cell-mediated immunity (vce-bio34-cell-mediated-immunity)

**c.** Memory B and T cells from the first infection recognise the antigen quickly, so the secondary response is faster and larger, clearing the virus before symptoms develop. *(2 marks)*

1. Memory cells remain after the primary response. *(1 mark)*
2. The secondary response is faster and produces more antibodies. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying antibodies from the first infection last forever.

Topic: Humoral immunity (vce-bio34-humoral-immunity)

**d.** Random mutations in the gene for the surface antigen produce variation. Variants that antibodies bind poorly infect more people and replicate more (a selective advantage), so their alleles increase in frequency over generations. *(3 marks)*

1. Mutation creates variation in surface antigens. *(1 mark)*
2. Immune escape gives a selective advantage: more infection and replication. *(1 mark)*
3. The variant allele becomes more frequent over many generations. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying the virus mutates in order to escape.

Topic: Natural selection and selective breeding (vce-bio34-natural-selection-and-selective-breeding)

**e.** 75% *(1 mark)*

1. 1 − 1/4 = 0.75, so 75% of the population. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Calculating 1/R₀ (25%).

Topic: Types of immunity and vaccination (vce-bio34-types-of-immunity-and-vaccination)

**f.** Advantage: immediate protection, useful for people who respond poorly to vaccines. Limitation: no memory cells, so protection fades; it is also expensive and may not bind new variants. *(2 marks)*

1. A valid advantage. *(1 mark)*
2. A valid limitation. *(1 mark)*

Common mistake: Saying monoclonal antibodies give lifelong immunity.

Topic: Emerging diseases and immunotherapy (vce-bio34-emerging-diseases-and-immunotherapy)
